madam president, ladies and gentlemen, a confederal europe with common objectives in foreign policy, economy, culture and defence, but without infringing the sovereignty of the nations which make it up: this was the idea of one of his predecessors, president de gaulle. unfortunately, that is not your idea, president macron. my group and i are fighting democratically but firmly against the project of a european superstate which reduces our nations to small administrative entities dominated by a cold and faceless bureaucracy. we reject the hypocrisy of wanting to deal with illegal immigration by closing the internal borders so that no one can come from france to italy, but at the same time requiring italy and the first-entry countries to keep their external borders open. europe will never be a state, but it is certainly a civilization to be loved, protected and passed on to our children, and that is what we are fighting for.
in the name of the id group. mr president, commissioners, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i would like to thank the members of the imco committee, the rapporteur for the work they have done and the members of the other committees. for the purpose of this regulation, the concept of illegal content should underline not only the principle that what is illegal offline should also be considered illegal online, but also that what is legal offline should be considered legal online. after a year of work, the digital services act has arrived in plenary and as always there is a division between those who are satisfied with the result and those who are not. i'm in the second category. the improvements made to the original text, which we have also contributed to, cannot be denied. i would like to highlight the article on accessibility requirements for services for people with disabilities: it is very important to me because i care about the hardships that our most vulnerable citizens face every day. for our society to truly be inclusive, it must be inclusive of everything. but let's get to the critical parts. first, the figure of the trusted flagger. we have strongly opposed it and called for it to be withdrawn because we wonder what guarantees of independence and impartiality this figure can have. in addition, notifications made by trusted flaggers are considered to be a-series and are given priority, whereas those submitted by any other citizen do not receive the same priority. second, the governance. the commission has too much power in the application of this regulation and despite our efforts we have not been able to get the board to balance it more. thirdly, the safety of european consumers, especially with regard to products or services from third countries. the platforms sell everything, and consumers must be able to buy safe products and services regardless of where they are produced. this is why i have strongly called for platforms that allow third country traders to trade online to be checked for compliance with eu product safety laws. fourth: the risk assessment and mitigation of risks. the text provides that large platforms must carry out at least annually an assessment of the systemic risks caused by or related to the operation and use of their services and take measures to mitigate these risks. the critical thing for citizens is that, on content and activities considered harmful but not illegal, it will be the large platform that decides the boundary between freedom of expression and the right to information with respect to the possible harm caused to users, thus leaving the platform the widest discretion. this risk is even greater when considering that such assessment can be made by automated moderation systems based on algorithms, which often contain preconceptions. in conclusion, the dsa was a great opportunity, but this opportunity was wasted. under the pretext of more control over platforms to protect citizens, their freedom of expression has been put at risk. i hope i am wrong and that there will be no regrets about some of the choices that have been made, because then the citizens will have to bear the consequences.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, when we surf the web, we feel free: we are free to inform ourselves, free to use new services, free to meet virtually and to share experiences and opinions. but this freedom can become an illusion or worse, a trap when it is not guaranteed and protected by a system of certain and shared rules. the first objective of a european law on digital services must therefore be to protect users, particularly those most exposed to the dangers of the network: women, children, the disabled, the elderly, affected by abhorrent phenomena such as revenge porn, cyberbullying, child pornography and online fraud. the amendments tabled by the league will provide greater protection for the most vulnerable and also for small and medium-sized businesses active on the web, but without this entailing new bureaucratic requirements, which are intended to further hinder their investments in cybersecurity at a time of great vulnerability of the system. i would therefore like to thank mrs alessandra basso for the great work she has done in committee to improve this measure, which is still inadequate in many respects. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the european parliament and the council for approval. the majority of this house has decided that the commission must continue to have enormous powers of surveillance, investigation, monitoring of large platforms and the possibility of imposing sanctions, despite the fact that the digital services act provides for the creation of a control system for each individual member state. as for platforms, they will have the power to decide which harmful but not illegal content can be removed, and they will determine the boundary between freedom of expression and the right to information. we believe that everything that threatens freedom of expression, democracy and the autonomy of states must be thoroughly studied and discussed.
mr president, madam vice-president, commissioners, ladies and gentlemen, it is a strange thing to have such a common understanding on such an important and difficult text, and we must therefore congratulate those who have succeeded in reaching an agreement, a broad and comprehensive compromise. it's a big deal for the dsa to update a regulation like the e-commerce regulation after 20 years. i must say, however, that the narrative we often hear in this house, that for 20 years nothing has been done, is not quite so. in the last parliamentary term there have been important interventions on sectoral legislation, copyright legislation being one of them. so i think that a step forward was a significant step forward that we're making, but you'll allow me, after a year of the proposal and after three hours of discussion, to make a joke. when i was a kid, i used to play a game called "the cuckoo dance", where you took one step forward and then you took one step back. so my fear, and the fear of my colleagues in the italian delegation of the epp of forza italia, is that we are taking a major step backwards, especially with regard to copyright legislation. so we must pay particular attention to what is happening and, in particular, to get back to being serious, there are a few points, four in particular, which raise serious questions. first of all, the discharge, which, unlike the e-commerce directive, is not only for passive and automatic technical subjects, but also for those who play an active intermediary role and who control and, above all, monetise content. then there are the due diligence obligations, the so-called "know your business customer", which is allowed only for marketplace platforms. then the stay down, the secondary control, the possibility for the platforms to perhaps remove some content. we therefore support the amendments tabled by mr verheyen and mr didier and hope that their approval will lead to a more balanced and more effective text.

in the name of the id group. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, after a year and a half of negotiations, we have come to an end. i have never concealed my perplexity about this committee of inquiry, because from the outset it has approached animal welfare with an ideological approach, aimed at demonizing a productive and cutting-edge sector rather than actually protecting animal welfare. in europe we have some of the highest animal welfare standards in the world and they are enshrined in regulation (ec) no 1/2005, a regulation which should not be distorted but implemented correctly and strictly respected by all member states, so as to reduce breaches and infringements. we have therefore called for strict and punctual checks, especially at departure, and perhaps for them to be made faster by means of a centralised data collection system which can be easily consulted by the authorities, in order to monitor, reduce delays and cut red tape. as a group we have also proposed that specific funds be made available to modernise vehicles in terms of structure, technology and safety, so as to ensure better transport conditions: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of air quality in europe, from ventilation systems to loading and unloading systems, because there is no point in hoping for improvements if we do not give the industry the right tools to put them into practice. the problem is not solved by banning travel or limiting the duration of travel, nor is it solved by requiring the transport of live animals to be replaced by the transport of meat, carcasses or genetic material, because it is not possible: the markets are different, as are the skills and the numbers employed. it is good to reduce transport where possible, particularly when dealing with third countries which do not meet our standards, but it is not good to talk about 'replacing' live transport and even to want to finance it with the cap, by taking money away from our farmers. it is also wrong to support a plant-based diet, while abandoning meat, milk and eggs, which are part of our culinary culture and the mediterranean diet. a note of merit goes to the call for greater commitment to the protection of species not adequately covered by the regulation, including dogs and cats: we all hope that the regulation will be supplemented by provisions to ensure the welfare of our four-legged friends who are transported for commercial purposes. attention to the most vulnerable animals, such as unweaned animals, pregnant females and animals at the end of their lives, is also positive. they need to be able to rely on scientific assessments and to link transport authorisation to veterinary advice in order to reduce their stress and to protect them adequately. as always, there is a need for balance, and some of these recommendations are wavering and do not fully satisfy us. we had a great opportunity. the commission's proposal is to make a contribution to the establishment of a european animal welfare system. that's too bad.

as you know, today we are marking international holocaust remembrance day with a formal address by ms margot friedländer. ms friedländer, i am honoured to welcome you to the european parliament and i thank you for being here today as a witness of the holocaust. honourable members, dear margot friedländer, dear colleagues, the horrors that took place in auschwitz are unspeakable, yet we must speak. this is a day of remembrance. today, 27 january, marks our collective responsibility to remember the unspeakable atrocities committed towards all the victims of the holocaust: 6 million jewish people, the romani people, the lgbtiq communities, and many others that were stripped of their dignity, because of their ethnicity, identity, race, behaviours, or because of their political or religious beliefs. the nazi regime callously murdered 17 million men, women and children and inflicted unimaginable suffering and pain on millions more, while others’ silence stood complicit. we remember those that perished and those that survived. survivors burdened with unimaginable hardship. survivors such as zilli schmidt and zoni weisz, whom we are fortunate to have present with us online today. we remember those that, to paraphrase the title of margot friedländer’s book, had to ‘try to make a life’ in a world that tried to completely annihilate them and their families. how low humanity sank. on holocaust remembrance day, we remember crimes committed against humanity in the past, but we also remember the importance of speaking up in the present. united in diversity, we speak up against holocaust deniers, against conspiracy myths, against disinformation and against violence of every kind that targets and singles out members of our communities.because, despite decades of effort, we have not yet done enough to combat discrimination. in recent years across europe, anti-semitic attacks are increasing, and our children are no exception to this violence. only two days ago, a twelve-year-old boy from livorno in tuscany was insulted, kicked and spat on, simply because he is jewish. fear still exists, because anti-semitism still exists, and because the fundamental right to be free from discrimination, irrespective of gender, race, ethnic origin or sexual orientation, is threatened daily. we cannot allow complacency. we cannot allow impunity for acts of hatred that instil fear in members of our communities. holocaust survivor and nobel prize winner elie wiesel said: ‘i swore never to be silent whenever and wherever human beings endure suffering and humiliation. we must always takes sides.’ and the european parliament will always take a side: the side of respect, the side of human dignity, the side of equality. the european parliament will never be silent. and today, we lit up the european parliament in honour of all the victims of the holocaust. we must consciously act every day, and in whatever we do, to ensure that our communities are not marginalised by exclusion, hatred and indifference. and we must learn and we must educate. the 2005 united nations resolution that designated 27 january as the international commemoration day in memory of holocaust victims also emphasises the importance of educating about holocaust history so that the darkest chapter of european history never, ever repeats itself. the united nations resolution calls for actively preserving all holocaust sites that served as nazi death camps, concentration camps, forced labour camps and prisons. these sites, as we have seen, offer history lessons in themselves. sites such as the alsace-moselle-memorial in schirmeck – just a few kilometres away from the european parliament’s official seat in strasbourg – where, through knowledge and education, current and future generations can learn about the hunger, thirst, disease, violence and death that were part of the everyday life of holocaust victims. they can learn to never forget. today we mark 77 years since the liberation of the auschwitz-birkenau concentration camp. a liberation from evil, that signalled that there was still hope. the european project was built as a political manifestation of ‘never again’: a response to the horrors of the third reich with the ambition to integrate democratic countries together in the hope for a better future. it stemmed from the bold ideal that strength could be extracted from tragedy. that from the ashes of our common history we could forge a common destiny: a destiny where the values of peace, fairness, openness and cooperation could be instilled in us all. because this is our europe. today it is an honour for the european parliament to welcome holocaust survivor margot friedländer. your lifelong commitment to combat discrimination through initiatives like the margot friedländer award, which supports young people in standing up against modern-day forms of anti-semitism, racism and exclusion, and your lifelong dedication in sharing the message of your life, is an inspiration to us all. and for this we thank you. 40 years ago, simone veil held the same role that i hold today. the first president of a democratically-elected european parliament was herself a former inmate of auschwitz-birkenau. and so this responsibility that we must carry as a house of democracy is not only institutional, but also deeply personal. and we will honour the legacy of the holocaust victims by never forgetting, by never being complacent, and by always, always speaking up. writer and holocaust survivor. mr president of the european parliament, mr president of the european commission, mr president of the european council, members of the european parliament, ladies and gentlemen! it is a great honor for me to speak to you today. a few days ago, on 20 january, an event at the wannsee conference in berlin reminded us that the national socialist leadership there had decided the unthinkable eighty years ago: the as they called it final solution, the murder of the european jews. today, throughout europe, we commemorate the liberation of the auschwitz concentration and extermination camp on 27 january 1945. this reminder is especially important today. it is not only in germany that the population has watched and done nothing to protect its jewish neighbours. in many other countries, too, little was done to save jewish neighbors from deportation. denmark was a proud exception, where the people, with the knowledge of the government, protected and saved their jewish fellow citizens. but that was the exception. the european union has a duty to help the people of the world, and to help the people of the world. perhaps without évian's failure, the wannsee conference would never have taken place. i was the only one in my family to survive. my wonderfully gifted brother ralph was taken from our berlin apartment by the gestapo in january 1943 and put on a transport to the east - he was then seventeen and a half years old. when mommy came home, the apartment was sealed, and a neighbor told her what had happened. my mother didn't wait for me. she turned herself in to the gestapo to accompany my brother. she left a message for me at the neighbors. when i come, i want you to tell me this: "i'm going with ralph wherever that may be. i want margot to try and make a life for herself. that was the last i ever heard from my mother and my brother. i was 21 years old at the time and at that moment i was all alone in berlin, without my mother, my brother and no other family members. i managed to find good people who hid me. these people did something that could have cost her her head. they shared their lodging and their food with me. i had about 16 helpers and many different shelters where i was hidden for a short time or longer - several times in difficult situations - until i was caught after 15 months in april 1944 and deported to the theresienstadt camp. many old people were also taken to theresienstadt. couples were separated, the elderly had to sleep in a small room in two-story wooden beds with straw mattresses. there was nothing but the wooden bed where one would hang his few things on a nail. there were no toilets or washing facilities. they had to go out to primitive latrines and wash at the well, even in the cold winter. they were given less food than the working people, many starved and died miserably in a short time after their arrival. in early february 1945, a transport of more than 200 men and 20 women arrived from wulkow, who had been sent a year earlier from theresienstadt to a eichmann project to build an alternate headquarters for the reich security main office. adolf friedländer had returned to theresienstadt by this transport. we met by chance in the hallway of the barracks where we both lived on different floors. i didn't recognize him because he had his arm tied in a noose, and his head and neck were also tied. when he came up, he greeted me with my name, margot, because we knew each other from berlin, from the jewish cultural association. we were so happy to meet someone from back in the day, and then we tried to get together every once in a while. in late april 1945, i was called along with others to help with an expected transport. when a long train arrived with cattle cars, we did not know what to expect. when the doors were opened, i experienced something i will never forget. people who hardly looked human fell or were pushed out of the crowded car. the living and the dead were hardly distinguishable. eyes were deep in the cavities, only the noses sticking out of the face. almost all of them were wearing some sort of striped pajamas, the prison clothes. they were just rags hanging from their bodies. instead of shoes, they wore wooden panties, many of whom had only one or no shoes left. many had swollen legs that swelled up. others were just garrisons. something fell into my arms, a human being, so weak that i had to carry him. he was light. someone grabbed my arm and said, "margot, don't you recognize me?" other but how could i recognize this figure who told me he was arnold kirchberg? they all came from auschwitz. i first heard the name auschwitz because we had always been told that the transports were going to the east. so auschwitz was the east, and these people came from there. they were sent on a death march just before the liberation of auschwitz on january 27. the ss did everything possible to prevent the survivors from falling into the hands of the russians and to prevent the allies from learning the true extent of the extermination in the concentration camps. for nearly three months, the people had been on the move, first on foot and then in long columns. they were just men, even though at first glance they seemed sexless, like skeletons. most of them had been dead for a long time, shot, starved, died of exhaustion. at some point, the last survivors were rounded up, crammed into cattle cars and sent to theresienstadt, one of the last camps that had not yet been liberated - with no food, along with typhus patients and in terrible dirt. many had not survived, but there were still hundreds who were coming to meet us. that was the moment when the east got its name, the moment we learned about the death camps, and the moment i realized that i would never see my mother or my brother again. now i had no hope. i wanted to survive because i wanted to see my family again. what was my survival worth now? many rumors that the war was soon over circulated in the camp, our mood was tense. we first noticed a change in the behavior of the ss men. they ran around, loading their belongings onto trucks. their possessions, that's all they stole from us. we were waiting anxiously. eventually, the gate opened and the first trucks left the ghetto. we could hardly believe it. were we soon free? a few days later, on may 5th, commander rahm left the camp. an ss man stood at the gate and pulled up the swastika and took it with him. they were crowded into vehicles that were still half-drivable. they rode out to the camp in a column. from the window of the barracks where i lived, i could see the road leading to prague, which was strictly forbidden, but i dared. i saw an open jeep with a red cross flag stretched across the hood. a little later, this flag was flying at the command post. a strange mood was spreading in the camp, without us knowing what was going to happen next. no one cheered, no one rejoiced, no one changed their daily routine. the ss is gone, are we free now? how does it feel to be free? we've waited for this moment for so long. now he's here, and we can, we don't want to believe it! for three days, we'll live as before. although the red cross was there, the red army had not yet taken theresienstadt. everything was holding its breath. then the russians took over. the soviet army moved along the road leading to prague, just outside the camp, from the earliest hours along open trucks, full of soldiers. tired horses were ridden by tired soldiers. now the gates were open. i'm standing at the open gate for the first time. there are no guards, no one can stop me from going out. i'm just standing there. i'm not dreaming, are you? is it possible that i survived? carefully, i take a few steps out onto the street. i don't want to leave, i just want to see if it's true that i can walk out without getting shot. next to me is adolf friedländer. we'll see each other. we will witness the liberation together on may 8, 1945. a moment we will never forget. we were free. what was my mood? was i happy? no, i don't think so, because now i'm going to find out what happened to my beloved mother and brother. how i survived theresienstadt, i don't really know. from the beginning, i have seen the ghetto as through a veil. i did what i had to do every day. now i was free, and that too seemed unreal. we stood at the gate, unable to move, to do anything. after the red army passed, it became a little quieter. we waited to see what would come next. suddenly, the place was full of doctors and nurses who were the first to try to control the typhus. the straw mattresses were burned, everything was sprayed with disinfectant. medication arrived, and we were given soap and toothpaste so that we could finally wash properly and brush our teeth. heavy trucks brought boxes and bags of food to the food pantry and kitchens, especially rice, so that the sick could have light meals. then something happened that we could hardly believe: people came into the camp, wearing large swastikas on the backs of their jackets. under the supervision of the russians, they now did the dirty and hard work that we had done. now i realized i was free. my first thought was, yes, there is a god! adolf and i sometimes went for a walk. then we sat on a bench by a river and talked about everything that moved us. one day in june, when we were sitting on our bench again, adolf asked me: "can you imagine a life with me? other adolf wanted to marry me. i always thought there was more to marriage. i wasn't in love with adolf, i was just frozen, i needed time to become human again, to feel. i've only ever felt pain and memories. adolf was the same way, maybe that pain brought us closer together than being in love. we longed for a normal life. i said, "yes". adolf gave me his father's wedding ring. wedding rings were the only thing that wasn't taken in theresienstadt. adolf carried him through the camp and saved him. i would never take it off. adolf wanted to get married as soon as possible. on june 26, 1945, we were married by rabbi neuhaus according to jewish ritual. on june 30, four days after our wedding, adolf received a telegram from his sister ilse in the united states. it gave him a new purpose. i believe it was july 10, 1945, when we left theresienstadt, this time in cattle cars, which were supposed to take us to freedom. we were in a dp camp in deggendorf, bavaria, until july 1946, and left germany in a troop transport for america, where we arrived in new york on july 28, 1946, and were warmly welcomed by adolf's sister and her husband, a cousin and friends. unfortunately, i haven't had time to write about my 64 years, from 1946 to 2010, living in new york. adolf and i had traveled a lot since the 1950s, at least 30 trips to europe. but we've only been to germany once for three days, to munich, but never to berlin. despite invitations, adolf never wanted to return to germany, and certainly not to berlin - the country of his mother's murderers. after 52 happy years of marriage, my husband passed away on december 25, 1997. now i had to take my life back. i went to the 92nd street y, the jewish cultural organization's senior club, and i took writing classes, and i started writing about my life. a documentary film director noticed this and wanted to make a film with me, and i got an invitation from the berlin senate. so in 2003, i returned to berlin for the first time. when i arrived in berlin in june 2003, i went for a walk on the first day. i stopped on a street i knew and said: "my berlin, i am so glad to be born in such a beautiful city". in 2005 the film "don't call it home" was shown in berlin about my first home visit after 61 years. i made more and more friends there. my new friends encouraged me to write about my life in my native language. after decades, i wrote down my memories in german. when i couldn't sleep, i would take a pencil and paper and go to bed and let my thoughts run wild. at that moment, memory had given me the right words. in the spring of 2008, when my memoirs were published as a book called "trying to make your life", it was the beginning of my fourth life. i was still living in new york, still commuting across the atlantic, because i was getting more and more invitations from germany for readings. and then the moment came. two years later, in 2010, i realized it: i came back to berlin when i was 88 years old. not only did i have many friends waiting for me there, but i returned with a message that i have been giving to students in my lectures ever since: after 64 years of living in america, i have come back to talk to you, to shake your hand, to ask you to be the witnesses of the times that we cannot be for much longer. it's for you. be human! people did it because they didn't recognize people as people. you can't love all people, but respect is due to everyone. there is no christian, jewish or muslim blood, only human blood. we're all the same. what's done is done, and we can't change it. it must never, never happen again. i am not speaking only for the six million jews who were murdered, but for all those who were innocently murdered by the nazi regime. it's for you, for your children, for your descendants. it's my mission. i say again, be human! ladies and gentlemen, you in this house represent millions of people on this continent, whose democratically elected representatives you are. this means a great responsibility, for even now we are all facing great challenges. i am deeply concerned to see that the holocaust, like the nazi war of conquest and extermination, is increasingly being forgotten. we're the only survivors left to tell the tale. today i see the memory of what happened being politically abused, sometimes even ridiculed and trampled underfoot. i was shocked to see, at the age of 100, how symbols of our exclusion by the nazis, the so-called "jewish star", are now shamelessly used by new enemies of democracy in the open street to portray themselves as victims in the midst of a democracy. on a day like today, we must stand together so that the memory of the holocaust remains true and is not abused by anyone. ladies and gentlemen, what's done is done. i've seen firsthand how people have denied other people their humanity. first to cut them off, plunder them, burn their temples, and finally to murder them. this can never happen again. so we must be vigilant now, not looking away as we did then. i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the european union. i had to see for myself where this was going. today, on january 27th, we remember the millions of innocent victims of national socialism. memory has tremendous meaning for us today. treating people as human beings, regardless of their skin color, religion, or background, is especially true today. humanity, tolerance and respect are more important than ever for peaceful coexistence. this is my wish on this important day of remembrance and commemoration for the world, for europe, for all of us. (parliament adopted the legislative resolution)
thank you very much from the bottom of all our hearts. i will now give the floor to president charles michel for his address.
president of the european council. madam president of the european parliament, madam president of the commission, ladies and gentlemen, dear margot friedländer, it is not easy for me to stand before you and speak after the power of such testimony, after the power, the intimacy, the personal character, the fierce will, the courage and the lucidity that you have expressed in this chamber, in this parliament which is the symbol of european democracy. ladies and gentlemen, i'd like to begin by expressing my admiration and my gratitude to you, ms margot friedländer. admiration for your courage and commitment to bear witness as you do today. it commands our respect and it forces each citizen, each of us here today, to reflect on our own commitment to defend and improve the society and the world we live in. and allow me to express our gratitude, dear margot friedländer, for your words of wisdom. your commitment has brought you to this parliament today, the home of european democracy, to commemorate the holocaust, when europe's jewish people were targeted for annihilation. the holocaust, the jews, home, democracy, and europe. allow me to say a few words about some of the convictions that link these powerful and resonant words. the holocaust was a european tragedy. some people, especially the younger generation, may see this event as remote and abstract, even mythical: a global commemoration of a unique, symbolic and long-ago tragedy. yet it was on european soil that this unimaginable crime was perpetrated by europeans against fellow europeans. the result of unbridled hatred. and we europeans are all heirs to this history our history. and you are all the guardians of this memory, our memory, this europe and europeans, all of us here today, have a special responsibility: the responsibility of guardians. they are sparse, and this responsibility becomes all the more consequential because unfortunately, we will not always be accompanied by survivors to bear witness. ladies and gentlemen, europe is the home of european jews, exactly as it is the home for all europeans. europe would not be what it is today without the contributions of the jewish people over many centuries, contributing to our societies, to the sciences, to the economy, to culture and to the vibrancy of our european intellectual life. europe is home for jewish people. that's why today, with anti-semitism resurging in europe, the idea that jewish people are under attack just because they are jews is absolutely unbearable. and it is why the strategy that the european commission adopted last year to combat anti-semitism and to secure a future for jewish life in europe is so vital. and this plan includes support for the creation of a network of young european ambassadors to promote the memory of the shoah. ladies and gentlemen, this parliament is a house of european democracy. anti-semitism is a denial of democracy. when we defend jewish life, when we promote jewish life along with the life of all communities in europe, when we fight discrimination, when we protect minorities, we defend european democracy. defending european democracy means fighting anti-semitism and all discrimination. it was therefore no coincidence that those who planned, organized and perpetrated the genocide of the jews were also the ones who in 1933 burned down the reichstag, the german parliament. this building was symbolically restored some 60 years later and re-inhabited by the bundestag, when democracy was restored throughout reunified germany. fighting anti-semitism today means fighting the hate speech and the conspiracy theories that have taken root and thrive, especially online. and this parliament, the forum for european democratic public debate, is a place where this essential battle is being waged. finally, i would like to return to the holocaust and the crucial role that this event plays in shaping our european spirit, our european vision. the european union is the strongest and most decisive response to the tragedy of the second world war: a project of peace, of solidarity, of shared prosperity, a political project, forward-looking and founded on solid values. and it is this tragedy, the holocaust, that has instilled in us europeans a special sense of duty: the duty to defend and promote respect for human dignity and freedom here at home and throughout the world. the duty to fight all that discriminates, all that weakens, and all that dispossesses or even excludes, as the jews have been in our european history. this duty, this fight, is no different than the fight for democracy. they are one, they are the same. dear friends, it is precisely because we have experienced the unspeakable horror of the holocaust that today we work so intensely for europe, its home to democracy. democracy: this is our struggle. for democracy is never fully won, never won forever. but it is the worthiest of battles. it requires commitment from all of us, just like the tireless commitment shown by you, margot friedländer. we would like to express to you, with all due solemnity, our admiration and gratitude for your poignant, powerful message, which gives us a shiver, which inspires us with emotion, but also with hope and strength. - thank you. - thank you. (applause) i would like to thank
president of the commission. – president metsola, president michel, honourable members, liebe frau friedländer. i think it was hard for all of us to hold back our tears when we were listening to you – you, margot friedländer, a survivor, a witness of the greatest tragedy in european history. you told us about the images that got stuck in a young woman’s mind and in her memory; the horror you witnessed. you told us of how hard it was to find your way back to life. and yes, this was the nazis’ plan: not just to exterminate millions of people based on ethnicity, religion or sexual orientation, but also to take humanity out of the people, to strip them of all dignity, all identity, all love for life. but your testimony, margot friedländer, and your energy at 100 years shows that dignity and identity are indestructible. i can’t even imagine how hard it must be, liebe frau friedländer, to bring these memories back to the surface. and i want to thank you from the bottom of my heart. it is the greatest service, the greatest act of love for all of us and future generations. because you make us see. you make us free. our freedom is built on the memory of the holocaust. because the very reason why our union was founded lies in two simple words: never again. nie wieder. my gratitude is even greater, as a german with europe in her heart. because i feel the deep scar in my country’s history and the indelible responsibility in our collective history. we want to keep our eyes wide open. we want to understand what happened and why, so it never happens again. and you, liebe frau friedländer, made us see and made us understand. there is one thing about your life that strikes me immensely – you spoke about it. indeed, right after the war, you decided to leave germany and europe because the pain was too big and the past would never get past you. but then, 20 years ago, you decided to come back. even if the memory was still alive – in fact, precisely because the memory had to remain alive – you came back so that history could never come back. and you explained your decisions, as we’ve just heard, with the words: what happened can’t be changed. but now it’s up to you, the next generation. and yes, the next generation deserve to hear your story. they became your mission. and today i would like to tell you that your mission is our mission too. it is because of this mission that we have to come up with our thoughts and our work. and this is why we came with our first strategy to combat anti—semitism and to foster jewish life in europe. we have listened to the warning that is coming from the people like you. anti—semitism has not disappeared. it still poisons our societies. a stunning 70 percent of european jews don’t feel safe when wearing a kippah or a star of david. and throughout the pandemic, jews have been the target of all sorts of conspiracy theories and hate speech. but this time we won’t close our eyes. nie wieder. never again. preserving the memory is the first step to avoid that history repeats itself. with the strategy, we are working so that all schools in europe teach the history of the shoah. we want to raise youth ambassadors of remembrance as the next generation who will keep the memory alive. and we will encourage students to learn about europe’s jewish culture and tradition. for instance, just 10 days ago, jewish people around the world planted trees to celebrate tu bishvat, the year of trees. our children should know about it, because it’s part of our european culture and history and because it teaches us to take care of our planet and our future. liebe margot friedländer, honourable members, today we have listened to the story of how life and dignity were taken away from millions right here in europe. people were killed and humiliated, their names replaced by numbers on their forearm – and all of this just because of who they were. the union we want to build is a place where everyone can be whoever they want to be. a place where a jewish woman like simone veil, a survivor of the holocaust, can rise to become the president of this proud european parliament. a place where everyone is entitled to the same rights and is treated with the same dignity. and this is what keeping the memory alive truly needs: a continent that is finally united in its beautiful diversity. vielen dank. thank you, liebe margot friedländer, for sharing your memories with us. long live europe. thank you. the next item is the debate on the 20th anniversary of the euro. i would invite you, dear colleagues, to watch a short video together. (a video on the history of the euro was shown in the chamber.) president lagarde, commissioner gentiloni, dear colleagues, 20 years since it was first introduced, the euro is the currency of over 340 million people in 19 eu member states. a political, financial, social project that brought people together, that challenged perceptions and cemented what europe is all about. the biggest monetary changeover in history created beneficial impacts on people, businesses and governments alike, with 78% of respondents of the latest eurobarometer survey stating that having the euro is a good thing for the european union. the single currency also unquestionably propelled europe forward, as a key player in the european and the global economy. the stability of our currency makes it attractive for businesses around the world to trade with the eu. in fact, more than half of the eu’s exports and almost 40% of all global cross—border payments use the euro. sixty other countries and territories around the world have chosen to use the euro as their currency or to peg their own currency to it. the euro is truly one of the greatest achievements of the european union. a single currency to complete the single market, guaranteeing long—term prosperity, growth, competitiveness, protection from exchange-rate volatility, and price stability. an economic and monetary union which ensures the confidence of our citizens. this is what the euro is all about. it is about european integration, unity, stability, identity and solidarity, and it is right here in our pockets as a tangible success story. when there is a vision for the future of europe and the political willingness to match it, anything is possible. and we have also seen this when the euro was faced with great economic challenges. the global financial and sovereign debt crises led to a reform of the governance structure of the european monetary union where we, the european parliament, played an important role as co—legislators. and together we acted swiftly to support our member states and stabilise our economies. two years ago, we again joined forces to provide flexible solutions to our member states, as we faced the economic challenges of the covid—19 pandemic. we took bold decisions and adopted an unprecedented eur 750 billion recovery plan – next generation eu – which is already supporting our economic recovery. this would not have been possible for the eu without the euro. the euro is resilient. nevertheless, challenges continue to lie ahead. we must ensure that the euro is fit for future generations. generations who will know the euro as their only currency. and for this, the european union must be at the forefront of green and digital transitions. with over half of green bond issuance denominated in euro, and with our plan to digitalise our economies, including by launching the digital euro, we are on the right track. we must continue on this trajectory. by reinforcing the international role of the euro we can contribute to a resilient eu banking sector and international financial system while strengthening the eu’s open strategic autonomy. with its credibility and cemented foundations, the euro will continue to grow and we will remain united. ultimately, we should never lose sight of what this project is all about. why it started, how it got us here and where it can take us. so here is to the next 20 years.
president of the european central bank. madam president of the european parliament, mrs metsola, mr commissioner for economic affairs, mr gentiloni, honourable members of the european parliament, it is a pleasure for me to be here in strasbourg on the occasion of the twentieth anniversary of the euro, which we are celebrating. it is also a pleasure to see that when the euro was launched, it was also a woman who was president and that, finally, those who would have forgotten the twenty years between the two probably feel that the european parliament is still chaired by a woman. so, the introduction of the euro banknotes and coins in 2002 was a milestone for european history, as it put as nicole fontaine said a tangible sign of european integration in the pocket and in the wallets of all europeans. there were twelve countries only to begin with, and seven more to join later on. sharing a currency is more than just simply using the same measure, the same means of payment; it is part of a common endeavour. this feeling of shared identity is actually recognised by our fellow citizens. when you ask them, when you ask european citizens what the european union means to them, what comes second is actually the euro among the list of things that they most often think of in terms of the european union. the euro has simplified the lives of many europeans those who study, those who travel, those who conduct business. it has improved the efficiency of conducting business throughout an enlarged economic zone and i'm going to mention to you two numbers. from 1990 to 2002, trade between the soontobe 19 countries of the euro area increased by less than 5% 5% more trade. if you look at the period since the euro has been introduced, trade between those same nations did not increase by 5% but by 200%. now, of course not all of it is attributable to the euro, but the fact that the exchange rate was no longer an issue that was to be discussed, put into contractual terms between parties, certainly had a role to play. the second element that i want to bring to your attention as well: as you all know, the euro is the second international currency in the world after the dollar. what maybe not all of us know so well is that in 2021 about half of total green bond issuance around the world was denominated in euros not necessarily issued from the euro area or europe, but around the world. so as you mentioned, madam president, last year we launched the digital euro project. we will investigate how a digital euro could offer a convenient, cost-free means of payment, allowing people to pay anywhere in the euro area with risk-free digital money for example, when making payments online, which preclude the use of cash. i would just like to add that, of course, this digital euro will not replace cash but it will complement cash. that is one of the reasons why we launched the process of redesigning our banknotes. the redesign is part of a long-term development process to ensure that the next generation of euro banknotes will continue to be secure, accepted and an efficient means of payment. we will keep parliament informed throughout the process, and we will seek input from citizens, through your intermediary, on the theme and design of the new banknotes before making our final decision in 2024. thereafter, the rollout of the new banknotes will be planned. the redesign of our banknotes is a unique opportunity to make them more relatable to europeans of all ages and backgrounds, thereby offering a new symbol of european integration. for more than 20 years, euro banknotes and coins have circulated smoothly. they are a tangible testament to the euro's success. the cash in our pockets reminds us that, despite our diversity, we share a common endeavor that goes way beyond our borders.
member of the commission. – madam president, madam president of the european central bank, honourable members, we are celebrating today one of the most tangible symbols of european integration and prosperity. since its launch, the single currency has come a long way. during this time, the euro area has grown from 12 countries to 19, and it will expand further in the near future. croatia and bulgaria are advancing towards this target. the euro has delivered on its promises: the promise of a strong and stable currency underpinning the cohesion of our economies; the promise to lower barriers for businesses and make it cheaper to borrow and invest; the promise to make our economies more resilient by improving the conditions for fully exploiting our single market, as president lagarde just said. during the pandemic, having a single currency has been instrumental in coordinating responses across europe and in avoiding further economic and social divergences. and today, as you remember, president metsola, four in five citizens in the euro area think that the euro is a good thing – the highest level of support since its introduction. with next generation eu, the union will become one of the major debt issuers worldwide. this will offer benefits in terms of stability and that of the european bond market, by providing a euro-denominated, safe asset, covering all maturities. and we see a huge appetite for this. as of today, we have already issued eur 78.5 billion in next generation eu bonds and every issuance was heavily oversubscribed. by 2026, we will have issued close to eur 1 trillion, providing an opportunity to establish global reference for international investors seeking to invest in euro-denominated assets. and with the issuance of up to eur 250 billion in green bonds, we will also become a world leader in the international green bond market. so today, we are celebrating, but this opportunity should also be a reminder of the need to complete and deepen our monetary union. this means completing the banking union and making further steps on the capital markets union. it also means learning the right lesson from our coordinated fiscal response to the crisis and from the unprecedented tools we have put in place. in today’s multipolar economic system, there is scope to further increase the role of the euro. in today’s geopolitical tension and uncertainty, there is scope to advance in the process of our strategic autonomy. the same political determination of those who designed and made possible the euro’s introduction, overcoming divergent views and interests, is needed now to build a stronger union.
chair the econ committee. – madam president, 20 years is an important anniversary for anyone, but i think that if we look back at all that the euro has been through, well, then we realise how special this anniversary is. at first, the introduction of the single currency in europe seemed like a risky bet. many feared it was too early. others thought that there were other priorities. there was some fear about it. and later, after the financial crisis, many thought that the euro was at risk and that a restoration of national currencies was upcoming. well, it turned out quite differently, and not only is the euro still here, but it’s stronger than ever. other member states have decided to adopt it, and others will soon be able to do so. the truth is that the euro has been the key element of the last 20 years of the union. it has complemented the single market. it has made it easier to trade and travel and live across different countries in the european union. it has boosted european value chains, strengthening our economies. it has been the prerequisite for preserving the european monetary sovereignty in a globalised world. it has represented the cornerstone of a peaceful union and a global actor after the end of the cold war and german reunification. without the euro, i’m quite sure we would be telling quite different stories. the success of the euro is not a matter of chance. its strength, its value and its very existence has constantly been guaranteed and preserved by the european central bank. over the past 20 years, the ecb has been the true guardian of the union’s currency, and i’m confident that it will continue to play its role even in the coming years of big innovations and changes. we’ve heard about some of those. but the euro’s success was also the result of a strong political will. the euro was the best investment that we could have made to ensure a stable environment for the new generations. this awareness was the fundamental element that allowed us to overcome even the most difficult moments, and we’ve heard just a few minutes ago about some of those moments. the euro has definitely brought to us many successes, but we have to also to acknowledge and to recognise that it has not delivered all the gains that it could have delivered in all the member states and to all the european citizens. and this is partly due to some national issues and partly the result of the fact that the economic and monetary union is still incomplete – as commissioner gentiloni just reminded us. so, the way ahead, therefore, is to identify the changes that are necessary to make our monetary union work for the benefit of everybody. the experience of the euro shows that even the riskiest bets, if supported by political will, can be successful and can mark a great leap forward in the history of our union.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, madam president lagarde, since this dossier was drawn up, many things have certainly changed: the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the commission notes that the bank's capital adequacy ratio is not a reliable indicator of the level of risk that banks face in the long term. the ambitious green objectives must also be read with pragmatism. high environmental standards must go hand in hand with economic sustainability. we must avoid that excessive rigidity, combined with a lack of realism, can benefit third countries to the detriment of european countries. smes, which account for 99% of all businesses in the european union and employ some 100 million people, need liquidity. they are the engine of economic recovery. we must therefore return to an economy in which the productive world is financed, not an economy in which finance is for finance. families need jobs and only by financing the real economy can new job opportunities be created. in this connection, mr president, you made extensive use of two terms in your last press conference on thursday 3 february and also during the monetary dialogue in the econ committee: uncertainty and flexibility. however, the markets sensed uncertainty about future choices and prompted an immediate rate hike. in the face of the falcons and doves on the ecb's board, we would like to make sure that future decisions are based on economic analysis and not on mere political processes. the ecb will be at a crossroads in the coming months and we hope that you, mr president, will be able to take the right path.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the ecb has certainly acted courageously during the pandemic. the programme of purchases of government bonds and an accommodative monetary policy have helped to bring about the current economic recovery, but we would like it to be fairer to all citizens. the ecb's annual report attests to the good work done, although there are at least two major challenges ahead: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem. raising interest rates too suddenly would risk a sovereign debt crisis and we cannot afford that; we are continuing to use unconventional monetary policy instruments to help businesses and households. second emergency: the ecb can help in the fight against money laundering and tax evasion. we must remember that much of tax evasion and money laundering also takes place through banks, and that they also rely on tax havens, which also exist within this europe.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the european directives on animal welfare, despite the studies and efforts made by the european parliament, have made it possible to achieve structural changes in certain farming methods - the directives on laying hens, pigs and calves - but they have had a modest impact if we look at the general directive and the poultry directive. the most important limitation within the union seems to be the lack of uniformity: the directives do not specify in detail the requirements for compliance and control procedures, with unacceptable consequences. the european labelling systems for animal welfare, which are mainly private, are all voluntary and in fact very different in terms of their operation and design, and there is no prospect of mandatory and uniform labelling in the eu. the commission should therefore proceed with directives to make them more effective, to improve their readability and precision and to bring them into line with the member states. the health crisis reveals the importance of proper livestock management, including for our health and that of our children. we listen to industry and medical professionals to find a definitive, pragmatic and realistic approach to both breeding and commercial practices.

in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, i am afraid we have some problems of scale, in the sense that there are international advisory reports estimating that potential offshore wind production will be proportionally much lower than the 60 gigawatts target that the european union, alone, wants to achieve by 2030. i have a question that is not a secondary question. the 800 billion euro of investment that the commission wants to mobilise by 2050 - where do we expect to find it? we should not underestimate the problems of coexistence with other maritime activities, such as fishing and shipping. nor do we minimize the problems of maintenance costs on the high seas, the wear and tear that wave impact and salinity inevitably bring to offshore wind farms. i say it again with conviction: renewables can be the future and it is right to promote them, but a real and painless energy transition needs above all programmable sources and bills that are affordable for businesses and households. so if we do not take this realistic approach, we are left with good, expensive intentions that often break down with cold reality.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the period of validity of the directive to the period of 1 january 1999. the commission has already made a number of proposals. the best way to fight cancer is to prevent it from developing in the first place. that is why my group and i have concentrated very much on the prevention aspect. i was particularly concerned about the protection of our children and i am glad that this is reflected throughout the report. children are becoming obese, which can lead to cancer in old age. the figures are more than alarming. the industry is now unable to stop its efforts to supply sugar, salt and fat products to adolescents. it is therefore only logical to prohibit advertising of such products for children. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of consumers in the event of a serious accident. and that applies especially to parents. of course, the tightening of consumer protection is always a fight against economic interests. but if it is scientifically proven that, for example, all alcohol consumption, even in moderation, is harmful, why should we not warn against it? this does not prevent anyone from drinking their glass of wine, but it protects young people in particular and informs consumers. i wonder what the objection is to this, if not lower sales or lower product placement. the label must not be reduced to a promotional information. and i have to be very clear here: no profit before health. the report does not only cover obvious risk factors for cancer, such as alcohol and cigarettes, but also other cancer-causing factors, such as environmental toxins and influences. prevention of cancer also involves reducing air, food, water and soil pollution, as well as exposure to chemicals. a healthy diet is no less important. however, this report goes beyond prevention. it also places a clear emphasis on access to cancer treatment for all. protecting yourself from cancer and getting good treatment and medication should not be a question of your wallet. we also advocate the right to be forgotten and stronger crossborder cooperation and better data exchange, especially in the treatment of childhood cancer. in short, we have a strong and ambitious report. i call on my colleagues not to weaken it. and i say to the commission: go beyond that!
on behalf of the id group. madam president, commissioner, rapporteur, as coordinator of the identity and democracy group, i have been able to demonstrate during the year and a half of operation of our special committee that coordinated european action has brought little or no added value to the national plans of certain countries, in particular france, whose current 10-year strategy, the fifth part of a very successful action, justifies its being taken as a model for this text. this model is good in that it prioritises prevention, both in the exposure of each person to all carcinogens and in excessive individual behaviours, such as excessive smoking, excessive drinking, junk food or sedentary behaviour. this is more than 40 percent of cancers that could be prevented. yes, also, mass screening and early detection, which are indispensable tools. yes, also, to targeted and holistic research into the causes, diagnoses, treatment and follow-up of our patients. yes to data exchange, particularly useful in cases of rare cancers. yes to access to quality care for all, without geographical or financial gaps. yes to the right to be forgotten, especially for young survivors. yes to caregiver support, and yes, in general, to any idea of common sense. unfortunately, in the course of the text, in the name of 'equal care for all', which is essential but not so defined, you can take advantage of it to prepare a legislative path to supranational constraints, which we consider unacceptable. the use of alcohol or tobacco is akin to compulsion, even if it is only a matter of celebratory behaviour, reward or, for some, even the forgetting of poverty. the european wine sector itself is in danger. what about food, where meat is increasingly stigmatized, all in favor of artificial food, which would have a perfect nutritional content? on behalf of the ecr group. – madam president, this is a great and powerful document, i believe, and i’m proud to be part of it. i’m already using it, even if it’s not finalised, to put pressure on the italian regional authorities to increase the screening programme and to increase the funding on new screening and therapeutic techniques. also, we already started locally, based on these programmes, to increase the quality of life of cancer patients through eras (enhanced recovery after surgery), which takes into account the entire system before and after the hospital stay. on the other side, i’m not happy with some decisions regarding prevention. i presented some amendments to correct this. first of all, the increase in taxation and limitation for alcohol and cigarettes has always been a counter—productive approach, potentially causing illegal and criminal activity but also really bad behaviour, especially in the poor sector of the population. second, i am a strong believer that there is a difference between moderate and excessive alcohol consumption. this was a big discussion, but we all know that the mediterranean diet has shown an increase in lifespan through the general population. third, i strongly believe that alternatives to traditional smoke is an important tool to reduce smoking in europe. it’s never been shown a positive outcome in reducing bad habits through taxation, as we have seen, and through banning marketing, as we have seen for cigarettes. to conclude, the report, again, is a very powerful tool, and we should all use it in a very aggressive and powerful way for the good of all european citizens.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat cancer, and i am sure that the commission will take them into account. cancer is the disease of this century and the european union must fight it with all its might, as it did with the coronavirus. we need concrete measures, more investment in research and no more inequalities in treatment. everyone should have the same opportunity for treatment, and i'm thinking particularly of children. the 10 flagship initiatives and 32 supporting actions envisaged in the european parliament's report deal with causes such as smog and malnutrition, but we must avoid easy slogans. responsible wine consumption is quite different from alcohol abuse. let us focus on promoting new, healthy and balanced lifestyles, prevention, early diagnosis and quality of life for the sick. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council. today we dedicate this day to them, we dedicate our commitment as european legislators to them. together we can defeat this terrible evil.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, this report unfortunately contains an indiscriminate attack on the wine industry, it has hit an entire production sector. we are the europe of regions, of local excellence and of particularities, which cannot all be put in the same pot. responsible consumption of wine, of quality wine such as the italian, cannot be considered the same as the abuse of alcoholic beverages and cannot be compared to smoking or tobacco. the black letter 'f' that is used to mark our national wines, our local products, in order to encourage large-scale distribution, is damaging the work of thousands of farms and family businesses which, i would remind you, with passion and love for their land, cultivate the vines and produce our wines, which are known throughout the world. i would invite those who are making this crazy proposal to drink responsibly a glass of good friulian wine, a good ribolla gialla, a prosecco doc, all excellent wines from my land, and then to visit our farms, made by proud entrepreneurs who, do not doubt, will also teach you the education, moderation and responsibility to avoid harmful risky alcohol consumption. this is clearly the way to go, not the useless, misleading and, yes, harmful labels like nutriscore.
the fight against cancer is a difficult challenge that must be met with courage and determination. as a doctor, i've always fought to raise awareness about prevention, and that can be done by providing higher quality care, ensuring access to essential medicines and opening up to new technologies. however, there are some points which need to be clarified, particularly with regard to the consumption of alcohol. the european commission also talks about reducing harmful alcohol consumption as a key priority in the european plan to beat cancer, and i think it is right to determine which habits and foods are harmful to health. but moderate use and harmful use of alcohol, and therefore abuse, are not the same thing. this should be clear from the text, otherwise a very important economy and culture for our country is seriously endangered. the mediterranean diet is a precious treasure that is part of our lifestyle and provides us with a rich and healthy diet, including responsible consumption of wine. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the wine sector is not affected by the increase in alcohol consumption. so let's promote healthy lifestyles, healthy eating habits, and join together once and for all in the fight against cancer.

the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, i am honoured to finally be here today after more than a year of work to present the fourth revision of directive 2004/37/ec on the protection of workers from carcinogens and mutagens. it has been a long and sometimes complicated process, but i have worked with dedication and commitment to bring about the amendments to the directive for which i am the rapporteur. i did so in the knowledge that europe can take concrete action to improve the working conditions and health of workers. my own personal history makes me particularly sensitive to the question: i've worked in health care and in the last few years i've had to fight three cancers and i've only recently finished treatment. these experiences have had a major influence on my political commitment from the first day in this parliament. i have worked with dedication and with the knowledge that cancer can be defeated and my commitment to the special committee against cancer is an example of this. i have discussed this with everyone, with the social partners, with the companies and with the unions. i think it is essential that this report gives concrete answers to the workers and citizens of europe who have been waiting for answers to safety at work for too long. cancer is the leading cause of work-related death in the european union. in fact, 52% of work-related deaths are due to occupational cancers. the european commission has made the fight against cancer one of the priorities of the five-year period 2019-2024. the commission estimates that 40% of cancers in europe are preventable. increased worker protection, together with a reduction or elimination of hazards, goes in the direction of reducing workers' exposure to dangerous substances. this amendment is the fourth legislative proposal for the directive and aims to establish new occupational exposure limit values for three substances: acrylonitrile, nickel compounds and benzene. the new amendment to the directive, as proposed by the commission, aims to ensure a new level of occupational protection for over one million workers across europe. the sectors concerned are the oil, textile, manufacturing, construction and chemical sectors. during the discussion new proposals were put forward, all of which were taken into consideration. it is with great satisfaction that the directive known as cmd will henceforth be called cmrd. in fact, we have added to the substances already provided reproductive toxicants, that is, substances harmful to reproduction. not only do these substances fall within the scope of the directive, but we have also drawn up a list of 11 substances which have harmful effects on fertility. this is a great victory for the protection of the health of workers, especially those working in the health sector. it is estimated that at least 2 million people in europe are exposed to these substances. another major goal has been reached with regard to dangerous medicines. in fact, there are several drugs, some of them fundamental to the fight against cancer, that can have serious effects on those who handle them and those who prepare them. for the first time we have been able to open up the debate on this subject. this is a huge step forward for the entire hospital industry. every year, 12 million healthcare workers in europe are potentially exposed to dangerous medicines. i think of the doctors, the nurses, the lab technicians, all the people who work in hospitals. i would like to thank them for being on the front line of the fight against the pandemic during these difficult years. the commission will have to define these medicines, draw up a list and give guidelines. we have also asked the commission to prepare an action plan to include 25 more substances in the directive and to address crystalline silica, which can have serious long-term effects. despite the difficulties in carrying out this report, we are here today with an excellent result, which was expected by all those involved in the decision-making process: workers, companies and governments. we have all acted with one common objective, the health of workers, and i believe we have made a huge step forward in ensuring greater protection and safety.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, in italy we have 1 200 deaths at work every year, an average of three a day, a real daily massacre. these losses add up to 8 000 deaths per year from exposure to carcinogens at the workplace, which occur in silence and with the utmost indifference of institutions, the media and society. at least 5% of these malignancies are due to exposure to carcinogens at work; only 20 percent of these cancers are identified and recognized as such, with the effect of destroying not only the lives of those who have them, but also their families, who are often left completely to fend for themselves. the agreement we are about to vote on strengthens the levels of protection against carcinogens in order to protect workers and prevent the formation of cancers. and finally, i say we do it with a different approach, because we've learned from the pandemic that health can't be considered a cost, but a fundamental investment for individual well-being and the common good. social justice is therefore more closely linked to the inalienable right to health and dignity than ever before, a prerequisite for a truly equitable and sustainable recovery that leaves no one behind.
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i have listened very carefully to your speeches. i believe that this report was a tribute to all european citizens who have been affected by cancer at work over the years. i thank the council, i thank the commission, i thank all my colleagues. i would like to thank the former president for the work we have done together. thank you, lucia! today, together, we have added an important piece to the fight against cancer, leaving our political colours behind. today is a great day for european workers. thank you, sir.
