rapporteur. – madam president, when the commission opened the 2022 semester cycle last autumn, war in europe was not even in our worst nightmares. on the contrary, there were many hopes that the union could leave behind the difficult months of the covid pandemic and start to face more decisively the digital, environmental and social challenges. when we started to draft this report, we thought – and we still believe – that the semester is the right tool with which to coordinate all these efforts in order to maximise their effects at union level. when the semester cycle started, the economic situation we faced was quite positive and expectations were good, although we still started to notice that were some elements that could have jeopardised the strength of the recovery in 2022. first of all, we had problems of labour shortages that have involved many service sectors in the reopening phase, that in the coming months could have affected other sectors, like logistics, transport and activities related to information technologies. second, we were still dealing with covid. we were still dealing with the new variants, we were afraid of new waves, and there were low rates of vaccination in some parts of the world and of europe as well. last but not least, we started to observe a sharp rise in energy prices and, for this reason, also of the inflation rate. the succession of these elements could of course have led to some slowdown, but with an adequate policy mix and a full coordination of monetary and fiscal policy action, both at the european and national level, we were optimistic and positive that we could preserve the good prospects for 2022. and then the war. the war has drastically changed this scenario. the tough but necessary sanctions against putin’s regime are the right response to his barbaric attack on ukraine, but we know they will have an important impact on our economies. we are, of course, willing to shoulder these costs. we will. we have to. but we have to be ready. we have to prepare. if the situation in ukraine does not improve, we could have considerable consequences on the availability of some goods and above all, of course, energy products. this will result in an increase not only of energy prices, but also of the prices of other important commodities that will affect various sectors like agriculture, food industries and others. in this regard, i see three possible risks that we have to be aware of and prepare to address. the first relates to the ability of the downstream market to absorb price increases. the risk that i see is that this could lead to a slowdown in aggregated demand, delaying the time of recovery or slowing it down compared to what we expected before. the second factor is the possible effect on wage dynamics, which could occur, of course, if the shock were to extend over a longer period of time. this will also have an impact on inflation. the third factor is that a prolonged energy crisis and, in particular, a possible asymmetric effect in energy costs for the union would also lead to a loss of competitiveness for european industry and also to divergences in the single market. on top of that, a prolonged phase of tension on energy prices would have consequences not only on inflation, as we have seen in the past months, but also on industry costs by influencing the investment decisions of manufacturing companies. therefore the risk is that we could see a phase of investment postponements or rescheduling. this is possible despite the high level of current demand. so against this complex and worrisome background, i really think that all policy tools should be used to manage this delicate phase and to do it in the most appropriate, incisive way. first, eu institutions will have to empower eu governments to rise to these epochal challenges. this is why i welcome the european commission’s willingness to adapt its fiscal guidance for 2023 to the new realities. this is not the time to go back to fiscal rules that were meant for completely different moments. they are not adequate to face the challenge that we have ahead and we need to support member states in keeping people safe and confident for the future. but second we also need to put in place an eu response, eu tools, as we did for covid. we usually say that we have to learn from past mistakes, but sometimes we also have to learn from what worked, from past success. if we look back at how we managed the covid crisis, if we look back at what worked, the key factors that explain the eu success in managing the consequences of the covid crisis were putting in place the right policy mix, balancing monetary policy, fiscal policies and effective coordination in the policy actions of member states, and the introduction of new european tools that were adequate to the challenge that we faced, tools that made us stronger, more united, and that allowed us to go back to a pre-crisis level of growth much faster than anybody would have thought. so let us approach this new phase with the same spirit, and i’m sure we can make it.
madam president, commissioners, madam minister, ladies and gentlemen, how long will we have to wait before even the most ardent supporters of austerity and austerity are convinced that we are at the last beach to change the whole of the union's failing economic governance? how long will we have to wait before all the automatic mechanisms, starting with the stability pact, end their ill effects on european economies? over the past two decades, the average gdp growth of the euro area countries has been considerably lower than the average of all other oecd countries. what is to happen beyond the pandemic and the vicious war on our eastern borders, which is literally upsetting our concept of democracy and our energy and raw material supply plans on a planetary level? the political and material effects of energy dependence on third countries are not enough to add to the green and digital policies a further dependence on technologies and raw materials imported from areas which are now dominating our development? i therefore call on this parliament to undertake a rapid, serious and immediate review of the rigidly outlined objectives of the next generation eu agenda to allow the respective national plans to be re-formulated in terms of timeliness and sustainability, otherwise the union will continue to be more and more a giant with feet of clay. finally, the rules of the current stability pact force over-indebted member countries to use funds with next generation eu conditionalities which, as specified in the commission's latest autumn economic forecast, will produce low growth multipliers, while other countries will instead turn to autonomous debt towards investments with high multipliers, generating the paradoxical effect of increasing asymmetries among the european economies themselves. let us meditate on these considerations.

in writing. – the european union is and must remain a union of peace. all eu member states together already have the second highest military expenditure in the world. i am therefore extremely critical of the additional militarisation of the eu, including increases in defence spending – which are also largely bypassing the european parliament and its scrutiny function. as a member of a neutral state, i would also like to stress the role that neural countries can play in regards to mediation, dialogue, civil crisis aid, humanitarian engagement and reconstruction efforts after a war. we need an additional investment programme for climate and social infrastructure, not new military armament!
in writing. thank you, prime minister kallas, for your excellent speech. it has been encouraging to see the unity of not only the eu but also the entire western alliance in responding to russian aggression and supporting ukraine. estonia, together with the other baltic states, has set a strong example for the eu as a whole. ukraine needs all our support in the fight. we must stop the russian savage attacks. it was a very important opening that the eu also directly finances the acquisition of arms by ukraine. next, eu member states must stop buying oil and gas from russia. it would really hit the russian economy once and for all. it is intolerable that while we are sanctioning the russian economy to a great extent, we are simultaneously financing the war with hundreds of millions of dollars in energy purchases every day. the eu's dependence on russian energy has made us weak. as a result, eu countries were not able to sanction russia strongly enough immediately after the illegal occupation of crimea. it was only now that the great war of aggression had begun that real action was taken. the eu's energy dependence must be brought to an end. this week, the european commission presented a repowereu action to break the energy dependence on russia. however, the timetable is disappointing, as the commission aims to end the dependency only by 2030. we need to move much faster. eu member states must phase out the use of russian fossil fuels and increase investment in clean technologies and energy storage. of course, stopping energy purchases will hit member countries, but so will war. all the other banks in russia must be disconnected from the swift system. the same action must be taken against belarus, which has supported the russian aggression.
in writing. russia's aggression against ukraine is a reckless act that we have a duty to counter. however, in the last few days i have heard some colleagues talk too lightly about war and military intervention by europe, without considering the consequences and the risks that could result: i think that the sanctions were an important step, but now it must be diplomacy that leads us to the resolution of the conflict. nevertheless, it must also be recognised that the eu's diplomatic effort and mediation capacity is made much more difficult by its energy dependence on russia. the inability to be self-sufficient and diversify our suppliers has left us vulnerable to the russian regime's demands and risks reversing the economic burden of the war on our citizens. this same energy dependency is also showing the most critical aspects of the green transition. in a matter of weeks, we went from "disagreements" on green taxonomy to unanimous approval of using nuclear and coal-fired power plants, emphasizing the sharp distinction between ideological and pragmatic goals. i would like to conclude by welcoming the intention of several countries to renew their commitment to nato spending. i believe this crisis reminds us of the importance of the trust and alliance we have with our partners overseas.
in writing. in the face of russian aggression and invasion of ukraine, the european union has come together in a coordinated and robust response driven by the free and democratic expression of its public views. this response must, however, also expose its weaknesses, in particular its external dependence on security and defence, its energy dependence and its relative lack of protection against disinformation and cyber attacks. the good coordination in the context of nato, with a focus on the transatlantic axis of global security and coordination of member states' efforts, enabled the vice-president of the european commission and high representative for foreign policy to state that the russian invasion of ukraine marked 'the birth of a geopolitical european union'. this proclamation has meaning. diplomacy, although it remains decisive, is no longer enough for the european union to guarantee its relevance and, above all, its security and defence. while maintaining the multilateral perspective which is at its core and its values and principles, the security and defence alliance must be strengthened and equipped with the means to ensure the protection of the union and to project its vision of peace and freedom in the global context.
the agenda includes the report by ms simona bonafè, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety, on the proposal for a regulation of the european parliament and of the council on batteries and battery waste, repealing directive 2006/66/ec and amending regulation (eu) 2019/1020 (com (2020) 0798 c9‐0400/2020 (2020/0353 cod)) (a9‐0031/2022).
the rapporteur. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, we need only look around this house to see that the use of batteries is already an integral part of everyone's daily life and will become even more so in the coming years if it is true, as it is true, that europe has set itself the objective of climate neutrality by 2050. mobility will become more sustainable, our cities will become smarter, and to focus more on renewable energy sources, we will need more and more batteries. in a nutshell, batteries will be the foundation on which to build our new paradigm of sustainable development. that is why the regulation we are discussing is so important. this is essential to create a real value chain in europe around this product, starting with the rules and standards in design and manufacture and ending with new targets in battery recovery and recycling. for the first time, european legislation takes into account the entire life cycle of a product. we're talking about the moment the product is conceived, then its design, to the end of life, as i said earlier. this new holistic approach - i would like to stress this - will henceforth be the rule for future legislation on sustainable products and the circular economy. and it's not by chance, it's not by chance that this holistic approach has been proposed for a product like batteries, which in the next few years will see its use increase significantly but also its application in new sectors. to give you some numbers: in fact, it is estimated that the use of batteries, for electric vehicles alone, will increase by 15 times from 2030 to 2035, not to mention that already today, already at this moment, more and more light transport means such as scooters, e-bikes and e-scooters are spreading. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the use of the strategic materials which underpin the technology, in particular lithium ions and therefore cobalt, lithium, nickel and manganese, which europe is unfortunately poor in and which are now completely dependent on imports, will increase at the same time. in order to keep pace with our global competitors, guaranteeing strategic autonomy in supply and exploiting the potential of new technologies, industrialisation policies using the principles of circularity will have to be put in place. the new regulation must therefore have a twofold objective: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of accidents. we must avoid the mistake made in the past with solar panels, for which the incentive to market is not matched by the creation of a new european industrial sector. the main sustainability requirements, introduced for the first time for batteries, are the maximum carbon footprint, minimum recycled content, performance standards, durability and removability. they are intended to become a benchmark for the entire global market and will apply to all types of batteries sold on the european market, including those imported from non-eu countries. to make batteries truly sustainable, it is not enough to respect the environment and avoid predatory actions on natural resources, but this must be combined with respect for social rights, one of which is the prohibition of the exploitation of child labour in the mines for the extraction of raw materials. we have set ourselves this objective by this regulation and have made due diligence mandatory for a specific product for the first time. therefore, all batteries placed on the market will be covered by these new rules. the increased use of batteries will also result in a higher amount of waste. for example, the number of lithium batteries ready for recycling is expected to increase 700-fold between 2020 and 2040. it is clear that ambitious quality and quantity collection for all categories of batteries is the essential prerequisite for implementing a circular model and creating an efficient market for raw and secondary materials. the introduction of mandatory requirements for extended producer responsibility and the increase in collection targets go exactly in this direction. in conclusion, i think we are all aware of the complexity of this legislation, which will also have to adapt to the evolution of new technologies, which are not yet fully mature. but we are faced with a challenge that requires an immediate response. we in parliament have taken it on board, and i hope the council will do the same, so that we can have ambitious legislation in the short term which will give the european union all the tools it needs to become a global leader in the battery market.
the commission has already submitted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, europe has often in the past made the mistake of not linking its environmental policies with a coherent industrial policy, as in the case of photovoltaic panels, a classic, and has thus created its dangerous dependence on other areas of the world, both for high-technology products and for low-technology products. we are now faced with the challenge of high-capacity batteries, which are essential for electric mobility, but also for the whole transition to renewable energy, as the rapporteur has well illustrated. many thought that it was too late, that the challenge was already lost for europe, but we believe that it is not, europe believes that it is not and that is why it is working to become a continent competitive in this production, both technologically and industrially, and to achieve its sovereignty as is often the case in our ambitions. that's why we've created the battery alliance, bringing together the world of research, business and public authorities, and in some ways we've also made progress, this very regulation makes progress: the commission has also proposed that the commission should be given the task of drawing up a report on the implementation of the directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster. the regulation on the one hand, the alliance for research and technological innovation, the pcis for joint projects of european interest, the gigafactories to create production capacity: only in this way, with all these instruments, can we have the best performing batteries in europe and the maximum recycling of their components. this is where our contribution as the itre committee and our ambition for the project has been. the challenge is won in europe if environmental policies are linked to industrial strategies, technological efforts and the full application of circular economy principles.
imco. - mr president, commissioner, the present sad state of affairs in the world shows more than ever that europe must build up its own battery cell production for the energy transition in order to be independent of third countries. we need 19 times as many batteries to meet the climate targets. i am very pleased with the compliments i have received that the dossier approved in the imco committee has been almost accepted. i'd like to pass on those compliments to simona bonafè. a new european strategic industrial policy was in the imco dossier and is in the dossier on which we are going to vote tonight and tomorrow. the same goes for protecting our environment, with a cycle from raw materials to batteries, from design to production and use to recycling. we wanted to ban the export of used batteries so that the raw materials could be kept within europe and reused for the production of new batteries. we also wanted a deposit system for consumer batteries, again to be able to reuse the raw materials and use the cash flow to promote the development of new battery cells made from european raw materials. i'm sorry that was taken out, but maybe it'll come out later. the ultimate aim is that europe should not and cannot be dependent on third countries for such an important sector as energy.
member of the commission. – madam president, let me start by sincerely thanking the european parliament for all the work that went into preparing the report that we are discussing this afternoon and on which you will vote later this evening. i will not be able to name all of you, but representing the lead committee on the environment, public health and food safety (envi), as well as the associated committees, the committee on the internal market and consumer protection (imco), the committee on industry, research and energy (itre) and the committee on transport and tourism (tran), i would address my thanks in particular to the rapporteur, simona bonafè, as well as to antonius manders, patrizia toia and ismail ertug. thanks to your relentless work on this important file over the last years and months and to your commitment to proceeding as fast as possible to build up a large support for your mandate, we are here today close to starting trilogues once the council has adopted its position next week at the environment council. in the light of russia’s unprecedented attack on ukraine and the imperative of making europe independent from fossil fuels, a new regulatory framework for the eu battery value chain is more needed and urgent than ever before. the fact that no less than four of your committees were involved in examining our proposal is testimony to its strategic importance for the eu. the proposal for a new batteries regulation was our first legislative proposal implementing the european green deal and, in some respects, it constitutes a blueprint for other legislation to come on sustainable product policy. when we adopted it in december 2020, we did not anticipate the level of support it generated among the co-legislators and the enthusiasm that it developed. when preparing the proposal we tried to strike a careful balance between the level of ambition, the burden on economic operators and the feasibility of implementing the requirements and provisions proposed. looking at the amendments you proposed today, i can see that you clearly considered our proposals a good starting point. but i also know that you would like to go further and faster with many of the sustainability requirements and end—of—life aspects. in the coming weeks and months, we will engage as an honest broker in the political negotiations over the final text. changes will need to be assessed with care. the regulation should be ambitious and, at the same time, its implementation should be realistic. we need to keep the balance. but what matters most is that, in the end, we will have a forward-looking predictable regulatory framework which ensures the sustainability and the competitiveness of the emerging battery value chain in the eu. you can count entirely on our active and constructive support to ensure a successful and satisfactory outcome of those negotiations. many business decisions and investments are already being made in the light of the changes this new regulatory framework will bring when progressively rolled out over time. we need to deliver on these expectations with ambition and credibility. thank you very much for your attention, and i am looking forward to today’s debate.
draft opinion of the consultative committee on transport and tourism. the commission is not prepared to accept this. i cannot stress enough how important this report is to achieving our climate goals. a few weeks ago, we might have been talking about the environmental, ecological and social benefits of the battery regulation, which will put europe on the path to a sustainable and zero-emission future. but with the war in ukraine, nothing is the same as it was before. the report, ladies and gentlemen, is also about the strategic securing of raw materials for a rapidly growing battery industry and the acceleration of our clean energy transition. it has been mentioned that the recycling of valuable and environmentally critical materials from batteries will be a key to a sustainable and competitive battery industry that should be at the forefront of the world. the introduction of minimum targets for recycling efficiency and for the collection of waste batteries will make a significant contribution to this objective. from the transport point of view, i would particularly like to emphasize the extension to light transport and the abolition of the 2 kw threshold. we are therefore making the legislation future-proof by including e-bikes and so-called e-scooters in the regulation. the sector employs some 1000 small and mediumsized enterprises in europe, employing more than 155 000 skilled workers directly or indirectly. the bicycle industry alone generates a turnover of more than 18 billion euros. that is, with the battery regulation we will ensure that the markets for electric vehicles and e-bikes, with all the environmental and health benefits of these vehicles, continue to flourish and that these vehicles are brought to the european market.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the period of validity of the directive to the period of 1 january 1999. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. i think we all know, it's never been more clear and it's never been more urgent than in these days and hours: we need to do everything we can to move away from fossil fuels, and batteries are absolutely key technology for that. climate change, species extinction, pollution - all these crises we must confront with all our might. and that's what we need laws to do. i would like to thank you, commissioner, for an excellent piece of legislation. for the first time, we're thinking about product policy in a truly sustainable and holistic way, from the extraction of raw materials to low-carbon production with long life spans to real recycling. this battery regulation can be a real blueprint for future legislation on sustainable products. but of course, in some places we had to make the proposal more precise. first, we need a strong right to repair. all batteries in mobile phones, laptops and similar devices must be able to be replaced by consumers themselves. here, in the past, companies have really ignored the interests of people and the environment for long enough. second, real recycling for all batteries. the important raw materials like lithium, cobalt, nickel, not only have to be recycled, but then of course reused. this is the only way to make the step towards a truly circular economy. and thirdly, exploitation in a cobalt mine, for example, does not suddenly become irrelevant just because the raw material is then used in a small battery. the commission has also proposed that the commission should be able to take part in the negotiations on the future of the european union. all in all, together we have been able to put together a truly revolutionary package that civil society around the world is looking forward to, especially indigenous communities. ladies and gentlemen, let us lay the foundations today for clean batteries made in europe, for future-proof jobs in europe, clean technology for the much-needed energy transition, effective consumer protection and global justice.
on behalf of the ecr group. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of accidents at work is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done. this uncritical attitude towards electricity confirms an approach which is not only manichean but also lacking in a strategic vision which leads us straight to another energy dependence, that of china, a country which monopolizes, with the brutality typical of totalitarian regimes, the deposits of raw materials necessary for the construction of the batteries on which renewable sources depend. have you really not had enough of the lesson of these days? in the course of a race to reduce co2 emissions, which would affect only the 8% of our global emissions, we have given up extracting gas from our fields, we have given up gas pipelines and gasifiers, we have closed nuclear power stations, we have abandoned coal-fired power stations, which we would like to reopen in a hurry, but, above all, we have become slaves to the purchase of energy from russia, qatar and other regimes that do not care about liberalism, which prevents us, among other things, from effectively sanctioning putin's insane war in ukraine, a conflict that could even become apolitical. i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the community, but that we should be concerned about the future of the community. we worry about a one or two degree temperature increase over the long term, but our children ask us to get to the long term first.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, this european union action programme for the environment up to 2030, as well as the green deal and other climate proposals, in this context of crisis and war, now seem totally out of touch with reality. the enormous increases in energy prices are taking a dramatic toll on household and business bills, with increases of up to 500%, forcing many firms to even temporarily suspend production. in my country, italy, electricity bills have increased by 131% in one year, gas by 94%. the cost of gas has jumped to a maximum of 295 euros per megawatt hour, a year ago the same gas cost about 18 euros, so the price has increased tenfold. what is the european commission's response to such a momentous crisis? it is still empty of environmentalism, denial of reality and unrealistic attempts to oppose the use, at least temporarily, of existing coal, gas and nuclear. in this programme you propose the elimination by 2025 of all direct and indirect subsidies to fossil fuels at national level and thus also at the level of the whole european union. irresponsible choice, especially now. i also do not share your call for the best possible use of environmental taxation. the european union gets about 40 percent of its gas and 30 percent of its oil from russia, and our dependence on foreign energy instead of getting better has gotten worse in recent years because of the energy transition you've brought about, which has led to the closure of coal-fired power plants; i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the question of the energy tax. the commission's answers are still rather vague, and there is still talk of accelerating renewables, which will not help our continent in the short term. instead of thinking about alternative series to dependence on russia you gave birth to yet another brilliant idea: i would like to ask the commissioner to give us a clear answer to this question. moreover, with the surge in raw materials, there are thousands of farms and livestock farmers in total crisis. i hope that the ideas of reducing agricultural production by 30% and leaving 10% of agricultural land uncultivated to protect biodiversity will be immediately put to rest. i mean, it's time to stop with the "greta" ideology. i think that companies, consumers and households need a little seriousness and a little healthy realism, a little realpolitik.
mr. president, i have to ask you... without effective prevention, the number of accidents, illnesses and deaths at work cannot be effectively reduced, or even eliminated. workers must be protected from exposure to carcinogens or toxins. but there are many other things that can cause disease. standing for long periods, sitting, lifting heavy objects or continuous vibration. it is time to take seriously the problems of stress, anxiety, depression, and burnout, as well as the negative effects of working at the expense of leisure. so we can talk about a multifaceted problem, and prevention can play an important role in workplaces by promoting healthy lifestyles, encouraging sports and physical activity.

we shall now continue our debate on the report by mrs marianne vind (a9-0023/2022).
mr president, commissioner, first of all i would like to stress the importance of the new strategic framework for health and safety at work. this framework represents an important milestone in the protection of workers, which is more than necessary in view of the major changes in the labour market due to digitalisation, demographic changes, but also due to the pandemic. faced with new conditions, we must also set up new strategies to protect workers from adverse effects on their physical and mental health. in the labour market, new challenges are emerging in the form of the need to adapt the labour market for older workers or people who have overcome serious illness and want to re-enter the labour market after a long absence. it is for these workers that we need to develop effective strategies, including for example adaptation of the workplace or flexible work organisation. digitalisation itself, as well as the current pandemic, has shifted much of the work to online environments, but this has meant that many have had to work from home in often unfavourable conditions, combining work with caring for children or relatives. this, unfortunately, has been attributed to increased stress and psychological problems. we know very well that stress and mental discomfort also contribute to the development of various serious diseases, including autoimmune diseases, rheumatic, inflammatory and even oncological diseases. we have to reflect on all of this. let us therefore promote prevention and healthy lifestyles. we protect workers from the negative effects of the workplace, but also from stress and work-life imbalance. on the other hand, it is also necessary to think about the vulnerable and disadvantaged workers and the disabled who want to be fully integrated into the labour market. colleagues, we must do everything we can to ensure that everyone can reach their full potential. i also want to remember the hundreds of thousands of mostly women who have sought refuge with us from the war in ukraine. reaching our full potential will make us stronger at this time of history's break.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in the report we are about to vote on, the european parliament is establishing, among other things, the principle of 'zero tolerance' for deaths at work. a crime which in my country, italy, alone sees the death of four workers a day on average. as the five star movement we welcome this report; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's health and safety legislation is applied in a uniform manner. the european strategy of zero deaths at work cannot be limited to a mere declaration of intent, but must include adequate funding, clear and traceable objectives and indicators, and binding mechanisms to ensure the health and safety of all workers. it is absurd that, when the european union monitors the economic and social performance of the member states in the context of the european semester, it does not take into account key indicators such as accidents and deaths at work, with a view to their progressive reduction. health and safety can then no longer be considered an optional matter but an inalienable right of every worker and the cornerstone of a just and civil society.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the world has changed dramatically. we live much faster, we work much faster, and we often work at the edge of our abilities. protecting against health and safety risks and ensuring decent working conditions for the 170 million workers in the union is therefore essential. policies must also reflect the realities of the new digital age. there are new terms. now, with the increased use of the home office in the covid-19 era, we should also reflect that workers in services or certain professions, where they work on computers in their homes, should have the right to disconnect. stress, anxiety, depression or burnout often become our unwilling colleagues, and we see it here in the staff of the european parliament or in you, commissioner, in the european commission. whether it is office or manual work, it is absolutely crucial to ensure standard and satisfactory conditions. i am therefore pleased that the report calls for a revision of the european list of occupational diseases and takes account of mental health disorders. we must also take into account innovative ways of treating them. and let me finish with one thing. the key to success is proper enforcement and implementation, and i therefore call on the european commission to not turn a blind eye to violations of staff conditions and to actively seek to change them.
mr president, if truth be told, we should not die from work. we should not get sick from work. we should not risk burnout because of work. the revision of the european strategy on health and safety at work after the pandemic is totally necessary. in italy, in 2021, one hundred people died at work each month; 10% of these were women. even two young people, giuseppe and lorenzo, aged 16 and 18, recently died during their internship. non-fatal accidents are also increasing, and risks and serious illnesses, stress, psychological disorders and cancer are growing and growing. the covid pandemic has worsened the phenomenon. in a labour market which is changing a lot, old jobs are disappearing and new jobs, often precarious, unpaid, discontinuous, are spreading. we have advanced the legislation in europe on health and safety. now we have to connect the dots with the transformation of the labour market after the pandemic, not to mention the consequences of this cynical and criminal russian aggression of ukraine, with refugees who will have to work in european countries in decent and regular conditions. platform workers, writers, remote workers. no big deal. but are we sure that smart working is really smart when you have to balance kids and work? the right to disconnect has to be part and parcel of remote working. we are now voting for a ‘vision zero’ approach for no more work-related deaths by 2030. this is a huge goal. but, at the end of the day, we have to transform work into what it has to be: good and rewarding. because work gives you dignity. it gives you perspective. it gives you confidence, self-esteem, and it gives you imagination. this is what we are going to fight for forever, we as socialists and democrats, for decent and healthy work everywhere and for everyone.
in writing. in recent months, the european parliament has worked tirelessly to prevent and combat occupational cancers. we are talking about the european plan to fight cancer, or the fourth revision of the cmrd directive, for which i was rapporteur. this report is in addition to our strategy to combat workplace deaths and accidents. the figures are still too high: every year in europe, 3,300 people die, and over 3 million are injured on the job. in italy, more than three deaths per day were recorded in 2021. a slaughter that is often too silent and one that we must take care of. we therefore support the commission's ambitious 'zero fatalities' target, focusing in particular on the prevention of cancer, which is the number one cause of death at work. particular attention should also be paid to cardiovascular problems, as well as to psychological aspects of work, which are on the rise in the post-pandemic period. reducing occupational exposure to dangerous substances, making workplaces safer and cleaner, and ensuring high standards of protection are commitments that we can no longer put off.
in the name of the id group. mr president, commissioner, madam president of the european commission, ladies and gentlemen, transparency for those who hold such a high-level institutional role is not only an administrative and above all ethical duty, but also a guarantee of their good work, which can be so free from illusions, doubts or misunderstandings. if the greatest crisis facing europe and its institutions is the subject of this lack of transparency, then clarity becomes an imperative. perhaps this is precisely the spirit in which the european ombudsman, when asked about access to the text of some of the sms messages which she had allegedly exchanged with the ceo of pfizer, reiterated, and i quote: "access to eu documents is a fundamental right". unfortunately, since the beginning of the covid emergency, there have been several instances where the commission has failed to be transparent, and each time this has happened, its credibility has been undermined. we think of the time when it was discovered by chance, thanks to wikileaks, that the commission was funding the wuhan laboratory and only thanks to a question from our group did the commission itself have to admit that the laboratory was not complying with the required documentation, or when this house strongly asked to be able to see the contracts for the purchase of anti-covid vaccines with the world's leading pharmaceutical companies, except then to see documents that were largely censored being handed over. we are convinced, mr president, of your good faith in dealing with a political and social crisis that has never been seen before and which has required an incredible political effort, but we cannot leave room for doubt, and that is why the question of the so-called sms is not just to know whether they exist or what they contain, but must be the instrument to demonstrate that the commission is above suspicion, so that no one can speculate, misrepresent the lack of transparency or, worse, think citizen, so let it be a house of glass and flooded with light, where there is never room for any shadow.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, allow me to say a few words on this subject. we have moved from a general debate on the transparency of european commission documents to a specific case. i would first like to thank the vice-president for her introduction, where she clearly described the commission's efforts to make certain processes transparent, the introduction and strengthening of the public portal, etc. but the topic eventually slipped to the negotiation of contracts for covid-19 vaccines and possible sms messages between the commission representative and the manufacturers. for me, it's not so much about transparency, but about political culture. i don't think the debate should be about whether or not the text messages of a high-ranking politician negotiating a contract with a manufacturer should be public. i don't think that texting should be allowed, if it was. such contracts should be negotiated transparently in clear documents in official negotiations and not through some sms message. personally, i do not think that any change in the legislation is necessary, the 2001 regulation, even with the definition of exceptions, is sufficient in my opinion. the question is back in the app. i think that if there are exceptions to transparency, then the commission should interpret them restrictively, not extensively. i see maybe a different approach. in addition to the case mentioned here, i am concerned as the representative of the czech republic with the case of our former prime minister, where an audit was conducted that was not just published with reference to these exceptions, although some experts thought that the audit should be published in the public interest. so again, there's this question of the restriction of the interpretation of exceptions. i think that in the case of the czech prime minister there was just an extensive interpretation of the exceptions and that it was simply not used correctly. i say this in general terms, i don't want to mr president, commissioner, it is more than 20 years since the transparency regulation came into force. two decades later, the practice of guaranteeing public access to documents is now established, but this regulation needs to be revised and adapted to today's reality, where communication is done through multiple channels, without necessarily leaving a trace. the european administration should be held accountable for these new media in order to guarantee transparent practices and procedures, which is a key element of democracy. this regulation shall apply without exception to all the bodies of the european union. a new, much broader definition of what a 'document' is will therefore be needed to adapt to the times. this is a matter of governance and trust, especially when taxpayers' money is at stake. and this practice should not be neglected in times of crisis, when the commission signs contracts with pharmaceutical companies where the best interests of citizens must prevail over purely commercial interests.
mr president, we are discussing today a very important topic that concerns a very fundamental general principle of the eu institutions, namely transparency. trust between citizens and the institutions depends on transparency and how it’s practiced. it is central that all institutions function in a transparent manner, according to the regulation that embodies the rules on public access to the documents of the institutions. that ruling is called regulation (ec) 1049/2001. of course, means of communications have changed since then. there is an urgent call to look if that is still unclear, and i think the strong message from the ombudsman, who accused the commission of maladministration, is a need to change the situation. so, important content can be a text message and can be something else. but of course, in real life, there are situations when information cannot be disclosed, but even then, these situations need to be well justified and based on eu law, not wrong predictions.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, when it comes to public money, transparency is a must, and yet we are in 2022 and we have a regulation that is 20 years old, mainly because of the blockade by the european governments in the council, and there are still cases of serious lack of transparency in the eu. i am talking about the turin-lyon high-speed rail project and the 813 million euros of public money to finance it. cinea, the agency in charge, continues to deny access to information on the financing, citing the exception provided for by the public safety regulation, without however giving any valid reasons. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used in a way that is compatible with the common market. i therefore ask the commission to shed light on this unacceptable situation, because the right of access to documents is one of the founding pillars of the democratic structure of our society.

the debate on the commission communication transparency and administrative standards on the processing of public access requests submitted under regulation (ec) no 1049/2001 (2022/0578 ((rsp)) is continuing.
president, ladies and gentlemen, you will remember how hillary clinton got into serious trouble with her email fraud back then. what came out as a conclusion is this: everyone with great responsibilities - certainly political responsibilities with taxpayer money - is obliged to act with care and without secrecy. the european commission is too little aware of the profound impact of the corona crisis on citizens. one of the many effects felt as a scandal is that besides the late and then useless restrictions on freedom the vaccine contracts, once made public, were found to have been rendered illegible by a so-called duty of secrecy. fortunately, thanks to a mistake by a belgian state secretary who tweeted the prices of the vaccines, we now know the price ratios between the vaccines. pfizer, with which the president of the commission had 'created a deep bond of trust', according to a statement by pfizer's bourla, appeared to have the second highest price. the fact that the personal messages between the two, as the commission now claims in its defence, are only 'temporary' is of course not convincing. in your high-flown bubble and in your electric limousines you do not realise the anger of the people who would like nothing more than to see the european commission prove itself ephemeral, of a passing nature.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we are here today for a simple but important reason: citizens deserve to know the truth about the extensive negotiation that appears to have taken place via an exchange of messages between president von der leyen and the ceo of pfizer, regarding the supply of the covid vaccine to the eu. the response of the institutions in the aftermath of the virus has been strong in the fight against it and today we can speak of an almost return to normalcy, despite many mistakes made at all levels. but one thing is certain, emergency cannot justify shady areas or disregard for the rules. it can't even justify doubt. the doubts that the commission's refusal to grant access to the file may be concealing something else. we are therefore here to shed light on what has happened, also following the indications of the european ombudsman, who has decided to open an inquiry. you see, there can be no power without responsibility and there can be no responsibility without power. today we are not talking about an exchange of messages, but about a violation of a principle, namely the transparency of the commission's action. and the commission's reply, that this kind of communication is not relevant, is as weak as it is suspicious. as the ombudsman pointed out, it is the content that counts and not the form used. we therefore ask the commission to answer for its actions: it owes it to us, but it owes it first and foremost to the citizens who have faced this pandemic with sacrifice and their respect for the rules.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, i am sure that president von der leyen is following this debate, but on the other hand, i think i am the right person to address it here today because i am paid to work on the transparency of the commission. so that’s why i am glad to be here and to be allowed to listen to your debate and also to be able to answer, which i want to do now. i agree with ms incir, who said that without transparency there is no accountability. i myself used this sentence so many times when we were discussing and negotiating the transparency register. and, believe me, the commission was the one that was pushing for as strong transparency as possible because, from what i heard, it was the other two institutions that were saying how we should be more careful, not be so open, that we have to be operational, not publish documents during trilogues, and so on. so i don’t agree with sophia in ‘t veld when she says that the commission is not trustworthy enough. i have been working in this institution for many years now, and i can see that there is a high level of support for the maximum possible transparency. so we will work with the parliament and the council to do better, because i agree with those of you who said that there is a lot of room for improvement. i will come to this because this is the debate about one case, and also systemic matters relating to the way in which we communicate and enable access to documents. on the ad-hoc case, the text messages, i have to say that in times of crisis, indeed, when you are an executive body and you have the burden of responsibility on your shoulders, you have to be operational; you sometimes have to engage in personal communication; you have to be fast. as for the burden of responsibility, well, we can discuss it, whether we should have done what we did: we invested in vaccine research; we invested a lot in having common procurement and, as you said, the eu has very little competence in the field of health care. in spite of that, president von der leyen took the risks and she engaged heavily, days and nights, in guaranteeing the early delivery of the vaccines to the european people, and i admire her for this work. i think that in this case, she is the role model, not the other way around. you may not see her as a role model of transparency but i see her as a role model for taking her responsibility seriously and acting quickly and in an efficient way. as for what mr annemans said, that the commission acted in an ephemeral way, well if that had been the case we would not have had the people vaccinated in europe. so i’m sorry but i have to look at this case also in context and the real nature of the situation, and this was at the peak of the covid-19 crisis. but this case is not closed, and i said before that we are preparing an answer to the ombudsman with a deadline of 26 april, and we will give all of the information necessary to reply to the ombudsman’s requests. on the systemic side, so let’s make an agreement to increase the system, or improve the system, of access to documents in all three institutions – in the commission, parliament and council. you know well that we have the legislation from 2001 and you said it here many times that the rules need to be modernised. i agree 100%. when we started in covid times with purely online communication, not face to face, and with a lot of telephone diplomacy, i felt myself that this was a new way of communicating and was something which should be reflected also in how transparent we are. so i agree that we need to reflect in new rules that there are modern channels and modern ways of communication, and that should be our new interpretation of the exemptions which mr pospíšil called for. there should be a new, precise definition of what constitutes a document. i agree with all that. so, my question to you is whether you will unblock the situation around the directives, which were prepared to update the rules from 2001, and whether we will be able to withdraw the legislation which is pending and prepare new rules. we are working on them. and if this will not be possible for all the institutions, we are determined to come with new internal rules for the commission. and as i said many times before, i don’t want to wait because i am really convinced that we have to modernise the rules and reflect also the new technological reality. so this is my call for cooperation with the parliament to push things forward where they should be pushed, in the interest of higher transparency and better access to documents. so i wanted to be concrete in my answer, also to confirm that i am aware that we should do better, and i am ready to cooperate.
the debate is closed.
the commission has also adopted a communication on the need to adopt an eu strategy for sustainable textiles (2022/2539 ((rsp)).
member of the commission. – mr president, the textiles sector has major effects on our climate and our environment and, in fact, textiles consumption is one of the top four supply-chain pressures on resource use, land use, water and greenhouse gas emissions. as a value chain, it places huge pressure on biodiversity. many hazardous chemicals are still used in textile products and a considerable share of the microplastics released into the environment can be traced back to the source. the sector also plays a crucial role to ensure the labour rights of workers in europe and abroad. worst of all, the trends are moving in the wrong direction. we throw away clothes faster than ever before, and we use them for ever shorter periods of time. most of them end up in landfill or incineration. around the world, less than one percent of all textiles are now recycled into new textiles. we need to encourage and support the sector to do better. in the eu, it employs 1.5 million people in more than 160 000 companies, most of which are smes. in 2019, the annual turnover was eur 162 billion. when we talk about textiles, we are talking about everything from the automotive and agriculture sectors to home furnishings, clothing and medical equipment. the largest share of our textiles consumption is fashion. we are a major destination for textiles and garments. in 2019, the eu was the second largest global importer of clothing and, in the first 15 years of this century, world production of textiles more or less doubled. the consumption of clothing, footwear and household textiles happening in europe creates the most impact in other regions of the world, where the majority of production takes place. for textile supply chains, the pandemic brought considerable disruption and many citizens realize that the sector needs radical change. as all other sectors, textiles needs to build back better. we need to help the sector to do that. we need to build in more circularity and sustainability, and that’s vital to address climate change, to help win the fight against pollution and to reduce the pressure on nature and reverse biodiversity loss. we need to do it while preserving and creating growth and decent jobs, respecting human rights. at the end of this month, as announced in the circular economy action plan, the commission will adopt an eu strategy for sustainable and circular textiles. it will be a strategy to strengthen innovation and reduce the environmental footprint, a strategy to boost the market for sustainable and circular textiles, a strategy for increasing textile reuse and repair, boosting circular business models and addressing fast fashion, and a strategy that can also boost the competitiveness of sustainable textiles industries. social aspects, including working conditions, will also be considered. the strategy builds on extensive consultations and input from stakeholders, who supported its development through thematic workshops and online public consultation. at its heart is a new approach to design. we want textiles to be more durable, recyclable and easier to reuse, repair and upgrade. that way, we reduce the consumption of resources, we extend the lifespan of textile products and we boost the recovery of valuable materials and components at the end-of-life stage. it means better material efficiency, a reduced environmental footprint and a longer textile-usage life. acting at the design phase makes it easier to choose materials free of hazardous substances and made of recycled fibres. it also means that microplastics can be designed out of the process. for all these reasons, textiles will also be a key value chain in the upcoming sustainable product initiative. as promised in the european green deal, we are acting to ensure that sustainable products become the norm. it is part of our drive for a climate-neutral europe by 2050, but more sustainable and circular design alone will not be enough. we need to reduce consumption altogether, in particular in the fashion sector. that’s why we find it especially important to boost the uptake of new business models. these include, for example, more circular models like reuse, resale, repair on demand, manufacturing and product as service. in our sustainable product initiative, we will propose another powerful tool, which will be useful for textiles, the digital product passport, which will improve access to information about product characteristics. that way, all economic actors can make decisions that are better informed. it will enable better transparency and traceability for the value chain, which is particularly important for textiles. action on the supply chain needs to be coupled with initiatives to empower consumers, and that’s why the commission is also preparing a legislative proposal on green claims in order to fight greenwashing and to ensure that claims are based on reliable, verifiable and comparable information. we aim to present that later this year. we also need to take into account the international dimension. what we do internally also has an impact on operators in partner countries, and that’s why our strategy ambition is, together with our partner countries, to tie sustainable and circular textile value chains globally. also important for the textile value chain is the legislative proposal on corporate sustainability due diligence recently adopted by the commission. it comes with a corporate sustainability due diligence obligation to prevent and mitigate actual adverse impacts for sustainability. when it comes to achieving our social, environmental and climate goals, the textile sector plays a pivotal role. but to play that pivotal role, the sector needs to change. the strategy we are presenting will help drive that change. it means new ways of doing business, different patterns of production and consumption, products that are durable and repairable, and citizens who care, refashioning and sharing the textiles they wear. it’s time to redefine the textile economy in europe and with our partner countries. we are counting on the support of the european parliament and we look forward to your views on the strategy we propose. with citizens, youth and stakeholders working together, it’s time to rethink the structure of textiles. it’s time to refashion fashion.
on behalf of the ppe group. – madam president, commissioner, to be honest, i don’t believe a word. we have been announcing that for years. it had been this parliament strengthening the research programme, doubling the challenge for the creative industries, for innovation. we are facing a transitional phase where clothing is not just about sustainability and new textiles. we are going to a world where fibres will change the consistence of textiles. it will be related to health, to information, to the digital world, to a lot of other things. but we hear announcements. we hear announcements. we hear announcements. there’s still no transition pathway which had been announced by the commission as part of the 14 ecosystems, where you described the textile industry as one of the 14. there is no transition pathway. there is no strategy. there are just an announcements. and yet another referred that what we share to sustainability. but it is just another administrative nightmare you’re describing. this industry is hit. we have been losing one million jobs before the pandemic, another 500 million within the pandemic, and the commission is waiting. for two and half years you could have done calls in the research programme. we have eur 2.3 billion for the creative industry. we have a new powerhouse and you are announcing, and you’re announcing, and you’re announcing. and this sector, yes, has to change. and this sector is up for potential for the future. that’s one of the european usps. that is what europe can do where fashion is leading, where a young generation is interested, but instead, announcement, and another administrative nightmare. so go to action, not announce, you would have had two and a half years, tons of money in the research programme to start innovation products. not a single one.
i am speaking on behalf of the renew group. the commission has already made a number of proposals. textiles are one of the biggest drivers of our raw material consumption. the global textile industry is the fifth largest emitter of co2, about on par with the automotive industry. the commission has also proposed that the european union should be able to provide the union with a financial contribution to the european union's climate action programme. however, the announcements so far have mainly focused on reuse, recycling and repairability, commissioner. the logic is clear. reducing resource consumption is a major lever for more efficient use. but the second major lever has been largely ignored - the promotion of sustainable materials. the textiles used have a major negative impact on land use and the environment. cotton, which accounts for 43% of all textiles, requires a lot of land and water, fertilisers and pesticides. the production takes place mainly in countries with significantly lower sustainability standards than in the eu. polyester is extracted from fossil raw materials and is a source of microplastics that end up in waterways. i would like to turn your attention today, commissioner, to the forests. they have the potential to make a substantial contribution to sustainable textiles. textiles containing cellulose derived from wood currently account for less than 10%. the european forest institute estimated that an increase of this proportion to about 50% is realistic. the three to five times lower carbon footprint compared to cotton and polyester means that emissions can be saved on the order of more than 400 million tonnes. this example calculation shows that the strategy is short-sighted if it does not also focus more on raw materials for textiles. i very much hope that the commission will not ignore this potential.
on behalf of the ecr group. – madam president, i fully agree with the position of the commissioner. there are unluckily two major problems regarding the textile sector. they are very technical. number one is the colouring of the textile, which is on one side strongly regulated by the workers’ safety regulation, the environmental emissions regulation and so on, but on the other side is still a problem. we need funds and we need research centres so that we can change 2 000 years of history. the other problem is the release of micro- and nanoplastics. and for this, we do not have a technical solution to filter and take out this nanoplastic from the water that goes into the sea and then gets eaten by the fish and then we eat them. now, of course, i would like to see on these funds to find a solution about this. reduction of artificial textiles is a solution but it is not the only solution. i would like to see a full economic, social and technical assessment of a more stringent regulation on the textile sector, because otherwise we are going to kill a very important economic thing. on the positive side, we do have all the social media platforms that are trading in used clothing which are reducing the number of clothes going to the landfills. last but not least, i really do not understand the politically correct approach in opposition to natural fibres and to natural material like leather and fur. they are natural, they last longer than the artificial stuff, they do not contain microplastic. so are we defending the environment or the animals?
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, thomas jefferson said that 'where the press is free and everyone can read, there is no danger'. the sunny side of our democracies is freedom of the press. because a free press can be good or bad, but without freedom the press can only be bad. that is why what has been happening in mexico for years is very worrying. official figures provided by the mexican government speak of 43 journalists killed from december 2018 to july 2021, and this unacceptable trend continues into 2022, with at least six journalists already murdered. two years ago today i was in mexico on an official mission of parliament. it is a country with great potential for growth, it is the 15th largest economy in the world and it is becoming over the years not a nation of immigrants to america but a destination for immigrants from south america. the security and crime problem in the country is one of the main problems that hamper greater development opportunities. mexico recorded 33,308 murders in 2021, and the official figure may be lower than the real figure. a scary report, which underlines the inability of the mexican authorities to cope with the emergency, is based on impunity. usually, the people accused of murder are just pawns, the people who did it go free and unpunished. the doubt is that all this is also attributable to the policies of the socialist mexican president obrador, who has made little impact in fighting criminals. it also prefers to invest in socialist policies, which block the development of the country, rather than professionals. we must act now so that no journalist has to die and the fundamental right to information can be guaranteed again in mexico.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, those of us who are lucky enough to have been born in the third millennium have long believed that we live in peace and prosperity. today, tragedy is in ukraine, but only two years ago, tragedy struck just a few hundred kilometres to the south, in a small strip of land between armenia and azerbaijan, in a region called artsakh, where thousands of innocent people, soldiers and civilians, lost their lives in a matter of weeks, and where a priceless cultural heritage is in danger. on this occasion, as in 1915, and as in 1938, we are facing a turning point in history: remain indifferent, passive spectators, or open our eyes to the systematic destruction of one of the world's oldest and most fascinating cultures. history, as we know, is made by peoples. but it's always the winners who write it. and when they do, the m.o. is ruthless and elaborate: erasing memory, denying history, creating a parallel narrative and finally propaganda. the duty of the institutions, and therefore our duty, is even greater where it is necessary to protect the memory and support those who, for fear of retaliation, have not even been able to express their dissent against russia's bombing of ukraine. we've let them down before, and now they don't want to expose themselves. respect for the history of the vanquished, laying the foundations for building a future of respect and civilized coexistence, acknowledging the atrocities of genocide. this is europe. for this reason it is our duty to commit ourselves and to commit unesco and all the parties concerned to carry out, census and protect the history and cultural heritage of artsakh. the destruction of the historical heritage of a people violates the principles of democratic europe, and it sets the clock back to 1915 and the human being repeats the same mistake for the umpteenth time, yet again.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, this bloody and unfortunate war cannot be paid for by the citizens of europe. we are too dependent on imported hydrocarbons and raw materials and we must work to ensure that european agriculture is soon self-sufficient and sustainable, while remaining safe for consumers. we encourage domestic production, especially of wheat, corn and sunflowers. we give resources that make it affordable to grow and process the product here. we must stop the bad habit of importing grain at a lower price than the price we pay for it, and we cannot always be sure of its quality. we must stop using glyphosate to lower the price of our own grain in a perverse market game where farmers lose and only multinationals gain. we are restoring those who have suffered losses, we are helping those who want to produce in a virtuous way, making ourselves independent of russian and ukrainian raw materials, but without even thinking of using war to betray the work done so far on organic, the limitation of pesticides, sustainability and the defence of biodiversity. today we are faced with a great responsibility, we have in our hands the future of millions of european citizens and their children. we can't turn back or turn around.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, since the beginning of the war there have been four million ukrainian refugees in the european union, including over one million children, of whom we diplomas and credits acquired in european universities will thus be facilitated and will thus be a key element for young ukrainians, also in their future participation in the reconstruction and rebirth of their country. the greatness of our european political project has always been to put before everyone the need to unite the peoples and to build together widespread prosperity and peace.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, there are almost 3 million children in urgent need and only 47 000 have been reached by educational and recreational material and psychosocial support. still too few. among those in need of immediate support are students who have been directly or indirectly affected by the war. the major challenges at the moment are for states to integrate all students rapidly into their education and training systems and to provide specialised educational support for those with specific needs. we must prevent this war from having further long-term effects, such as school dropout or segregation in schools, which lead to social exclusion and discrimination. in this sense, the absence of representatives of the national states here today is a bad sign. it is essential that digital education tools are used to the full so that all students can continue their education. however, these are and must remain measures complementary to the teaching in the presence of the pupils; in the context of the european year of the environment, the commission has launched a new initiative entitled 'a european year of the environment'. it's a way to give them moments of normal life, things like school, socializing, sports, affection. member states will have to make a further effort by recognising diplomas, qualifications and periods of learning not only for students but also for educators. another element that we cannot forget is that all students fleeing from ukraine, including international students, can complete their diplomas because they too are in an emergency situation. i would like to conclude by reminding you that it is crucial that we take a coordinated approach to this emergency, but even more important is that we learn from this terrible war to provide immediate support for refugee children, of any origin, fleeing from any war. europe must change course and invest in an open system of reception, capable of receiving those fleeing war and violence, starting with children. and those who violate the rule of law and human rights remain adversaries of the european union, both within and outside our borders.
in writing. the atrocities that are taking place in ukraine are claiming many victims. sadly, many of these are children, with an estimated death toll of over 130. many children have also been forced to leave their homes, often unaccompanied by their families or mothers. we must give the utmost importance to the protection of these innocent lives, so that they do not end up as prisoners of criminal organizations involved in human trafficking, one of the most real dangers for unaccompanied minors. the creation of ad hoc humanitarian corridors must be a priority. in addition, great attention should be paid to children in ukrainian orphanages. in fact, even before the war, ukraine was one of the countries that couples were turning to when they wanted to adopt. estimates suggest that at least 100,000 children are being cared for in 663 orphanages. we must ensure these children are protected immediately: it is therefore important to facilitate the process of intercountry adoption in order to remove hundreds of children from a situation of insecurity. protecting children from the horrors of war and ensuring a future of peace, hope and happiness is one of the most important challenges we face.

in the name of the id group. madam president, commissioner, madam rapporteur, ladies and gentlemen, on 10 december 2021, a political agreement was reached on the data governance act, for which i was the shadow rapporteur. the data economy is estimated to be worth about 900 billion euros in the next five years, at least we read that, and it also concerns the effectiveness of scientific research, and that is very important. the covid-19 pandemic has accelerated the use of digital and, with it, the production of data that can be considered a mine of information that should not be wasted, but must be protected and used in noble ways as well. today, much data remains unused for fear of being illegally exchanged. the need to create secure national and european structures where businesses and citizens can provide and store data, and make some of it available in a reworked form under certain conditions, has long been felt. this agreement encourages the wider use and reuse of data held by public bodies. this shall be achieved by using secure processing environments and anonymization techniques, such as differential privacy and the creation of synthetic data. we are aware of the importance of the future european digital sovereignty in the context of global competition balances and, as the league, we will monitor the effectiveness of these rules so that the privacy rights of citizens are guaranteed, but also the protection of industrial and business secrets. in addition, data altruism should be encouraged: those who want wider access to data and in particular for scientific research -
