madam president, ladies and gentlemen, today we remember sir plumb, knowing that organic is the only way to protect the environment, food safety and greater independence from non-eu producers, and to prevent the damage caused by unhealthy products to the health of citizens. we must therefore be ambitious, demanding targets such as a minimum of 25% organic production and a genuine ban on harmful pesticides. italy is already at 20% of organic, sicily at 22%. so it is possible. so we reward the producers, we give more money to farmers in sicily, sardinia, italy or any other region in europe who are really virtuous, who are really organic. stop helping people who don't deserve it. after years of pandemics and war, we are experiencing firsthand the cost of being dependent on other producing countries, from which we often import poor quality products that lower the price of our excellences. it is a broken system and it is up to us to change it to make it work for a happy future for us and our children. it takes courage and free hands. do we have them, ladies and gentlemen?
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner wojciechowski, the organic action plan can be an important instrument for reconciling environmental objectives and european agricultural sovereignty, which are more important than ever today. but to set a target of 25 percent of organically farmed land by 2030, when some states are not even getting to 5 percent, means two things: either it is an excuse to create a biological watering hole that will fuel environmental distortion and dumping, or it is proposing something that is doomed to fail from the start. in my country, italy, millions of farmers have already invested considerable resources in organic production and they certainly do not want to compete with new organic products, the result of a soft or trivial european policy. the market for organic products must certainly be extended, but without putting it in competition with products with designations of origin, which are the excellence that must be protected and not abolished. there are no shortcuts, therefore, such as the exclusive distribution of organic products in public canteens, or the transfer of european funds intended instead for the promotion of agri-food products, or the total absence of controls on organic products coming from third countries. so we want a real organic, environmentally friendly system which pays farmers a fair wage and a fair profit.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, i thank you all for your work. this work has touched on issues of great impact on the lives of citizens, from transportation to healthcare to agriculture to the use of artificial intelligence in the military sector. the aim was to analyse the various perspectives in order to provide the basis for future union legislation in this area. i do not know whether we have succeeded, given the high rate of innovation in this sector, we are faced with many opportunities, but also with many risks. we see so many improvements in the well-being and lives of citizens, but also enormous risks to personal freedoms and the risk of error, with catastrophic social consequences: i'm thinking about that real estate platform that, because of an algorithm error, bought homes at a price above market value, suffered financial losses and was forced to lay off 25 percent of its employees. during the discussions our group tabled amendments aimed at minimising these risks, which unfortunately were not shared by all. it is vital for our citizens that there be as little risk as possible. in the medical field, we have to be very careful because here too we have algorithms that only target white patients and artificial intelligence must be free of any kind of bias. and especially in the military, we think it's critical that ai doesn't take over human decision-making. it is essential that freedom of speech and expression is not put at risk by systems that use artificial intelligence, forcing us to use so-called "algospeak", a code language to bypass algorithms and online censorship. we are not opposed to progress, we are not thinking of a luddite uprising. we don't want to destroy machines, we simply want a future of this technology that is driven by ethics and a desire to protect our citizens.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, may god bless you, your family and hungary. this is what pope francis said to viktor orbán at the end of their meeting, after presenting him with a bronze medallion depicting saint martin, the patron saint of the poor. this beautiful and meaningful meeting was a real blow to the european left, which for years has been trying to attack hungary and poland by the most subtle means, simply because these countries have centre-right governments. the announcement of the start of the rule of law infringement procedure comes just after viktor orbán's victory in the recent elections. this harassment of fully legitimate governments is absurd and harmful at this time. i would remind you that in these tragic days poland has taken in three million refugees and hungary half a million. that is why europe should show its solidarity and not threaten useless sanctions. we have heard many legitimate concerns about the rule of law in these two member countries, but we are underestimating a much more serious danger: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the principle of subsidiarity on which the union is founded is respected. does the polish judicial reform violate the spirit of the treaties, undermining the principle of the separation of powers? that's the question we've been asking ourselves for the last few months and years, but i would like to invite everyone to think about something else: how far does the european union have the power to interfere in the reforms of the judiciary of a member state? it is also quite ridiculous that members of parliament from certain countries where the judges are almost entirely on the left should dare to judge poland or hungary. the same goes for the media, certainly in hungary and poland there are more pro-government media than anti-government media, but is it different in france or italy? i'm not going to. the gay rights discourse is also full of fake news. it is not true that civil unions are not recognized in hungary. they are, but they are not in many other countries of the european union which, strangely enough, are not in the sights of brussels, obviously for other reasons, perhaps political ones. if there is one distinctive feature of our european civilization which has enabled the affirmation of human rights, prosperity and development which have been unparalleled for centuries, it is thanks to pluralism. the peaceful and creative competition between nations with different languages, traditions and even legal systems has made europe great. where centralism is sought, however, division and conflict are created. prime minister orbán was elected for the fourth time in a completely fair election. those who deny this and claim that orbán is an unshakable dictator at the head of a para-fascist party should explain why orbán has long been a valued leader within the epp. in the lead european media, a letter from a famous british opinion writer was published immediately after orbán's victory, explaining how it was necessary to cut all european funds to hungary because the hungarian government uses the funds to help families and thus gains popularity. this is crazy. a government that uses funds to help families instead of being praised is criticized to the point of demanding punishment. the european union must stop fighting the legitimate governments of the member states politically and instead focus on how to defend our europe from the enemies that threaten it, those who have always been at our doorstep ready to attack and whom you have always pretended not to see until they entered our house.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, we have reached the end of this experiment. i don't know what else to call it. an experiment that had the ambition to bring the institutions to the citizens, but it has done nothing but bring the citizens, or rather a remarkable minority of citizens, who we do not know how well chosen, into the vortex of the institutions. an experiment which from the outset gave the impression of not looking so much at the result, but at doing, a compulsive, disorganized doing and above all without clear goals and objectives. the result, of course, could not have been anything other than what is now before everyone's eyes. the mountain gave birth to a mouse. a little mouse made up of recommendations, which are largely acceptable, but which are also largely the subject of political discussions and have therefore not brought any major changes. a mouse who also made straightforward recommendations such as those on the transnational lists. if we think that the european citizens consider them a priority, perhaps it would be better to step out of the comfort zone of the palace for a moment and go and talk to real people who have other problems and other concerns at the moment. let's face it, it was an experiment that completely missed its purpose, unless its purpose was to give a semblance of popular legitimacy to positions that are very deeply rooted within the palace. but only here. of course, we will never know exactly how much this experiment cost, but we hope that the lesson is that politics can regain its rightful primacy in decision-making without shame, while actively and concretely involving all citizens, including and especially the most critical, who had no voice in this initiative, all the more reason to consider this experiment a failure. that's too bad. we all believed it, but we have, you have missed another opportunity to really bring the european citizens closer to these institutions.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, vice-president schinas, the issue of the european union's preparedness against cyberattacks following the russian invasion of ukraine is mainly influenced by the different level and degree of preparedness of the member states. strong member states make a strong union, but this is true in all fields, in all sectors and in all domains. the current italian government has also increased its capacity, starting the national cyber security agency, unifying all activities to protect against cyber threats, thus contributing to the security of the european union. cybersecurity ensures national defence, the defence of the european union, socioeconomic stability, the democratic order of states, the cohesion of society, the fact that citizens can rely on reliable information and remain psychologically intact, that is, they do not lose confidence in the leadership of their countries. moreover, european citizens must have access to vital services and be protected from espionage and disinformation. in the hybrid war that is consuming ukraine we have evidence of the deadly attacks of the russian federation. it is our duty to help the ukrainian people and organizations to protect themselves. it is our duty to extend this protective and capacity-building umbrella to all those who are related to us and to continue deepening the nato exercises. that is why i am calling for an end to the disinformation which is plaguing the european union, which we have perhaps all too often forgotten.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the actions and preparations for the russian cyberattacks began a year before the invasion of ukraine, confirming that the conflict had been planned well in advance. it is now clear that there is a synergistic activity of russian cyber warfare activities, designed to destabilize some sensitive and strategic infrastructure of a territory that russia intended to hit. expansion of cyber attacks to the west, outside the ukrainian borders, is one of the possible scenarios for the development of the conflict, and knowing that these actions have been prepared by moscow again in advance, it is obviously necessary not to be unprepared. it is not appropriate to wait for further tensions with russia to develop a plan to respond and contain these attacks. instead, we must immediately identify a strategy to maximize our defenses. we also need to have a vision of perspective, a vision that fights cyber warfare, that will not end with the end of the conflict in ukraine, but will probably be exacerbated. we need greater cooperation not only between the european states of the west as a whole, of course, but also a strengthening of cooperation with our historic allies, the united states, but also the united kingdom and international organizations such as nato. only with the unity of the west can we face these hybrid threats and defend ourselves and above all defend our sensitive and strategic infrastructure, because when we talk about cybersecurity we are not just talking about cybersecurity in the strict sense, but about real national security.
mr president, commissioner, i have to ask you to stop this. ladies and gentlemen! russia has been waging a war in cyberspace against the european union for many years. the aggression against ukraine was preceded by hacking attacks on public institutions, public officials and critical infrastructure in many member states, including poland. building resilience to the conflict that is happening online is a fundamental issue for our security. the potential of advanced technologies, including artificial intelligence, can be used to this end. we have european companies that can provide the right cybersecurity tools in both military and civilian contexts. but we have to create the right conditions for them to thrive. the ai act, which we're working on, is a great opportunity to do that and to make it easier to do research and invest in cybersecurity measures.
a uachtaráin, cyber-attacks from russia did not start on 24 february, nor will they end when the war ends in ukraine. europe must be prepared for the war to expand online, with critical infrastructure at risk. as technology advances and becomes further intertwined into our economies and infrastructure, we must be aware that cyber-threats are also rapidly evolving in nature and sophistication. the eu and all member states need a digital security architecture to defend us robustly against cyber-attacks. ireland is a neutral country. yet in may 2021, our health-care systems suffered the biggest cyber-attack in the history of the state. this affected most of the country’s health services, including coronavirus testing, maternal care services, cancer care, covid-19 tracking and routine referrals for secondary care at a time when the whole system was under pressure. this, it appears, was at the hands of russian hackers and criminals. this has led to a shift in thinking in ireland, as we’ve seen that neutrality per se doesn’t mean that you’re exempt from russian attacks. we have to take our defence much more seriously, within the confines of neutrality. cyber-defence should be a priority, which means investment. that is what we must do.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in this unprecedented, complex and difficult time, we have learned that there are many things that we can classify as war, from the missiles that are hitting leopoli in these minutes, and i would like from this house to express solidarity with the mayor, with the people who are being hit precisely in these minutes, as we are discussing this. to the old propaganda tools, to the disinformation, to the muddy boots of infantry invasions, which we have rediscovered to exist in these months, to the threats we are discussing tonight that run along the fibres of the world, the so-called hybrid war. we have come to know it in recent months, and despite the fact that the nature of this conflict is complex and complex, the responses of our institutions must be united and strong, from the increasingly structural alliance between the member states to the new instruments of defence against the unjustified and brutal aggression of putin. and on that we shall find ourselves once more ready.
mr president, russia’s military and hybrid aggression against ukraine only confirms the global nature and importance of cybersecurity. russia’s direct and indirect attacks against ukraine have also increased the impact of the ongoing ‘cold hybrid war’ on the west. deterring cyber threats emanating from russia and its allies, such as belarus – don’t forget that – requires immediate attention to strengthening the eu’s cyber resilience. we need to reassess existing threats and take the necessary decisions to protect our critical infrastructure and, more importantly, our people. we need to understand that cyber threats are here to stay and can have serious consequences if not properly addressed. we must pay particular attention to protecting our information space and our people from russian trolls and attempts to interfere in our democratic processes by manipulating public opinion. with russia waging a brutal war against democracy and freedom in ukraine, we no longer have the luxury of talking about action. now it’s time to act and ensure the eu’s and our partners’ cybersecurity.
mr president, we are all aware that wars are not limited to traditional types of warfare. the russian invasion on ukraine is just an evident example. the kremlin started testing its tactics in ukraine many years ago, targeting even a power grid. disinformation examples are well—known, and particularly russia disseminated 70% of all disinformation and fake news lately. russian state—sponsored hackers and cybercriminals are still increasing their presence. they are very keen to target critical infrastructure. those are basically the same actors, with the same intentions, that launched cyberattacks during the covid pandemic, on hospitals or our medicines agency. that’s why it is of utmost importance to be prepared. these attacks may have unimaginable repercussions and our unity is a prerequisite for safety. therefore, eu cybersecurity needs to be shown in practice. we absolutely have to support our agency for cybersecurity to strengthen the capacities of our member states. with enough efforts and enough resources. otherwise, we risk too much.
mr. president, in 2022, with war at the border, we're wondering if we're prepared for cyberattacks. and while the pandemic has shown us exactly how vulnerable we are, it has cost us a lot that we didn't already have a health union, that we didn't digitize enough, that we didn't digitize fully. digitalization means opening up the state to people, it means transparency, it means speed, it means bureaucratization, it means less corruption, if we digitize properly, not just to check some indicators. after two years of online, national and european institutions are still asking for a rail file. are we prepared for russian attacks? certainly not. but why? we talk about it a dozen times before we make a decision. we've been sitting on the drawer for years with proposals. we negotiate for the interests of a party or a group, not for all europeans. we listen to the office types, not the professionals in the field. that's why we're not ready. we won't be until we change something, the way we work, and the citizens will tell us to vote until we understand.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, mr putin's terrible war carries with it a burden of violence and death that is before our eyes every day. but we are facing - and it is good to be fully aware of this - a multidimensional war that is also being played out in less visible fields, thus also taking the form of a computer, hybrid, cyber war. in this area, we have indeed seen foreign attempts to influence the democratic processes of the european union for several years. and we need to open our eyes more. a huge investment is needed to rapidly build up strategic capacity and expertise in this area, with a particular focus on critical infrastructure. this must be an integral part of a common security and defence project and a counter-offensive - as mr schieder rightly said - to support free voices in russia. we must also support those in russia who oppose putin's terrible war.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, first the pandemic, now the war in ukraine, the transport and tourism sectors are once again in a state of severe distress and with the increase in energy costs reflected in the increase in ticket prices, even travelling is becoming impossible for many families. the galloping inflation in transport and tourism risks creating new forms of poverty and loneliness which we must prevent. we therefore reiterate our request to the european commission to set up an ad hoc tourism fund to support tourism businesses which employ millions of people across europe. i would also like to make another appeal: the right sanctions that hit belarus for its involvement in the war in ukraine had a boomerang effect. thousands of belarusian children in rehabilitation cannot reach italy and other european countries. we must intervene by allowing humanitarian flights and by protecting all children who wish to be reunited with their families in europe. let's open our eyes to the rubble that this war is causing.
mr. chairman, the war in ukraine, triggered by russian aggression, has created a state of emergency in all sectors. transport and tourism are certainly such sectors. fuel prices are at record highs, there is already a shortage of raw materials, and all of this is affecting transport links and transport relations. in this sense, the situation in ukraine today is particularly worrying, with its sea and river ports blocked and this hindering the country's exports and trade. however, some colleagues in this chamber are doing everything they can to disrupt the transport industry by continually introducing new and new regulations and restrictions, as they did with the 'mobility' package. i want to draw attention to another serious problem. it is clear that both the russian and the ukrainian markets will remain closed for the foreseeable future. i therefore call on the european commission to take steps to ensure not only the supply of gas at european level, but also the supply of raw materials to all sectors of the european union. particular attention should be paid to the industrial production sector, the provision of sufficient cheap steel for the construction sector and, of course, access to cheap fuels. in conclusion, we must make sure that this mad war ends an hour sooner and ensure maximum stability to these sectors of our societies.
mr president, commissioner, colleagues, i think something has to change. if we want more people to join in watching debates from the european parliament at home and if we want more people to believe in and believe in the european union, we must remember that in times of crisis, we will - and i quote - we will express concern, we will watch with alarm and we will condemn sharply. what we need to do in a crisis is to be closer to people than at any other time. let me give you an example. for 70 days now, the ports of ukraine have been blocked, dozens of ships cannot leave these ports. among them is the bulgarian "rosen", which is blocked at the port in chernomyrsk. some of the sailors have been evacuated, others are on board the ships and surviving with what's left in the holds. as more time passes, more mines are being dumped in the waters around the harbor, making it almost impossible to get the ships out. and if you ask me what we should do, i'll tell you. immediately, a delegation from the european institutions with an action plan in hand left for ukraine and returned on the last ship flying the european flag there. and then, ladies and gentlemen, from the television sets, the european union will move into the hearts of the people.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the russian invasion of ukraine has devastated the lives of millions of people who deserve our support and our deep solidarity, but the consequences of this war are also being felt in europe, particularly in sectors such as transport and tourism, which have already been significantly affected by two years of the pandemic. we are approaching the summer season with an indiscriminate increase in fuel prices, high energy prices and inflation reducing the economic availability of european citizens. travel and vacationing will be difficult for many families again this year. it will therefore be important to adopt the instruments highlighted in this resolution, starting with the european crisis management mechanism and short-term financial support. europe must be there for its citizens, for the micro and small enterprises that populate the tourist sector and for those territories which, because of their proximity to the war theatre or because of the historical predominance of russian and ukrainian tourists, will be most affected.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in addition to providing concrete assistance to ukraine in defending itself against the aggressor, we must take clear measures to protect the european economy from the adverse effects of the war. we have already started in energy and some other sectors, but tourism as a sector is also directly affected, as tourism activities are not essential for life, and the decline in consumption is most rapidly felt in the tourism sector in the crisis. i come from croatia, a country where tourism is an extremely important part of the economy and has a significant share of gdp. i think it is important that, in addition to national governments, the european commission actively encourages citizens to holiday in the member states of the union and helps to provide additional transport routes for tourists from the rest of the world to come to europe. the world is slowly entering recession, war is adding to the uncertainty, and we cannot allow the tourism sector to deal with this alone.
mr president, commissioner, the war in ukraine is having a devastating effect on our economy, including on the transport and tourism sectors. the european union will not be the same after this war, whose consequences add to the ravages of the pandemic. we see supply shortages, disruptions in the logistics sector and high energy prices. the automobile industry, for example, is suffering from a shortage of electronic components and many of these companies have stopped their activity. europe needs a competitive industry and not just a service provider. the transport sector is essential to this. we must look back and find ways of maintaining european competitiveness. we must embrace digitalisation to optimise the logistics chain in air, sea and land transport. we must be aware of the situation, of the challenges we face and of the threats that exist, and rethink how we want to deal with them in order to maintain a europe that is peaceful, prosperous and competitive. to maintain the logistics chain, the transport sector is key. tourism is also about protecting and promoting our values and our way of life.
mr president, among the devastating effects of russia’s criminal aggression against ukraine, there are those on tourism – which accounts for 10% of gdp in europe – a sector which has already suffered a lot during the pandemic. we are facing naturally a loss of russian and ukrainian tourists all over europe, and the costs of energy, foodstuff and fuel are growing fast. an explosive mix. we ask for financial assistance for big and small touristic companies within the internal market programme, together with a full crisis management action plan. putin has invaded a peaceful country in mainland europe. this is crystal clear. we have to do our part and support the ukrainian people in the right to choose their own destiny. why should a country surrender to the arrogance of a despotic leader? why should a country submit to the invaders’ will? putin will respond for his crimes in front of the history, but for now, we have to work for peace. and we have to relieve the most damaging humanitarian, economic and social effects of war, united as ever.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, thank you for being here, commissioner, the consequences, if there are any, for the social and productive fabric of the countries of the european union, but of course there is also the positive element of the fact that we are all united in supporting the sanctions, including the new package, which is a just instrument and is bringing results, of course it is also bringing consequences and costs to our system, to our business model, but we must go on because it is one of the most important strategies. the unity we have shown here is a value, but it should not prevent us from also analysing the situation that is being created, an analysis also of the mistakes we have made in the past, of why we have come to this dependence on russia, of why until a few weeks before the outbreak of the war, someone in this house continued to defend projects such as nord stream 2 and eastmed, but nothing was said. project eastmed that we may be able to relaunch soon, but it's going to take a long time to become active. we have some elements that have come up in the last few weeks: the problem of storing energy resources for the coming winter; madam president, ladies and gentlemen, by examining the discharges of the european institutions we ensure that public money is spent properly. i have been fighting a battle since the beginning of this parliament's term over the housing policy of parliament and the commission, which frankly seems to be out of date and out of step with the times. but i would like to point out one thing: between covid and the russian-ukrainian conflict, europeans are experiencing the biggest economic and social crisis since the post-war period, with energy prices skyrocketing and the cost of living doubling. the european commission has given us advice on this, urging us to reduce heating and travel at reduced speed on motorways and to prefer public transport to our cars. anything that's legit is gonna be missed. but this is in conflict with the reality that we are all here in strasbourg again, members and staff who have come here by plane, by car from all over europe. the usual caravan that moves in droves to strasbourg every month, while european citizens are asked to make efforts to reduce their dependence on russian gas. the relocation of plenary sessions to brussels during covid has shown that with a single venue it is possible to reduce pollution and costs.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank the chairman and all the members of the committee on budgetary control for the work that has been done. the verification of the conditions for deciding whether or not to grant discharge is a demanding task and not just a check of manners. the debates within the cont committee have highlighted the criticisms, problems and errors, but also the good practices that are being adopted in the european agencies. this complex work is of the utmost importance for the transparency of our institutions and is therefore necessary to increase the confidence of the citizens. it is therefore important to know the final beneficiaries of european funds in order to carry out all the checks. only by highlighting what is not working and indicating what action needs to be taken can action be taken to improve and prevent further problems. it is not by hiding the dust under the carpet that we provide a better picture of the european institutions, but by highlighting the criticisms and working to solve them.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the pegasus spyware incident is first and foremost a matter of credibility for europe, for its values and its rights, the ones we want to defend, and therefore the rights and values of european democracy and also of its governments. let's face it, the people who should have been looking after the privacy and data integrity of their citizens clearly didn't. committees of inquiry have also been set up by some national governments, but they have not come to any conclusions. and let's face it, it's even worse than our colleagues in here, and so their families have been treated the same way. this is an attack on european democracy. the pegasus spyware can't be hijacked by accident on the way. for all these reasons, i sincerely wish the european parliament's pegasus committee of inquiry a very successful outcome. we do not have democracy and we will not have it without respect for our values, which have unfortunately been violated.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, technology today enables us to do wonderful things. the development of artificial intelligence then led mankind to unprecedented discoveries. sharing is now a core concept of any experience, real or virtual. unfortunately, sometimes technology allows for silent sharing by gathering information. pegasus is an algorithm developed in israel that can collect information from mps, journalists or citizens from any device connected to the internet. many computer scientists have called pegasus a "jewel of computing" with unprecedented discretionary autonomy. photos, e-mails, everything on the phone is targets that pegasus uses for social, military and political purposes. pegasus, like any other more advanced spyware, is a serious threat to the rule of law. as the spanish colleagues have said, when a government is threatened in its constitutional integrity, it has the right to defend itself against these threats. spying is what we were taught in school not to do, yet knowing in advance of a potential threat is the best survival strategy possible. pegasus could become the vile deformation of a technology useful to a few and living off our daily lives. i must say that this excess has provoked, albeit in a different way, the social realities of the world. i am not sure that this is the case. the work of the pega committee will be very complicated. the freedoms of 500 million europeans, their governments and the challenges of the future between search algorithms and ultra-artificial intelligence are on the table. but now it is time to ask ourselves the essential questions: how much is our freedom worth?
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we are here for the umpteenth time to discuss the human rights violations in turkey. free voices, as in the case of osman kavala, are silenced. in the country of sultan erdogan we see the systematic imprisonment of those who defend freedom and those who dare to criticize the government. turkey is persecuting christians and other religious minorities and is constantly violating its international obligations as a member of the council of europe. in recent times women's rights have also seen a worrying tightening, with associations dissolved because they are contrary to religious morality. turkey must make concrete progress on respect for the rule of law, democracy and fundamental rights and it must do so immediately. we are here for the umpteenth time to say that turkey is not europe. turkey cannot be part of the european union and all accession negotiations must be stopped. we call on the commission and the council to put an end to all the funding to the turkish government which over the years, especially with the migration pact, has been seen in all its effects as a shameful blackmail against us.
, on behalf of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, in communist china there are numerous reports of the horrendous practice of organ harvesting from prisoners belonging to religious groups, political opponents, ethnic and linguistic minorities. this entrenched system has been a reality for decades now, it is a crime against humanity, but unfortunately the chinese regime is not interested in respecting human rights. the government holds as many as two million uighurs and other minority groups in a system of internment camps where detainees are forced to work, tortured and often killed. christians have faced persecution and discrimination for decades now, including the closing of churches, the arrest of priests, and the banning of bibles. in this context europe is often guilty of silence. it prefers to make trade agreements with china without raising the issue of human rights, freedom and democracy. unfortunately, i see the european institutions as being too subservient to the chinese regime. we need strong action, pressure action and a blockade on imports of any goods made with forced labour. we cannot continue to turn a blind eye to these atrocities. i therefore call on the european union to regain some dignity, to put its fists on the table with china and finally to assert human rights.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are protected. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to sincerely thank my colleagues, first of all the members of the imco committee, those who have been rapporteurs, shadow rapporteurs, but all the members for the work we have done in this examination of this report. it was a very fruitful and practical work, especially without instrumentation, which allowed us to get closer even from somewhat distant positions. and that allowed us to have a near unanimous vote as well. it would therefore be a real pity if, as has happened in other circumstances, the committee on economic and monetary affairs did not take due account of the suggestions we have put forward. i would like to thank mr schwab and all the members of the econ committee for having taken our suggestions into account. it's an important starting point. there's a lot of things to cover. new challenges for you. the commission has had the capacity to deal with the pandemic with derogations, with exceptions. it will be able to do the same in dealing with the conflict in ukraine, maintaining the important points of reference but knowing how to derogate.
mr president, this competition report is an opportunity for some kind of democratic scrutiny of eu action in the competition area. it is essential, however, that the report retains a strong focus on the direct impact eu competition policy has on eu citizens. coming from a small island, which is also a member state of the union, i believe that state-aid rules should allow margins for proper transport connectivity among the bloc’s insular, peripheral and remote regions. for them, the medium-term future is becoming bleak. the transport connections between these regions and major european transportation hubs remain brittle and subject to disruption. this creates for their citizens and businesses a permanent economic and social disadvantage. investment attractiveness remains below par. tourism, a major income earner, becomes more difficult in shoulder and low seasons. general state-aid rules in this context make limited sense. rather, air and shipping lines serving these regions need to be incentivised – subsidised if you want, so that constant and reliable transport connections are ensured. for this reason, i would like to request that colleagues support the amendment to this resolution that makes this point. the citizens of insular, peripheral and remote regions need to feel they are not a forgotten part of europe.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we want to reduce our emissions by 55% by 2030. we even want to be climate neutral by 2030. but this will only work if we involve and support the many small and mediumsized enterprises, the smes, in this process. smes are the backbone of our economy. without it, nothing in europe works, not even the energy transition. if these companies want to invest in climate-friendly energy and energy efficiency, we need to give them much stronger government support, as well as the products and goods they produce. without state aid, this transition, this epochal transformation, will not work. we must also support those companies which are suffering most from the current high energy prices. i therefore welcome the fact that the commission has accepted the criticism, including that from parliament, and has revised its original draft guidelines on state aid for climate, environmental protection and energy. more companies in more sectors, which are suffering greatly from the current high energy costs, can be relieved in the future. that's good and right. but, mrs vestager, perhaps your list is still too narrow. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. we need - and you said it in your speech - flexible action. climate action must remain affordable for everyone and be an opportunity, not a burden, for smes. it's not gonna work otherwise. every company that succeeds in this transformation is good for the climate and creates jobs in our towns and cities.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the oecd is telling us that we will lose one and a half points of economic growth in europe, one third of what is expected, because of the explosion in energy prices, inflation and the sanctions that have rightly been imposed on russia. small and mediumsized enterprises will suffer particularly, but they provide 90 million jobs in the union. i am referring to the ceramic district of my region, emilia romagna, and its importance for social cohesion and the labour market. the flexibility shown by the commission on state aid during the pandemic and the decision to extend first the temporary framework for the health emergency and then the crisis framework were indispensable, enabling the implementation of extraordinary national measures such as liquidity support, access to finance, public guarantees, soft loans. we must continue, the commission must continue to listen to parliament to show that europe is here.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the concerns of the moldovan people have been very high since the conflict in ukraine broke out. moldova borders ukraine and has for years been facing a very complicated situation with transnistria, a self-declared independent territory, but in fact under russian influence. in transnistria there are not only arms depots, but also power stations that supply moldova with energy and the presence of 2 000 russian soldiers, plus 6 000 transnistrian troops. moldova is 100% dependent on russian gas and for them a power cut from moscow would mean economic catastrophe. i have had the opportunity to speak to people during the recent visit of the foreign affairs committee to moldova, where i was a member. and the average salary there is 120 euros, gasoline has already reached 1.40 euros per litre. the country has a still strongly rural economy and the rising prices of agricultural diesel, fertilisers and seeds are likely to seriously affect the entire economy. despite widespread poverty, moldova has shown great generosity. this small country has hosted the largest number of war refugees in relation to its population. moldova is not a member of the european union, but it is part of european culture. it shares our values and is bound to us by the association agreement. it is an increasingly important partner for italy, with a trade turnover in 2021 reaching 520 million euros out of a moldovan gdp of 12 billion. the courage shown by a young woman president, maia sandu, and a government led by another very enterprising young woman, natalia gavrilița, should be supported. in the total absence of female figures at the negotiating tables between russia and ukraine, the strength of women in the governments on the border of the war is an element that should not be ignored. let us learn from their courage and not ignore the very worrying signals coming from that border.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, five million refugees from ukraine are heading for the european union, and 90 per cent of them are women and children. people fleeing war, in need of health care, work, education for children, shelter and protection. today ukrainian women expect real support from this parliament without resorting to ideological rhetoric. unfortunately, in the text, a fundamental word such as motherhood, a sign of life and hope, is mentioned only once in this resolution. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. all vulnerable people must be protected. let us be clear: although this text contains positive elements, it risks exploiting the situation for ideological purposes and to once again beat those countries that do not bow to the mainstream in brussels. on the contrary, we should be grateful to all the countries that are helping, including those on the border, for the immense work of welcoming and protecting women and children. the refugee women are particularly at risk, i am thinking of trafficking and the prostitution market, of which ukraine was already a target, i am thinking of abuse, violence and among the hidden violence in ukraine, let us not forget the rented uterus. surrogate mothers, exploited women who are pressured not to seek refuge in the european union because they risk legal problems in the delivery of the creatures they carry in their wombs. our member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members of the parliament, i thank you for all the remarks. the eu will continue to reiterate its call for a thorough and independent investigation that clarifies all the circumstances of this murder. there must be accountability for her killing. the eu and its member states have more generally recently noted that the eu is concerned about the increasingly high number of casualties, including women and children, and recalls that the use of force must be proportionate and in line with international law, and only as a last resort when it is strictly unavoidable in order to protect human life. the latest developments in israel and the occupied palestinian territory over the last few weeks and months highlight the dire need for renewed engagement between the parties to renew confidence and to restore a political horizon and open the path towards the relaunch of the peace process as soon as possible. the eu has condemned terrorist attacks against israeli civilians. we have condemned the new settlement announcements and possible forced transfers. the eu urges both parties to demonstrate their stated commitment to a two—state solution and to avoid unilateral actions which threaten it.

the next item is the debate on the report by mr terry reintke, on behalf of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, on the commission report on the rule of law 2021 (a9-0139/2022) (2021/2180 (ini)).
rapporteur. – mr president, dear colleagues, some of us, including me, have grown up believing that the global trend towards democracy and freedom only knew one direction – emancipating citizens from authoritarian regimes, strengthening the rights of all people in our societies, making europe and the whole world a fairer, a more equal and a more democratic place. i must admit, colleagues, that i do not believe that any more, and we do not have to look as far as moscow to see attempts to undermine rule of law, democracy and fundamental rights, because the rule of law crisis is also happening within our union – a union built on values, not just economic cooperation, a union built on a promise to all its citizens to safeguard their rights and freedoms. but we are not helpless to these attacks on our values – quite the opposite. in the last years, with the clear leadership of this parliament and many colleagues before me from different political groups and member states, we have managed to expand the instruments for monitoring, for preventing and sanctioning attacks on the rule of law, democracy and fundamental rights. one of those instruments is indeed the rule of law report of the european commission, and the european parliament welcomed the introduction of the rule of law report as a tool to firstly create a common basis, an overview of the situation of rule of law in the member states. we are happy about the commitment and the energy that a lot of people in the commission have actually put into making it a reality, and before i come to the very much needed criticism, i would like to give some credit to those people in the commission who have actually put effort into writing and preparing the reports. but it is also clear that a lot still needs to be done to improve the impact of the rule of law report of the european commission. first of all, so far the report has mostly been an exercise of description – no doubt an important part, but regarding the urgency of the situation also in the european union, we need to go beyond that. we want the next report not only to include recommendations, but to suggest very concrete follow—up in cases of non—compliance including, for example, infringement procedures, the rule of law conditionality and article 7 proceedings. this would not only strengthen the report itself, but would also make the reasoning of the commission behind how they are planning to defend the rule of law more transparent and comprehensible, not only to this parliament, but also to the citizens in the european union. secondly, regarding the scope, parliament has always called for a more holistic approach in the assessment. the fact that an illegitimate constitutional tribunal is restricting the reproductive rights of women shows how closely the independence of the judiciary and fundamental rights are interlinked, and this should also be reflected in this report. thirdly, we want this report to be prepared in an inclusive and transparent way, because civil society organisations, academics and activists, are not ‘nice to have’ input givers, but an absolutely vital part to get full insight into what is happening on the ground. that is why we want ngos to be able to give input, to make the process of writing and preparing this report more foreseeable – and yes, to create a panel of independent experts to give a much—needed outside view into the preparing of this report. lastly, let me say this, colleagues. i have learned that rights we have taken for granted can be taken away from us again. i went into politics not only to leave a habitable planet to future generations, but also to make sure that our daughters and granddaughters do not have less rights than we have, but actually more – to enjoy more freedom, equality and more diversity in their lives. the same is true for all of society. i want this report to become a tool to make sure that our democracies thrive, that everyone can feel safe in their rights, and that we build a union that shows crystal clear not only to the people in europe, but the whole world, that even – and especially – in times of transformation, conflict and disruption, strong, viable democracies are the future, not authoritarian leaders invoking an imagined ‘better, cleaner’ past, to show clearly that commitment to the rule of law and not the law of the ruler wins the battle, not only in ukraine, but all over europe.
on behalf of the author of the opinion of the committee on budgetary control. the commission has already made a number of proposals. i am speaking today on behalf of mr petri sarvamaa, who unfortunately cannot be here and who wanted to address two specific points. first of all, thank you very much for this year's report on the rule of law. we are glad that this year it has become a little more precise, which is very important for us. the committee on budgetary control now knows that the conditionality mechanism has been triggered for the first time. it would be very important for us, however, to see that the future rule of law report also includes an assessment of the fulfilment of the various conditions set out in the conditionality mechanism by law, and that we can analyse them in it, and also an assessment of the measures taken by the member states to solve the problems that have been addressed by the commission. it would also be important to make a clear distinction between individual problems and systemic problems. it may be that at some point in time in one member state a specific error or tradition is not necessarily great, but it is not a fundamental problem, whereas in others it comes down to real systemic problems that run through the whole administration and through the funding and through the state. it is also necessary to make the differences clear.
the commission has already submitted a proposal for a directive to the council. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, respect for the rule of law must be our highest political and institutional priority. it is therefore regrettable that the inaction of the bodies responsible for monitoring the protection of the rights of european citizens has led to such a worrying deterioration of the situation in hungary and poland. our task is to defuse, through regulatory and persuasive tools, the attacks, including media attacks, against judges, journalists, ngos and lgbt people, which unfortunately persistently circulate on our continent. the rule of law is the foundation of the european union, as we are seeing these days, and it strengthens citizens' trust in the institutions. it is fragile, however, because it is based only on the rule of law, it claims the exclusive rule of law. this is possible only when the powers of the state are separated and well balanced, so that none of them can overpower the others. other conditions essential to guaranteeing equality and the protection of the rights of citizens before the law are the efficiency of judicial systems and the independence of judges, since the efficient administration of justice is only achieved where independent judges and prosecutors are active, where adequate human and financial resources are available and where permanent and professional training of the judges themselves is provided: we are also seeing this these days with the topic under discussion of applying artificial intelligence to judicial systems. in addition, and i conclude, an efficient and transparent public administration with management codes and procedures to prevent illegal behaviour is of the utmost importance for a decisive fight against corruption; we must aim to strengthen measures to limit the length and cost of litigation and trials, as well as the funds available for legal aid and means of redress for citizens in need, so as to promote access to justice for all.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i thank the rapporteur for his work. north macedonia confirms that it is an active member of the liberal and democratic tradition of the european union and nato, of which it has been a member since 2020. the european union is also aligning itself with the european union's foreign and security policy and with the european defence policy, including against unjustified and illegal russian aggression, by deciding to send military equipment to support the ukrainian army in its fight for freedom and the rule of law. we strongly hope that the bilateral dialogue with bulgaria will continue, so that we can finally encourage the process of national reforms necessary for full cooperation within the european framework, especially on the minority issue. this is a historic moment to demonstrate our common will to converge towards a single, large and strong european family, bearing the values of freedom and democracy. north macedonia represents a geostrategic investment for peace, stability and growth on the entire european continent; the european union is not a mere institution.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i agree with president roberta metsola that the european union must think of ways to accelerate the enlargement process in the western balkans, because stability in the immediate neighbourhood means stability in the union. and in the face of this new geopolitical context, we clearly need to find a way of thinking about our europe and its unity without weakening it from within. as an italian member of the european parliament, i reiterate my strong support for albania's european path, which is already set out and cannot be slowed down, and i join this parliament in recognising albania's reform and convergence efforts to become a fully-fledged, pluralistic and just democracy, capable of strengthening its regional and international role within nato and in aligning itself with european foreign, security and defence policy, as we have seen in the reaction against russian aggression. the rule of law, one of the fundamental values of the union and the sine qua non for the protection of all the other fundamental values of the union, must be fully realised in albania, so that the past can be turned around once and for all. the albanian people want europe in their future, europe to play its part.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, as an italian and a taxpayer i can only support the directive which finally introduces a minimum taxation for multinational companies and which should therefore be seen as a starting point and not as an end point. the 15% threshold is still too low compared with the taxation applied to companies resident in the union and does not in the least compensate for the enormous competitive advantage acquired and consolidated by multinationals. it is true that this directive will reduce the risk of the evasive phenomena created by the large international groups, which for too many years have not contributed to the revenue of the state in which they carried out their activities because they were not regulated. applying a minimum threshold of 15% is in effect a flat tax and citizens and businesses resident in the union would not understand this blatant and discriminatory difference in treatment. for example, in italy my party, the lega, has long been proposing the same flat tax of 15% on all incomes, but has also encountered enormous obstacles from the european institutions, citing the lack of progressivity. i am, like all of you, elected by the people and not by the multinationals, and i would like to see the 15% minimum threshold raised or, even better, the tax rates for small and mediumsized enterprises reduced.
mr. president, i have to ask you... i welcome the efforts to create a european education area, as it is important that as many european citizens as possible can benefit from quality and accessible education. i also support the recommendation on lifelong learning and micro-certificates for employability, as we need to help those who need to participate in education, training and further training, even for life. as skills and competences acquired in basic education quickly become obsolete due to rapid changes in the labour market, there is often a need for retraining or for acquiring experience abroad. therefore, micro-certificates are very important and it is essential that their introduction remains voluntary for member states, while respecting national competences. the introduction of the european education area and micro-certificates could be a step forward in the automatic and mutual recognition of qualifications, both for european and third-country students, such as those relocated from ukraine due to the war, which would be a great help to them even now, in the current situation.
mr president, commissioner, the committee on culture and education welcomes the commission's ambitions in the field of education and training with these three initiatives which will pave the way for greater access to learning opportunities for all. it is important that these initiatives are fully inclusive, addressing everyone, regardless of age, occupation or gender. member states should bear in mind that the opportunities available through individual learning accounts and the micro-credential system must not only meet the needs of the labour market but also the individual choices of those who want to change their personal development path. it is essential to have a monitoring and reporting mechanism in place to identify any limitations. finally, learning for environmental sustainability must be integrated into school curricula, including through citizenship education, to enable students to become active promoters of sustainable and inclusive societies.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the creation of a european education area by 2025 will include greater cooperation between educational institutions, the digital transition and the development of foreign languages. the cooperation between member states in achieving a common vision of education is welcome, but this must be done in full compliance with article 165, which states that the union may only intervene in education in full respect of the responsibility of the states and their cultural and linguistic diversity. as laid down in the treaties, the european union can only assist, coordinate and supplement the action of the member states in the field of education. we will not accept any interference in our education system. the willingness to cooperate should not become a pretext for the european union to give unsolicited directives to the states.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the pandemic and the changes in the world of work require the european union to strengthen its vocational retraining instruments in order to take advantage of the opportunities offered by the ecological and digital transition. micro-credentials can certainly contribute to the process of up-skilling and re-skilling of staff in the light of digital and green skills, as well as to the re-skilling of workers, provided that there is a clear definition that ensures quality, portability and transparency. however, it should be stressed that micro-credentials should only be a complementary tool and cannot replace traditional vocational education and training pathways. formal education must remain a public good. we therefore support a holistic approach to education to enable students to acquire social and civic skills and not just knowledge relevant to the short-term needs of the labour market.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we all want to see that war crimes against civilian and military prisoners in ukraine do not go unpunished, but unfortunately this will happen, as has happened so far. for example, the perpetrators of the 2014 odessa massacre, in which dozens of men and women were massacred and burned alive in the trade union house, as well as thousands of other civilians in donbass were exterminated by the ukrainian army, as confirmed by reports from un and osce humanitarian missions, have remained unpunished; for eight years we have waited for justice for those victims, but it never came. today, putin is on the dock, and the russian army is the only one, regardless of the responsibility that can be objectively attributed to one or another militia. the european union ceased to be credible in pursuing justice when it ceased to be impartial and uncritically sided with a leader who was not immune from responsibility for the degeneration of the crisis with russia that led to this war. only if and when the union, starting from this house, regains its autonomy and objectivity, will this aim of ours become credible and achievable.
in the name of the id group. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, there are many shadows, few lights, too many intentions that are destined to remain on paper. repowereu is a sign that the commission is living in a bubble. europe is taking the risk of zero emissions, ignoring economic indicators and dramatic growth estimates. this is a plan that starts from the wrong premise, namely that the current crisis coincides only with the conflict in ukraine, ignoring years of wrong policies on energy and climate. and there is a very serious lack: the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of renewable energy sources. from energy dependence on moscow to increased technological dependence on beijing. that's very clever. the extraction, processing and transport of minerals and metals used extensively in solar panels for wind turbines depend on an adequate supply of diesel and other fossil products made, say, by those who produce energy using coal-fired capacity. to write that energy saving is the fastest and cheapest way to lower your energy bills seems a bit trite to me, i would say on the verge of offensive; the commission did not have to tell us this. repowereu is only the latest piece of an ideologised narrative; the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, as the interruption of supplies to poland and bulgaria shows, we are faced with the need to substantially reduce our dependence on imports of fossil fuels from russia. the new repowereu package is no longer just an environmental choice, but sets out strategies and measures that must lead europe to regain its energy sovereignty. i have no time to mention just two points which i consider very important. the first: the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a natural disaster is a good one. the new target of 45% proposed will only be achievable if we can recover and overcome the obstacles by a real reduction in bureaucracy on future projects, as the commission has proposed. the second point concerns energy efficiency. we are very pleased that the commission has proposed a higher target for reducing energy consumption. 13% is the proposal that renew has been pushing for these months: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
mr president, madam commissioner, putin’s criminal adventures make it very urgent now to disconnect from russian fossil fuels. but we must not only become independent of russian gas and oil: we need to disconnect from all gas and oil, no matter where it comes from, because all of it accelerates climate change. we must avoid that the urgency of finding alternatives to the supply of russian gas and oil leads us to strategies, investment and contracts that will end up prolonging the use of these fossil fuels. it wouldn’t be wise to get rid of a politically uncomfortable dependency on gas to generate others, maybe not so uncomfortable, but just as harmful to the planet. because the real alternative is to speed up the deployment of renewables, setting more ambitious targets, establishing new legal obligations, and offering more european funding for deployment. that’s why we welcome repowereu, but without forgetting that the goal is to remove fossil fuels from our energy mix as quickly as possible and at the same time create 100% clean generation model that is local and distributed.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it has taken only a few weeks to destroy the lives of too many people in ukraine, but also to destroy your green utopia, which in recent years has given priority to reducing co2 emissions rather than to the energy independence of the european union, with its consequences for peace in europe. i'll just give you an example, and i only have a minute. president von der leyen, in order to justify the inevitable use of gas, nuclear energy and coal, announced with great emphasis that she wanted to cover our cities with photovoltaic panels. but who produces almost all the photovoltaic panels and who has a monopoly on the minerals needed to make them? china, the world champion of co2 emissions, environmental destruction, suppression of freedoms, ethnic and political violence, is a master beyond the reach of even vladimir putin's russia.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would also like to thank the commissioner to whom our work and appeal is addressed, but i see that she is absent, perhaps she had something more interesting to do than listen to us. i represent in this house the two largest italian islands, sicily and sardinia, which, thanks to their extraordinary beauty and the great adaptability of their citizens, have survived for decades inadequate european policies which have not favoured their development. not a single piece of legislation in any sector - agriculture, fisheries, tourism, transport, to name just a few - has taken account of their particularities. rules made for you and imposed on us. the blackmail of state aid legislation to limit any attempt by the state to compensate economically for the disadvantages of insularity. it is good that today, before the administrators, there is a cross-sectional, compact and united front, which demands with us a pact for the islands, a structured pact not linked to individual emergencies, lasting and which provides for development guided by a precise strategic agenda, with compensation for the disadvantages and the recognition, as for the outermost regions, of dedicated funding. labour, infrastructure, transport, energy costs and technical assistance: these are just some of the things we need to live and create development in our land. anything else, like slaves dressed as immigrants, thank you, but we don't need that!
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, mr rapporteur, islands such as my own sicily and sardinia have suffered and are still suffering the effects of the pandemic crisis, which has severely affected their natural vocation as tourists and has aggravated their structural isolation. if we add to this the effects of the war in ukraine, with the significant increase in fuel and energy prices, the situation is likely to become unsustainable for activities such as agriculture, fisheries, handicrafts and trade. the islands need to start a process immediately to close the structural gaps which prevent them from being truly integrated into the global context. for this reason, a pact for the islands, as conceived, structured and articulated by the rapporteur, which puts in place a wide-ranging planning and takes account of the needs of the territories by including them within the economic and commercial mechanisms, is the way forward. that is why we shall vote in favour of the report, as president fitto has also announced. i think that insularity, therefore, can become a positive factor and not yet considered as a disadvantage.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, 60 years of the cap, whose aim should be to support agriculture and farmers by providing healthy, high-quality food, food that is sustainable for the environment, food that creates wealth and jobs and food that combats climate change. the war in ukraine risks upsetting the entire european and world food system, and we must respond to this risk by drastically reducing our dependence on foreign supplies and by helping self-sufficiency, especially in wheat, in european countries, italy in particular, which also means careful management of fertilisers and pesticides. some people are trying to take advantage of the war to return to practices which are disastrous from an environmental point of view - i am talking about pesticides and not only - and which would harm farmers and consumers, that is, all european citizens. then it takes courage. the cap money is our money, billions of our euros, which should be used to go forward, not backward, to support our farmers, to stimulate production of excellence, to encourage organic production and to protect citizens. never look back, look ahead and use our money well.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, in a context of war, crisis and serious difficulties for businesses and households, how do you respond? with a package of measures which are pure green utopia and which do not answer european problems at all. the prospect you are giving us is one of new european taxes, of a switch to electric cars that will put entire supply chains out of business, of new businesses that will be brought to their knees by new taxes linked to energy efficiency that will affect both companies and citizens, of new conditions and new obligations that entrepreneurs will not be able to support. in the face of your energy failures, you respond with further misguided strategies, which risk creating enormous economic and social damage. the european union contributes about 8-9% of global co2 emissions but you behave as if europe had a much higher share. this is absurd! the european plans, without support for businesses and without incentives for investment, risk undermining our competitiveness. i should also like to say that the prospect of electricity is putting the employment of entire sectors of the automotive industry at risk. relying on a technology that is entirely in the hands of the chinese, who control 80% of the raw materials needed to make batteries, is a glaring mistake. the commissioner for the internal market, mr breton, has admitted, i must say with many months' delay, that the switch to electric cars could mean hundreds of thousands of jobs being lost along the supply chain. well, here it is! and all this while energy and commodity surcharges are sinking companies. europe is building a future which will find us subjugated to the asian giant: this is not what the european citizens are asking for. i heard someone say that this vote will change history. unfortunately, history has already changed and you have chosen not to take the side of the european citizens.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, while noting the need to decarbonise road transport and the importance of orienting the market in this direction, it should be stressed that zero-emission mobility requires significant investment by manufacturers and the component industry, which includes many small and medium-sized enterprises. it also requires the rapid development in europe of a sustainable battery supply chain and a market for affordable new and second-hand electrified vehicles. it should be noted that an exclusively electric strategy would increase dependence on totalitarian states such as russia and china, as evidenced by the first bottlenecks in the supply of raw materials for batteries. we therefore support low-emission mobility, provided that it is economically viable and that it provides for a credible commitment to reducing pollution, without, however, envisaging a definitive stop to the use of combustion-powered vehicles from 2035 onwards. environmental sustainability and economic sustainability have to go hand in hand, or we'll end up in a green world full of failed businesses and unemployed workers.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the conference on the future of europe was supposed to open a debate on the real causes of the crises affecting the union in order to make it sustainable, instead of proposing mechanisms to circumvent parliament, legitimising initiatives in the interests of some and to the detriment of others, since the most discussed amendment to the treaty is the overcoming of the unanimity vote in favour of qualified majority voting in the council, also in the field of foreign policy, common security, budget and taxation. if the objective is to overcome rapidly a mechanism which impedes the development of a common defence, article 48 offers no way of making this possible in a short time, because the simplified procedure concerns only amendments relating to the internal policies and actions of the union and the procedure laid down in article 48 (7) cannot apply to decisions in the field of defence. the elimination of unanimity would also jeopardize the sovereignty of the member states, because it would allow the creation of block alliances between countries, and this is not in the spirit of the treaties. the amendments will have to cover all economic governance, starting with the so-called monetary statute in the treaty on the functioning of the eu, to articles 123, 124 and 125 on the prohibition of monetisation, bailouts and fiscal solidarity, which have made the eurozone unable to cope with the crises of the last decades. the citizens we represent expect a policy of investment and structural reforms, already partly achieved but driven by pro-cyclical punitive objectives, not dictated solely by the deficit/gdp ratio or by shortsighted obsolete budgetary constraints and conditionality, such as state aid, and by an ecb statute with only inflationary targets and not employment targets. it is the ban on lender of last resort and certainly not the modification of the unanimous vote in the council that would compromise the
