mr president, commissioner johansson, corruptio optimi pessima: this latin adage tells us that the corruption of the best is the worst of all, just as the misuse of power by those entrusted with the task of enforcing the law is harmful to the whole of the system of which they are a part. that is the case with frontex, unfortunately. we are not surprised, because we have been on top of it for a good number of years in the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs. you know that very well. frontex is now the giant agency of the european union constellation. it has a budget of 750, almost 800 million euros and an army of 10 000 uniformed and armed officers, whose job is to enforce european law, including the charter of fundamental rights of the european union. that is why, in addition to the misuse of power, which is linked to harassment at work or abuse of power within the agency, the libe committee of the european parliament is particularly concerned about actions in which there have been violations of fundamental rights, unlawful returns and cooperation with illegal border operations and illegal border returns, as has been reported, unfortunately, time and again. so lessons must be learned and of course we agree that the management board and the director need to inform the consultative forum and the agency's fundamental rights officer to ensure that fundamental rights are an essential part of the diet and agenda of the daily work of the agency and all its staff. but, in addition to that, we are now faced with nothing less than the prospect of a renewal of the agency's management because - let us be clear - the political pressure of this european parliament - and you know this very well, commissioner - has been decisive in the resignation of its previous director. so the message is clear. the next management must be fundamentally committed to fundamental rights. he must have political experience of interinstitutional relations and transparent accountability to the european parliament. but above all, it must have a belligerent commitment, which is reflected in its service record, to respect for fundamental rights, which have been binding since the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon with the charter of fundamental rights of the european union.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, your attitude to frontex tonight has the merit of clarity. the sudden change of leadership and the smear campaign against the agency speak for themselves. for a long time we were told that frontex was a european border guard agency. we have always expressed our reservations and your attitude today raises all doubts. schengen, which should be a protected area of free movement, is in fact an area of free movement open to all winds. for you, immigration is not a problem, it is your project. we will be able to inform the people of europe, who are tired of the immigration you are subjecting them to.

mr president, the management of the external borders is not done without faith and law. i would even say that in these extremely sensitive policies, respect for human rights must be at the heart of the concerns of this agency which is now presented as essential for the implementation of migration policy. however, we know this, and it is not the first time that frontex has come under fire, both for its inefficiency, and i refer you to the report of the court of auditors, and for its serious violations of human rights, as mentioned here in the olaf report, which only confirms what we have been saying for months and months: the systematic lack of respect for human rights within this agency. commissioner, you are responsible for respecting the international treaties and conventions which place respect for human rights at the heart of the european project. the message must be clear. the warrant has to be clear. the organisation of frontex must be transparent. these are laws, these are rights and this is good governance in full transparency. and obviously, we're a long way from that today.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, frontex is a completely negative and failed page, it is even a bad example of the management of public money, but, even before that, it is an instrument of blatant violation of european values. it is a shame that the systematic repression of an agency which should help to manage migration phenomena well has instead become a laboratory for institutionalized discrimination, as is also shown by the revelations in the olaf report concerning declassification to conceal cases of clear violations of human rights. i am sorry to see that the european commission is very clumsy and contradictory on the whole issue of migration policies, and certainly not just frontex. the pact on migration and asylum is a glaringly negative example and everything must be changed, with the focus on managing migration flows, reviewing rules, anti-discrimination measures, cooperation between states and european integration and inclusion policies. in this context, we need the opposite of what frontex has been. we need an agency to help and support rescue operations at sea and on land, to cross borders and to manage migration flows in a peaceful manner. if we look at the situation today, there are many examples of situations where we can no longer afford to turn our backs hypocritically, from what is happening in the mediterranean, with the libyan camps scandal, to what is happening in the balkans or, just to give another example, between belarus and poland.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the good food good farming initiative tells us that almost one in three european citizens has hair contaminated by pesticides: the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of pesticides. the farm to fork and biodiversity strategies for 2030 indicate a 50% reduction by 2030, but many states are lagging behind in welcoming the proposed regulation that sets constraints for member states. italy has a national plan that expired in february 2019 and the draghi government seems not to have grasped the importance of the ecological transition, which also goes through a cultural revolution regarding crops and livestock. we have a duty to explain to the citizens how important it is to clean up our agriculture and therefore our waters, our blood, our hair from pesticides and all kinds of poisons. it is a transition that will benefit everyone and that can also be a business and boost agriculture instead of sinking it, as those who do not want to change anything say. but it takes courage, vision and resources. does europe have them? you want to spend it?
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, article 3 of the treaty on european union states that the european union 'shall promote economic, social and territorial cohesion and solidarity among its member states'. the principle is commendable and acceptable, but it only works on paper. we have seen little solidarity between member states lately. it is enough to think of the failure of the management of migration flows and the obstructionism of the frugal countries in adopting the recovery fund. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the principle of solidarity is invoked only when it is in the interests of the european elite and not when it is necessary for the real needs of our families and our businesses, for which adequate and immediate economic aid, more courage and determination are indispensable.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in view of the problems that have arisen since the invasion of ukraine, we are disappointed that the commission has not carried out a study in advance of the social and economic impact that would have been generated by the further impetus given to price increases and the cost structure of enterprises. i think the commission should consider the solution suggested by keynes in his 'how to pay for the war' of '39. eight months after the war began, we still have no common solutions between member countries and european citizens are faced with the dilemma of whether to pay their bills or to buy food, while companies are faced with whether to pay higher energy costs by raising prices or to stop production by laying off their employees. the financial situation appears precarious at both the public and private levels, as the instruments to deal with the first symptoms of a possible systemic crisis have not been put in place. this condition cannot justify the independent initiative of individual member states, which creates serious asymmetries within the single market. if the commission allows the states to intervene directly with their own resources without offering their own alternatives, this will create unlawful competition in the european common market, in clear violation of articles 107 and 108 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union. the most obvious example is the €200 billion aid plan launched by germany to support its own production system. the delayed consequences of this policy would work against the people's consensus towards the institutions, causing disturbances to the stability of the european agreements, just at the moment when their validity is finally being examined in a rapidly changing world.
president, ladies and gentlemen, in my home town of ghent, i see people every day who can no longer pay their energy bills or whose transport has become too expensive, and this while energy companies and other sectors are making record profits. that's to be furious about, because we're not all in the same boat in this crisis. she cynically produces winners. these big winners don't deliver a different product than they did a year ago, but they do get rich off a war in their sleep. so we have to make sure that the proceeds go back to the vulnerable families to insulate their homes, to install solar panels. the commission proposal does not go far enough. we need to take half of those gains in all the sectors that are now sleeping rich. this should be done retroactively, including for previously recorded profits, for example for the pharmaceutical companies during the covid crisis. we must ensure that member states give back those pennies to the people who really need it, the people who today have to choose between food and heating their homes.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the conflict in ukraine, which we must deplore above all, is a boon for the pro-european elites. this conflict must be ended by the only possible way, that of negotiation. i welcome the proposal by mrs marine le pen to call for a large peace conference. in france, other voices are finally speaking up to explain that the implementation of the minsk agreements could have avoided this tragedy. two former ministers of jacques chirac and nicolas sarkozy, luc ferry and pierre lellouche, have recently made their views very clear. no, mrs von der leyen, there can be no armed solution to this conflict because the outcome would be uncertain. there would certainly be only one big loser: europe, our europe, the europe of nations and peoples, of which the european union, the globalist, immigrationist and vassal of the united states, is the permanent negation. but there are probably some in the commission or in these circles who would prefer a good war to a bad peace. yet peace is priceless. and since we are talking about prices, ukraine should not be the pretext for a new european tax - since europeans, as elsewhere in the world, must, it seems, be called upon to pay for damage they did not cause. the ukrainian president himself estimated the amount of reconstruction of his country at €55 billion - in euros, not dollars, of course. we should nevertheless recall the recent report of the court of auditors of the european union, which, although particularly inspired by pro-european views, was very severe on government corruption in ukraine. do you want a tax to finance an endless war? we do not want a war tax or a new european tax.
president, commissioners, ladies and gentlemen, we have entered the triangle of danger that i warned about this summer. climate, energy supply and purchasing power are the three corners of that triangle. instead of wasting our time now with semantic discussions about price ceilings which are inefficient anyway, it is urgent to develop a framework which provides long-term security and in which nuclear energy deserves a prominent place as a stable and sustainable source. in the case of capital gains taxation, some member states, including belgium, are even going further than the european plans and are therefore on slippery ground. energy prices are no longer even the main determinant of inflation today. the problem has become much broader than just energy. social unrest seems to be inevitable at this point. unfortunately, to bring this inflation under control, we must tighten our belts, both in budgetary and monetary terms. the fact that this will lead to a recession has today - unfortunately, because of the long hesitation in some policy areas - become almost inevitable.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, throughout the european union, the high cost of energy is pushing millions of families below the poverty line, and since my country is a country of small and mediumsized enterprises, it is also seriously endangering three and a half million jobs in our country. so the joint purchase of gas is what we have been calling for for some time, and even better the abandonment of the price index of the amsterdam market, where the majority of operators are no longer energy companies but speculators. finally, it is good to set a ceiling on the price of gas, a measure we have been calling for since the beginning of the crisis and which can certainly help the citizens if it is implemented correctly. but what would really make a difference for businesses and households would be to create a new energy recovery fund, thereby collectively recovering capital from the market and transferring it to europeans in need. the measures against the dear bill are not needed now, they were needed yesterday, and one of these will not be enough, they are all needed and they are needed now.
mr president, commissioners, mr president-in-office of the council, we know that there is great concern about the exorbitant rise in prices. we therefore need decisive action by the commission and the member states to enable immediate action to be taken by means of temporary measures. the commission and the council are discussing the possibility of imposing a solidarity levy on fossil fuel companies. it is essential that an exceptional measure such as this achieves its objective and that the funds go directly to the consumers and businesses that need them most. furthermore, contributions should focus on profits, not turnover. they're two very different concepts. it is also very necessary to establish measures to enable the industry to finance gas prices with tax credits, less contributions and a reduction in vat. we must help our industry, because the first objective of the european union and of governments must be to preserve jobs.
mr. chairman, citizens are crushed under the weight of bills, and greed is often the only reason for price increases. consumers are getting bills every day that they don't understand. we need to protect them while they're making deals with the energy companies. we need to pool energy supplies, limit gas prices, separate gas prices from electricity, stop speculation in the energy market. the response to the crisis is solidarity, but that doesn't mean that citizens need to carry the burden themselves in some way. it's time to take from those who have and give to those who have not. some people have a crisis that's a blessing. the digital giants, despite their huge profits, are laying off workers. pharmaceutical companies have become richer by selling vaccines than the countries that have funded them with public money. the surplus of energy companies this year will be 200 billion euros. it's war profiteering. we socialists in crisis don't just offer ideas, we offer solutions. the windfall tax must apply to all those who have profited significantly from this crisis. the pandemic has created a new billionaire every 30 hours, while pushing nearly a million people into extreme poverty. just the ten richest people have more than a third of humanity. it's time for everyone to pay their fair share. we don't know because we need the money, and we need the money, but because it's fair first and foremost.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, luigi einaudi, the second president of the italian republic, called inflation an odious tax on the poor. these are prophetic words in light of how much the rising energy prices are weighing on the low-income groups and i think that in a normal world, people would start to reflect on how harmful the eu's climate and energy policies have been because let's be honest the green deal is becoming a rich man's issue. the goal is shared, the path and the tools are no longer. the war on gas has been raging for years, reducing investment and making our continent increasingly dependent on russia. we cannot wait any longer, we must now show humility and realism and decide whether the union wants to act as a small circle for the rich and the spoiled, or whether, on the contrary, it will abandon the overdose of ideology and start working for the common people.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the cost of the energy bill is no longer sustainable for households and is dealing a death blow to manufacturing companies, which are still bleeding from the economic crisis triggered by covid-19. there are many measures that the european union could have taken to contain the cost of energy: the commission has already made a number of proposals, including the gas price ceiling, the decoupling of energy prices, the tax on extra profits, joint purchases from third countries, but nothing has been done so far. i would remind you that it was the supply of energy from coal that brought the first european nations together 70 years ago. so when we conservatives say that the european union should be less concerned with so many small things and more concerned with a few big things, we are clearly not wrong. we're running out of time. it is time to give the european union a meaning, then it will be too late.
president, when the music stops, it's complicated in terms of liquidity. but as long as the music is playing, you have to get up and dance. and we are still dancing. these are the words of chuck prince, ceo of citigroup, in 2007, just before the financial crisis. today we're back on that dance floor. people back home are watching us to see what the music is going to do in a week, a month, a year. but when i listen to my colleagues today, i hear more of the same: more reckless spending, more european debt sharing, more reckless climate policy, more regulation, more government, more eu. what many of my colleagues are pointing out today is not the solution, but the problem. we are on the verge of a global recession. it's time to kick the habit of free money, subsidies and government interference. the music's stopped, the party's over.
mr president, commissioner, mr president-in-office of the council, prices are still high, electricity and gas bills are still very high and the european central bank is raising rates and will continue to do so. and i think it is important that the political authorities - the commission, the council and parliament - agree that we cannot leave inflation in the hands of the european central bank alone, because in that situation rates would rise so high that we would certainly have a hard time. and if we do not leave the responsibility solely in the hands of the ecb, what can we do? i believe that, in the energy field, there is still much to be done. i think we welcome the commission's announcements, but the capacity to consolidate the european energy market remains unfulfilled. we have a lot to do there. we also need funding to help families and households and the commission's own policy action. and i believe that the solidarity contribution proposed by the commission could well help to fund a european instrument which would, by the way, reduce the problems within the single market.
mr president, mr commissioner, it has been eight months now since this energy crisis started. eight months, and i have still not seen a coherent plan by the european commission. i find it hugely disappointing because your job is not to come up with a fragment approach but with a global structural approach. and there isn’t one. you are talking about price caps, okay. you’re talking about windfall tax. but what is the whole picture? what will you do to tackle this energy crisis? i think you have to do three things. that is an energy purchase platform that is completely different to what you are doing now. you will buy together 15% in 2023 – it is the opposite of what you have to do, to buy together 85%. where is the security fund for the investments that we absolutely need? and where is the energy assistance plan for households or companies, the same as we did with covid-19? why do we not repeat it now? you are right when you criticise in this op-ed the german approach with the 200 billion but it is not only necessary to criticise it, you have to also to come forward with an alternative. and we are going to do that with a number of meps. in a few moments, we are going to send you a letter about it, with these cross-party proposals.
mr president, mr vice-president of the commission, honourable members of the council, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! winter is coming. many households and businesses are asking themselves: how can i pay my electricity and gas bills? how do i cope with the high cost of living? it has been said by many colleagues that valuable time has been wasted. but now we finally have proposals from the commission on the table - late, but as i said, there is something on the table: solidarity levy, depletion of electricity, gas price brake, joint purchasing of gas, new benchmark for fft, so a lot is on the table now. i would like to use my speaking time today to call on the member states to finally take action here and to ask once again, also in the direction of the council presidency, to finally tap the fingers of the member states. so far, each country, each member state has done its own thing. we are far from having a european internal energy market, ladies and gentlemen. but if we want to get a handle on these problems, then we have to act together, which means we have to use all the resources that we have in the country to generate energy.
mr president, dear colleagues, dear commissioner, for months, we’ve been saying to people that the rising cost of energy was something limited and temporary. but it’s not been like that. and, let’s face it, it will not be like this in the future. governments had to intervene massively to support families and businesses and will have to do that again in the future. but it’s not going to be sustainable in the long term, we know that. so what does it mean? it means that this will affect the competitiveness of our businesses, will affect the single market and bring about fragmentation and will also have spill-overs that will aggravate and accelerate a possible recession all over the european union. so we need to intervene very quickly, urgently, on two fronts: one, provide a genuine european instrument to provide support to those countries that don’t have the fiscal space, and also to restore the single market. and then we need an incisive intervention on the energy market – yes, the joint procurement. but we need something more ambitious on the gas price, decoupling gas from energy prices. if we’re not ambitious now, we will regret it when it will be too late.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the particular historical moment we are living in, with its strong economic and social repercussions, requires us to pay even greater attention to the issues connected with the management of the community budget. the sensitivity of citizens, burdened first by covid and now by expensive bills, would impose that call for ethical conduct already reiterated by the court of auditors in its special report no. 13 of 2019. unfortunately, that is not the reality. in recent days, the european union agency for asylum has been in the news again. the current director, nina gregori, who was appointed just three years ago to restore the credibility of the agency following the abrupt resignation of her predecessor, accused of mismanagement and harassment of staff, has now been the focus of a new investigation with allegations of nepotism, irregularities in financial management and cover-up of internal complaints. the agency was given a new name and a new strengthened mandate last january to help unify the way member states handle asylum applications, but just three years after the previous scandal, history seems to be repeating itself with a new olaf investigation into director gregori. cases like this, with a union agency with more than 2 000 employees and regularly making the headlines for scandals involving it rather than for its own work, undermine the confidence of citizens in the european institutions and, frankly, madam president, i find this mismanagement of our taxpayers' funds during the worst economic crisis since the second world war to date to be irreconcilable.
mr president, the general eu budget for 2023 is a powerful political tool. there is a lot of politics inside, not only economics. in times of skyrocketing inflation and economic recession, politics has to deliver. if politics does not deliver, we will have explosive populist upsurges, anti-system protests and nationalist outcries. during the pandemic, we were able to basically double the budget of the eu: with next generation eu, sure, react-eu, we got another eur 1 000 billion – exactly like the multiannual financial framework. we were kind of magic. now we have a similar challenge with the energy crisis. increasing resources for erasmus+, the just transition, is good news, but it is not enough. we need fresh money coming from new own resources to provide on-the-spot responses to gas deprivation and energy prices. we cannot lose the credit we had during the pandemic. we cannot lose the battle against inequalities and social despair. we cannot lose face in front of our people’s expectations. we simply cannot.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the european union is facing its greatest challenge, that of reviewing its economic governance. the stability pact has shown its weaknesses over the years and no longer meets the needs of the european citizens. the time has come for a fundamental reform that will put an end to austerity once and for all and promote green investment and the sustainable energy transition, which are also necessary to achieve the objectives we have set ourselves in this house. however, we regret that the commission's work programme this year also lacks action for full equality for lgbtq+ people, a promise made by president ursula von der leyen right here in 2020. the paternity and even the union of many gay couples are not recognised anywhere in europe and this is an injustice that we must address immediately. i therefore expect the european commission to present a legislative initiative to defend the rights of all as soon as possible. enough with the discrimination.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, many years ago europe decided to abolish the internal frontiers between its member states in order to develop the exchange of goods and people on a continental scale. this has helped to develop networks, infrastructure and trade within the different areas of europe. but prosperity and economic development cannot be achieved unless they are accompanied by the fulfilment of a fundamental duty: the security. the roman empire was based on the pax romana, which guaranteed the peoples within it to prosper, while the emperor's legions guarded its borders, administering the possibility for the barbarians to enter or not. when border security was lost, the prosperity of the peoples was lost. let us ask ourselves how we intend to administer and defend this european peace if we give up internal security and the defence of external frontiers. for years the countries bordering the mediterranean have been the ones regulating the inflows that then flow into the whole of europe. in the years of leftist rule, the policy of indiscriminate reception has brought in hundreds of thousands of irregular migrants. now, fortunately for us, the league has returned to power in italy, right on the southern border of europe, and we italians will be doing this immense job of controlling and guarding our european borders. we expect your support.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the the commission is not every single refugee from ukraine is under our protection. i really mean all the people from ukraine who then come to us. i am also talking about the roma who come to us and have great problems at the borders. i am referring to third-country nationals who work there or are present there in any way. i mean students and people who aren't ready to take up arms. we therefore also call for the protection of russian oppositionists, but also of conscientious objectors and deserters, and of all, not just russians. there must be rules, open channels. with all the things you've portrayed here, there's no such thing. we need political asylum for these people, we need humanitarian visas that can be distributed in the eu embassies of all countries, not just individual ones. a stop to deportation is necessary for these people. i would also like to say one thing in conclusion: it is also a moral question whether these people are also given support. because the best way to end wars is to not go.

mr. president, i have to ask you... hungary is making extraordinary efforts to welcome ukrainian refugees as part of the largest humanitarian action in its history. we are one of the eu member states that has the largest number of ukrainian refugees per capita. the provision of care for refugees is continuous and comprehensive. thousands of ukrainian children are being placed in our public schools. we provide hospital beds for over a thousand patients, and we spend on medical equipment and we help ukrainian soldiers rehabilitate. while the war has brought 1.5 million war refugees from the east, mostly women, children and the elderly, the number of illegal immigrants from the south - mostly young men - arriving on the western balkans route is growing rapidly. armed groups are organized to attack border guards. this also shows the need to strengthen the union's external borders. instead of the failed migration strategy in brussels, support should be given to the member states that ensure the protection of the union's external borders, including hungary.
(the speaker begins his speech in english) the russian war of aggression against ukraine has several facets which are increasing the migratory pressure and the humanitarian pressure in the european union space. on the one hand, the ukrainian citizens, where the european response has been excellent, has been very, very, very strong, has been very helpful to these families who are hopefully able to return. we cannot, however, rule out, with the increase in the violence of the russian attacks, a new wave of ukrainian refugees, since the war has escalated and is now at a much more severe stage than it was a few weeks ago. but a second point we must also draw attention to is that there is at the moment a state, serbia, which has visa-free agreements with a number of countries and is greatly increasing the flow of migrants to europe, particularly from cuba, india, tunisia and burundi. at the various european borders, whether in hungary, croatia, greece, or even partly in romania and bulgaria, we see the arrival of this new flow of migrants and it is essentially done, as lukashenko did in belarus. the idea is to bring as many citizens as possible to the european area, in order to create a stress at european borders. this action by serbia cannot be accepted and must be strongly condemned.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the horrors of this war continue to be witnessed by many of us, and with the goodwill of much of the international community. the bombings are multiplying, the civilian casualties are increasing, those who can leave everything and flee, fuelling the flows to europe, which this time welcomes, activates a directive and shows solidarity, but which unfortunately fails to create the opportunity to create a systemic reform of immigration and asylum policies. i ask the commission: we are not going to be able to do that in the near future, i think, but we are going to be able to do that in the future, in the very near future, to enforce the rights of those citizens in many parts of asia and africa who depend on russia and ukraine for their food. we are not going to allow them to knock on our doors, as we have done with the ukrainian citizens, or are we going to regard them as victims of exploitation, or, even worse, are we going to reject them as enemies, perhaps with the complicity of some european agency?
madam president, (start of speech off microphone) meeting the council will reaffirm the eu military, humanitarian and financial assistance to ukraine. the commission could propose a more structural solution by granting ukraine eur 1.5 billion per month in 2023. as for military aid, the eu has so far provided eur 2.5 billion to ukraine and the foreign affairs council a few days ago agreed an additional eur 500 million and a new eu military assistance mission for ukraine with an envelope of eur 106 million. how will this new expenditure be justified in front of the european court of auditors, which in its last audit report has issued an adverse opinion on eu budget expenditure, and on this precise chapter explicitly recommends the commission to take appropriate action to reduce outstanding commitments in the long term? moreover, after adopting eight packages of sanctions on russia, the european council wishes to introduce additional sanctions that, as previous ones did, will just heavily harm eu citizens and assets. at the same time, the only concrete measures so far introduced to contain high energy prices is just rationing to curb demand, which will lead to further downturn in economic dynamics. we are on the edge of an abyss. shall we dive into it to understand that it will kill us?

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, unfortunately we are again participating in a debate today which we hoped would not be pure propaganda or one which would lead to factionalism if the italian right wing were called into question. the attempt to label a specific political party as extreme is evident, with the intention of criminalizing the electoral results which, even in a founding nation of europe such as italy, have been there. telling the story of an anti-european right-wing underlines the precise desire to tell a reality that does not exist, since the italian right-wing has always been, over the decades, pro-european and rooted in the west. in the last thirty years the italian right has participated in national governments, has elected tens of thousands of administrators, hundreds of mayors and regional presidents, i myself have been mayor of a large city, we have carried out our political and administrative action in accordance with the italian constitution and the laws and we have submitted ourselves, at the end of the mandate, to the judgment of the electors. now the italians have given a large majority to the centre-right and giorgia meloni, leader of the brothers of italy, is preparing to chair the italian government. we do not need any rehabilitation and we will continue to fight all forms of extremism and violence.
mr. president, i have to ask you... the left side of this house is not going to rest. every month, he generates a right-wing insulting debate. this is often called the hungary or poland debate, but now that citizens in two more member states have dared to vote en masse for right-wing parties, it was time to put a broader mudslinging on the agenda. you are doing this at a time when it is more necessary than ever to stick together and focus on how to jointly remedy the grave situation caused by the war and the sanctions. you've been shouting "snake" to the right in italy and sweden in the campaign. and then, because it didn't work, because the citizens didn't buy your insulting words, they're trying to retaliate. much of the left has long been anti-european. how come they put all these labels on governments that are in the process of forming? you think the left side has a center and the right side has an edge. why didn't they cry wolf when in spain the maduro regime's favorite, the far-left podemos, was elevated to the socialist sánchez government? or when in hungary, the anti-semitic, anti-gypsy jobbik teamed up with the socialists and liberals? you're now post-fascist, and the crimes of communism and nazism are forgivable? enough of this double standard. i want more respect for the people's free and democratic choice.
mr president, the far right, which is now present in many european countries, is illiberal and therefore opposed to european democratic values, the separation of powers and the rule of law. it is nationalistic and xenophobic, and therefore opposed to human rights, the rights of migrants and minorities. it is also populist and eurosceptic, and therefore opposed to the european project. and these are not insults, this is the exact description of the political reality that is the extreme right in europe today. and that is why we must say that the governmental and parliamentary alliances which the centre-right and the liberals are now making with the far-right in various european countries are not only a dangerous game which normalises and strengthens the far-right, but also a betrayal of our european values. and so this debate is not just about the far right, it is about whitewashing them, it is about mr berlusconi, about mr tajani, about the epp, about the renew liberals. the cordon sanitaire is not discrimination, the cordon sanitaire is a response in legitimate defence of democracy and a respect we owe to the mandate we have received from our own voters. it is time for the centre-right and liberals to think twice before they continue this dangerous game and before it is too late.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all, congratulations on your appointment, mr president. the court's study of the eu's financial situation shows us how far we still have to go to have a truly effective and efficient budget. the court stressed that there were still too many budgetary errors and that all the expenditure on the erdf had to be managed. these conclusions are urgent and strongly express the need for efficient management of expenditure, especially in the light of the present downturn. first covid, now russia's war against ukraine and the worrying energy crisis with the concomitant rise in prices. the budget has been increased, and rightly so, because the eu is mobilising its resources and providing greater flexibility to respond to the crisis. in a context where resources are needed to help our citizens and our businesses in a concrete way, errors in the european budget must be reduced as much as possible and there is a need for a stronger fight against fraud, also improving the recovery of money that has been wrongly paid.
madam president, mr murphy, congratulations, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the court has again delivered a negative opinion on the european commission's budgetary management in 2021, noting a high error rate in several specifically identified expenditure headings. the commission has also provided information on the implementation of the union's macro-financial assistance to the countries of the region, including the implementation of the union's macro-financial assistance to the countries of the region. the eca therefore recommended that the commission ensure that vaccine manufacturers comply with the terms of the ppas. the court then pointed out the risk to the european budget of high-risk loans and credits to ukraine turning into liabilities, recommending measures to reduce existing financial commitments and mitigate the risk of ukraine's insolvency, but so far the commission has totally ignored these recommendations, exposing the eu budget to certain future losses. it is therefore brazen to ask member states for more resources to deal with the difficulties arising from a commission decision which does not intend to correct the course to bring the eu budget back on a sustainable footing. the money of the european taxpayers must be managed with diligence and prudence and if the commission does not intend to do so, we will draw the appropriate conclusions.

in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, a few months is all it took to bring about a collapse in world food security. the war in ukraine, the global shortage, the general panic. with a chain effect, the great powers began to ban agricultural exports. china, india, malaysia and the united states have decided to protect themselves at the expense of those who, like the european union, relied on these products. in fact, in a few months' time, the european union has been confronted with a crisis involving fuels, fertilisers and foodstuffs, in particular wheat, maize and vegetable oils. these raw materials are experiencing low stocks, reduced production and disrupted supply chains. what do you do? at the global level, the g20 summit in bali called for a formal commitment from the world's major economies to focus on food security and lift trade restrictions. at european level, we must review the supply policies pursued so far, which are heavily dependent on the outside world, and we must start producing again and strengthen our food sovereignty. as an italian, i want to commend the technological and digital advances of our agricultural sector, which, by combining innovation and tradition, continues to provide us with safe and quality products. local produce that we need to value, not sabotage from within with follies like nutri-score, which make corporations fat at the expense of citizens. now is the time for action, to ensure food on our tables. enough with the ideology.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, art, beauty and culture are the antidote to violence and horror and the highest form of human expression. today, with the return of bombs and tanks to the heart of europe, which are the responsibility of putin, and tens of thousands of innocent people forced to abandon their homes and their lives to face fear and the unknown, we must be firm in rejecting the logic of war and oppression and ready to offer our solidarity to the ukrainian cultural world and try to maintain the thread of dialogue. the war has hit artists, cultural workers and the academic world hard, spreading a climate of fear, at the expense of artistic freedom, freedom of teaching and expression. art and culture will then have the difficult role of healing these wounds, rebuilding countries and ties, and for this we owe a great tribute to those artists and creators who did not stop in the face of barbarism, but reacted by continuing to practice their art, art as a form of resistance and critical thinking, as well as free and independent journalism. we must pay attention to the ukrainian cultural heritage. cultural and historical works are a treasure to be protected with all the legal instruments at our disposal to protect and prevent the illegal trafficking of works of art. this crisis has demonstrated the need to set up - and i am particularly referring to the commission - a european response and recovery mechanism dedicated to cultural heritage and the cultural ecosystem. a mechanism that brings together public and private partners and that takes into account the strategic role that philanthropy can play in supporting the cultural world, just as it is important that we invest strongly again in creative europe. art and culture in general speak the language of peace. they belong to the whole world. weapons are instruments of destruction. culture and education are bridges, informal diplomacy, they foster dialogue. the conflict has created a false vision, which blames anyone who defends freedom of artistic expression. the polarization has also sparked criticism and harassment of russian intellectuals, artists and scientists, and has helped isolate those who have spoken out against putin's misguided choices. racism towards the ukrainian world and the attempt to "wipe out" ukrainian culture are simply unacceptable. the responsibility for the war lies with putin and his authoritarian regime. but i also want to say that these responsibilities have nothing to do with the extraordinary artistic, literary and musical production that russia has given to the world over the centuries. we need to hit putin, not the culture. protecting the role of cultural dialogue is the first step towards a ceasefire and post-war peace.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, this is a precedent. we are concerned about this regulation, not only for the reasons which have been stated by my colleagues, but also because it may set a precedent for other seas. i am thinking of the mediterranean sea, for example, or my adriatic sea, where a ban on trawling would be detrimental to our fisheries and our economy. fisheries management is a complex matter in general and trawling is no exception. the fleet is specialized. to stop this trade would mean to destroy the fleet itself. much has been done and is still being done for the european cod, both in the common fisheries policy and in national policies. progress must be made in the right direction by means of gradual adjustments, assessing the impact of the measures, the response of stocks, the economic and productive performance of a sector which is facing substantial investment, employment and a flourishing market. in short, we must be sustainable in the full sense of the term. instead, we have seen for years a demonization of fishing, and in particular of trawling. the real problem is that we do not want to distinguish between the targeted, sustainable and cutting-edge trawling that our fishermen use and that which is practised by third countries, which are predators of the deep sea without reservations. this is nothing but worrying and discouraging for european fishermen, who are distressed by continuous policies of reduction and prohibition, even where there is a commitment and a desire to be sustainable and above all to survive. the commission must then take responsibility for saying that it wants to dismantle the european union's fishing fleet, that it wants to import only fish and fish products from outside the eu, perhaps from the same countries that have yellow and red cards for iuu fishing. i ask you, commissioner, do you really want the death of european fisheries? draft opinion of the consultative committee on agriculture and rural development. the commission has already made a number of proposals. as the author of the opinion on agriculture, i have to say: repowereu a good idea. and even if agriculture is not involved, or, rather, does not have to be involved, it is still a good idea. perhaps there are three aspects, three reasons why the committee on agriculture decided against making funds available for repowereu: firstly, the budget: 20 billion in total. the agricultural sector would have been in the dark here. but the second, and more important, point is the philosophy of support: support in rural areas, which is, of course, the bottom-up principle. the people on the ground should decide what the money is used for. it's for the farmers and for small business, it's for the people. and thirdly, the main argument: everything described in repowereu use of renewable energy, use for rural areas is already possible in the foreground of rural areas today, but for people, for small businesses. and that is why we wish repowereu all the best. the rural areas are involved in their support programmes, and i ask the commissioner to be careful here too: the member states are very keen to use agriculture as a financial quarry. you can't do that!
executive vice-president of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, two years ago, we were working together on devising the recovery and resilience facility – an unprecedented demonstration of eu solidarity arising from the challenges posed by the covid—19 pandemic. now, today, two years later, we are faced with another crisis, triggered by russia’s unprovoked invasion of ukraine. again, as the eu has set out a coordinated response, we are moving to stop our dependence on russian fossil fuels. the commission would like to thank the honourable members for your intense work on the proposals to introduce repowereu chapters in the national recovery and resilience blocks. essentially, it’s focused on green and digital transitions and the rrf remains the best—suited instrument to address the energy challenges emerging from the current geopolitical context. in fact, the rrf has already shown itself to be an efficient tool of quickly deploying financial support in times of crisis and bring about ambitious investments and reforms that respond to the common european priorities in a manner tailored to national circumstances. with a proposal to enable member states to tap into additional funding and introduce repowereu chapters into their recovery and resilience plans, we will leverage the strengths of the rrf framework to respond to russia’s mobilisation on energy markets. now, more important than ever, it is vital to accelerate a green transition, increase the use of renewables and raise the energy efficiency of our building stock and industrial processes. the commission welcomes the strong work done by parliament. we look forward to the expedient handling of the legislative process to make the repowereu chapters a reality. the member states have already started to consider measures to feature in their repowereu chapters. they should now start to accelerate those preparations as the legislative process moves forward. it will also be important for these new investments and reforms to take into account the eu’s collective energy security so that measures taken by one member state can reinforce those taken by others. we hope that tomorrow’s vote will again demonstrate the strength and determination of our european unity and solidarity.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, commissioners, ladies and gentlemen, the energy union is essential for the european union and repowereu is very positive in this respect. however, there are some problems here, and i would draw the commission's attention to them. there will be no energy independence for the european union without cross-border projects and interconnections. that leaves one question: what are the cross-border interconnections and projects that the european commission is advocating? we should have met them by now. they should be quantified by now, so that they can be financed later. what the european commission has done is to announce a nice sum of 300 billion euros, but it does not define what is really essential, taking a risk. because this is going to be in addition to the recovery and resilience plans, which are currently in poor execution. a country like portugal, for example, needs interconnections, crossborder projects and the proper implementation of these funds, and there must be no national selfishness, as mr macron in france has shown, which has made it impossible to implement a project that was essential for the european union and for portugal.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, the current energy crisis and the high inflation require urgent decisions. tomorrow, the european parliament is called upon to vote on this regulation, which allows member states to amend their national recovery and resilience plans by including new reforms and investments in energy related projects financed by additional requests for grants and loans. the urgency of the situation leaves no option but to proceed quickly to the negotiations with other eu institutions and deliver to our citizens. as the european parliament, our proposal is to ensure that projects aim at tackling energy poverty for households and smes can benefit from this funding. this has been one of our group’s priorities. we have also included a provision requiring mandatory consultations with social partners and other civil society stakeholders, as we have noticed the lack of proper consultation during the drafting of the national recovery and resilience plans. it is also important that we ensure that members are allowed to amend their plans due to the consequences of war inflation by including various measures such as social housing. we also support cross—border projects and include a reference for the eu to give particular consideration to remote, peripheral and isolated regions and islands which already experience additional constraints. the rrf, with the repowereu chapter, is an important instrument for member states to cope with the short-term effects of energy crises, safeguarding security of supply and accelerating the long-term transition towards a decarbonised energy system. however, this is not the end, and for certain this is not enough. we need more courageous decisions immediately by the council and the commission, such as a reform of eu economic and fiscal governance, eu fiscal capacity and common tools to respond to economic shocks on a european level, learning from experience. because, after all, our job is to deliver to the eu citizens.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, tomorrow we will be voting in this house on another historic milestone for the european union: repowereu. with this text we adapt the recovery funds to the energy crisis. repowereu is not about improving energy supply in poland or storage in spain; repowereu is about ensuring that no household or business ever runs out of energy on european soil. however, receiving funds from the european union comes with responsibility. that's why, when we passed the recovery funds two years ago, this house called for reforms instead. we got countries like spain to commit to very important reforms that had been pending for decades, for example, to make their pension system more sustainable or their labour market more efficient. the european socialists now want to scrap these reforms; they are removing a phrase from the commission's proposal which explicitly prohibits using the energy crisis as an excuse to change the reforms which have been committed to. if the socialists' text is adopted tomorrow, we will be putting the credibility of our economies at risk, and good luck if we want to repeat in the future a recovery package like nextgenerationeu. ladies and gentlemen, we will do whatever it takes to overcome the energy crisis, but please, let's not create another crisis to pay for this one.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of energy from renewable sources. recently it has become clear to everyone in our part of the world that energy security and energy independence are essential, strategically invaluable and necessary for our free way of life. repowereu should help to reduce our energy dependence in the short term without taboos, including in the area of investments in oil and gas supply. parliament rightly calls for crossborder investment to form a solid european network. repowereu was created as an extension of the national recovery and resilience plans. it should ensure rapid implementation, but should under no circumstances undermine the much-needed reforms already agreed under the recovery and resilience plans.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner gentiloni, when the pnrrs were drawn up and approved, we were in a very different geopolitical context from the present one. a year ago, energy production was not a priority for us in europe, unfortunately, nor was renewable energy production. for example, in the italian plan, under the measure called 'renewables and batteries', only one billion euro was allocated, compared with the total of 190 billion, or 1 in 190. very little! so welcome the possibility of amending national plans so that we can invest in energy production from fossil fuels such as gas for as long as it is necessary. it was a tragic mistake to become dependent on energy imports from russia and china. let's not do it again. to err is human, but to persevere is diabolical.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. first of all, i would like to thank you, commissioner, for taking up our suggestion to deal with fertilisers and for presenting the communication today. unfortunately, i see more shadows than light in this announcement. i see little effort in the short term to improve the fertiliser situation in europe. the anti-dumping duties on trinidad and tobago and the usa could have been suspended; i'm afraid that's not part of the communication. i see a lot of good long-term approaches that you've described - so far, so good - particularly the increased use of organic fertilisers. however, the possible solutions already on the table are mentioned only in a footnote, such as renure, struvit, stripping or regional flexibilities under the nitrates directive; they are either mentioned in passing or in a footnote or not at all. i would say that the commission has unfortunately fallen short of the expectations i would have had for this commission. i would ask you to look again at the situation in europe and the world and to act accordingly, not only in the longer term but also in the short term, in the interests of food security and our agriculture in europe.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not undermined by the failure of the member states to implement the common agricultural policy. in addition to high energy prices, food prices are also a very important driver of inflation in the european union. however, the communication does not contain any specific commitment to raise the use of renure above the standard for the use of animal manure. only with such a commitment can renure do what it was designed to do, namely replace and save fertiliser. in 70% of the eu, farmers are restricted in the use of their own animal manure. however, this restriction does not apply to the use of fertilisers. farmers must, often for a fee, dispose of their own animal manure and then buy expensive fertilizer to meet their crop needs. this is highly illogical. i am convinced, however, that the adoption of the new action plan for integrated nutrient management will give animal manure, and thus also renure, the recognition it deserves.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the war in ukraine and the energy crisis have had the effect of limiting the quantities of fertiliser available to farmers. in this context, the agricultural sector is facing one of the greatest challenges today with the increase in fertiliser prices, with an average of around 200%. in many cases, farmers are forced to reduce their activity, which implies a reduction in future production. the european commission, in today's communication, identifies some solutions which i would regard as insufficient, commissioner. the use of the agricultural crisis reserve should, in my view, be activated as early as 2023. member states were given the possibility to grant some state aid schemes to farmers, which they did. but we must understand, commissioner, that the action of the member states is limited by their own budgetary resources. that is why, in this context, i urge you to come up with new, serious money for the agricultural sector, if we really want to save it. in the long term, the european commission's objective must be to develop fertiliser production capacity within the european union, but in the short term it is vital to turn our attention to other third countries that are able to supply fertilisers to farmers in the european union at prices that are affordable for them.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the commissioner has just announced a communication on fertilisers, which i welcome as the subject is explosive for our european farmers. you recall measures that already exist: the prioritization of gas supplies for the fertiliser sector, flexibility in state aid... all of this is obviously in the right direction. however, these are essentially measures that have already been taken. these are also measures which refer to the member states. my question is therefore simple, commissioner: what is the real european strategy, what new measures are needed to cope with this explosion in costs and to make fertilisers more accessible to farmers? the risk, with this referral to the member states, is a risk of renationalisation, but also of fragmentation and competition at the level of the market and at the level of european farmers. finally, commissioner, you did not want to lift the anti-dumping duties on fertilisers produced by third countries. i think we need a measure along these lines here too. beyond a strategy for fertilisers, we need a strategy for food security.
madam president, commissioner, thank you for the message we received today on food safety and the link with mineral fertilisers. you spoke yesterday to the committee on agriculture and again today about the scale of these increases and the cost to farmers. and indeed, he himself and the european farmers expect some concrete help from the commission. the measures you have mentioned are measures which, i must simply say, i do not know what to tell the farmers to help themselves. by encouraging investment in hydrogen to produce ammonia from natural sources, i do not know whether we will save european farmers, but neither will we save food security, because i am afraid that the fall in fertiliser consumption and consequently in crops will be so great that next autumn we will be more afraid than we were of whether there will be gas in european storage facilities, whether we will be afraid of whether there will be food in european soups. the way we're handling this message, i don't think we're going to solve the problem.
the commission has already done so. you can't praise much in a minute. that is why i have four critical remarks. firstly, we have heard from mr dorfmann that there is a loss of 44 billion, and that our agricultural catastrophes amount to 450 million. we're not gonna be able to figure this out. secondly, i would ask you to reconsider carefully. it would be important to suspend the import tax and the anti-dumping duty temporarily as a signal to our european farmers. third comment: promoting circular agriculture, organic fertilisers, taking a more bold approach in place. i would like to see some footnotes in the original text or vice versa. i ask you to make it clear to the commission that we need a circular agriculture, and that includes the proper re-use of organic fertilisers. my last request - the key word is harmonization - is that you have presented a package of measures. i am afraid that different member states will apply this extremely differently and that this will lead to distortions of competition. we've got to be careful. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter.
a member of the commission. - madam president! - i have to go. i thank all members for their very interesting, inspiring and sometimes critical comments on the communication. i expected this because the subject is difficult and in a short time we presented what the commission was able to propose to improve the situation. i did not say everything in my first speech, so i can only add that the issue of certain derogations relating to manure, the use of which is subject to certain limits, the application of certain derogations will be considered in the next document that the commission will present at the beginning of next year. integrated nutrient management action plan is the name of this document and this issue will be considered in this document. it is not closed, but we did not mention it in the communication in question because it requires more analysis and we were not able to address it in that short time. as regards the anti-dumping duties, it was a conscious decision not to decide to remove them. we have to protect our production. the european union is heavily dependent on imported fertilisers: for nitrogen fertilisers, it is around 30% and for phosphorus potash fertilisers, the majority is imported, so the protection of producers in the european union against dumping is simply necessary. we might have improved the situation in the very short term, but in the long term we could have made it worse and we could have lost what we have - the ability to produce locally. it's a real burden on public assistance, i'll give you that. in the common agricultural policy, which has a budget of €58 billion per year (that's about the amount available, which is overwhelmingly allocated to direct payments, to the second pillar programmes), there is little room for financial action from the eu budget. we all know that. that's how the common agricultural policy works. the possibilities for emergency intervention are limited, and this is essentially a reserve for emergency, to which we are open. and indeed, the burden of fighting the crisis has largely been shifted to public aid. i would like to remind you once again of the 455 billion euro that member states have allocated to public aid for the economy affected by the russian aggression against ukraine. we raise the issue of public aid to also point out to the member states that food safety issues should be more clearly identified and that this sector, such as fertiliser production, should be included. this then gives you the opportunity to control these entities (are they not making excessive profits?), to create a policy in this area. i know that this is not a long-term solution and that it is risky because it also creates some competitive inequalities in the market. but today we have no other instrument, which is why we are so strongly in favour of public aid. i think that the action that we are pointing out in the communication, that the public authorities try to take such action, to organize public purchases of fertilizer and distribute it at a lower, much lower price to the farmers, that would be some solution. aid to the production plants, to the fertiliser companies was necessary or is still necessary if only because at some point these plants stopped production. we had a situation like this in august, where a number of them, or a significant number of them, stopped production because because of the high energy costs, because they were not sure if the farmers would be able to buy fertilizer, so this kind of action is necessary. i think what we could offer at this point is contained in this communication. of course, we'll be analyzing and monitoring the situation at all times. there are certain things that will be in that document that i mentioned, which is due to be adopted early next year and which the commission is working on. i would just add that the commission has proposed the abolition of customs duties on imports of ammonia and urea. we are now awaiting the council's position on this matter. the anti-dumping duties, on the other hand, are intended to ensure the production of fertilisers in the european union and to protect our producers from dumping, which is always a bad thing and in the long term very dangerous. i would like to thank the house and the members for, firstly, the inspiration for this communication and, secondly, for a very interesting and important debate.
thank you, commissioner, for this very timely discussion. the debate is closed.
the next item is the debate on the report by pascal durand, on behalf of the committee on legal affairs, on the proposal for a directive of the european parliament and of the council amending directive 2013/34/eu, directive 2004/109/ec, directive 2006/43/ec and regulation (eu) no 537/2014, as regards corporate sustainability reporting (com(2021)0189 – c9-0147/2021 – 2021/0104(cod)) (a9-0059/2022).
the rapporteur. madam president, i would like to begin by thanking all those who worked on this dossier, with a special word for the advisers and assistants who worked under absolutely incredible emergency conditions, and also thanking all the fictitious rapporteurs and all the fictitious rapporteurs i see in the room tonight who helped me, who helped us to ensure that this text could be presented in time and that it would take up the essence of the elements that were dear to us. we did so with a very constructive spirit, to the extent that this text was voted - as you know, madam commissioner - unanimously in the juri committee, which is rare. i also take this opportunity to thank, beyond your text, the commission services, who helped us find the answers and build this consensus with the council, since the council also unanimously adopted this proposal for a directive. so i won't go on too long: everyone knows this text in detail; i would just like to emphasize two points. this text is a major development in our view of business and the role of business in society. we are certain that the world in which we are currently operating is a changing and moving world, and europe wanted - this is an extremely important sovereignty issue - to be a law-maker on these extra-financial, environmental, social or human rights issues, that is to say, to be the originator of rules and texts, not to isolate itself, but to be able to discuss them on an equal footing and no longer simply be a debtor of texts that were imposed on us from outside. you have raised this essential question, madam commissioner. parliament has given its full support to this approach, to the desire to make europe, which has developed its green pact, its green taxonomy and its own vision of the implementation of the paris agreement, the main driver, including in the economic world. you know - i say this every time, but we must never forget - that the european union is certainly the place on this planet where we have the strongest balance between the three pillars of sustainable development: environmental issues, social issues and economic issues. we are trying to ensure that these three pillars are never separated from each other and that we move forward together. your directive, mrs mcguinness, perfectly incorporated that constraint. it has also perfectly integrated the fact that the short-term, often cynical vision of a society in which the first and foremost objective is to produce at low cost, to relocate, to exploit people, nature and the environment, is now outdated. this worldview is no longer desired by investors, who now want to ensure that the reputation of the company is not damaged and that the risks and effects it may have are taken into account, nor is it borne by society, by consumers, who want to know the conditions under which products are made. this is what this directive is about: moving away from the arbitrary declaration to standards that are shared, standardized and controlled. this is the strength of our union, this is the strength of the rule of law. you implemented it, we followed it too. it is now up to the member states to take their responsibilities on this matter and ensure that we have effective control of these declarations.
member of the commission. – madam president, colleagues, honourable members, i’m trying to catch the eye of our very committed rapporteur to say, at the very outset, thank you for your passion this evening and your commitment during the process. it is as a result of that and your collaboration with the shadow rapporteurs that we are able to have this debate and to sign off on this important piece of legislation. i think we are all reminded of the challenges we face, particularly this week as cop27 takes place in egypt. there is so much to do around climate and biodiversity, and we need both finance and information to be successful, to change the scenarios which are so alarming. it is a huge challenge. this debate on the corporate sustainability reporting directive, in my view, represents a very important achievement in our collective efforts towards a more sustainable economy and society. it will support and steer companies as they make the transition to more sustainable business models and a more sustainable economy. the directive will strengthen the rules on the environmental and the social information that companies have to report. for the first time – and this is how significant this debate and this directive is – we are putting sustainability reporting on an equal footing with financial reporting, and this is hugely significant, as other speakers have already alluded to. we need accurate and reliable information to ensure that investments are being made towards a more sustainable future. companies need the information to plan their transition paths, and investors need the information to have clarity about what they are investing in and to combat greenwashing. because greenwashing, if left unaddressed, could reduce investor confidence in sustainable investment products, not to mention public confidence in sustainable finance and in companies’ efforts to transition. clear and consistent disclosure requirements, together with the new assurance requirement, will improve the reliability of sustainability information. the final text of the directive provides a good basis for alignment with the proposed corporate sustainability due diligence directive, which is currently in trilogues. the text also addresses the particular situation of smes. only listed smes are subject to reporting requirements, and there are provisions to prevent smes in supply chains from being unduly burdened by trickle-down information requests from larger companies. the european financial reporting advisory group, efrac, is now in the process of improving its draft standards so that they are in line with the directive and implementable by companies. european standards need to strike a balance between providing useful information for investors and stakeholders and the potential cost and burden for reporting companies. the commission is fully committed to ensuring that this balance is achieve, considering the current economic situation. eu standards must take account of global standards, including the standards currently being developed by the international sustainability standards board, the issb. technical discussions between the commission, efrac and the issb are taking place to achieve as much commonality as possible between european and global standards. so i now look forward to the rest of this debate on what is a really ground—breaking piece of legislation that we must insist is implemented fully, and meets the needs of companies, investors and citizens.
the commission has already submitted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the new directive on corporate communication and sustainability is certainly an important step forward in improving the level of transparency on the behaviour of companies and also a lever to direct and improve their commitment. this is essential for achieving the objectives we have set ourselves, for countering the effects of the climate crisis, for respecting human rights, life, dignity, community, the rule of law, good governance practices. the transparency principle can only be the first step towards a new way of doing business, towards socially and environmentally responsible behaviour, behaviour which cannot be left to the goodwill of a few enlightened experiences, which obviously already exist and whose value must be underlined, but which must be governed by precise, uniform rules, rules which apply to everyone. we are therefore facing a very ambitious project, which refers to a more sustainable, more humane and more just globalisation, and it is a great opportunity that we cannot afford to lose.
on behalf of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the globalisation of capitalism is promoting the violation of human rights and the degradation of the environment. large companies in the european union, like those in the united states, rely on supply chains that originate in poor and therefore vulnerable countries. everyone remembers the 2013 rana plaza tragedy, a dilapidated building in bangladesh that housed workers working for major western brands and whose collapse caused more than 1,000 deaths. it is in an attempt to remedy this kind of abuse that the durand report, which we are debating, proposes to force large companies to publish information on the impact of their actions on human rights and the environment along their supply chain. during the discussions i had set two conditions in support of this text. first, exempt smes from this obligation, except on a voluntary basis, as most of them would have difficulty coping with it. secondly, to make large non-european companies operating in the union subject to it in order to avoid distorting competition in comparison with large european companies. both conditions have been met. i therefore support this text, which should respond to the legitimate aspirations of the european nations by initiating a moralisation of capitalism.
on behalf of the ecr group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, first of all i would like to thank mr durand for the excellent work he has done and, above all, for always listening to the different political positions, so much so that a consensus has been reached. at a time of great economic turmoil due to rising energy costs and the critical issues in some supply chains, we are faced with the difficult task of finding a balance between the competitiveness of our businesses and the need to promote sustainable and environmentally friendly business behaviour. that is why, throughout the legislative process, i have worked on behalf of the ecr group as a shadow rapporteur with the aim of reaching a pragmatic solution. we have succeeded in excluding small and mediumsized enterprises from the scope of application and in including non-european companies: a very important change. although the outcome of the interinstitutional negotiations includes some aspects which i would have preferred to exclude, i believe that we have managed to avoid a heavy bureaucratic burden for european companies by reaching a more balanced agreement than the initial proposal. for this, i thank all those who have contributed to this work.
