madam president, ladies and gentlemen, imposing equality on the boards of large companies is a frontal operation, which is certainly pleasing to progressives, but which unfortunately does not solve the problem of gender disparity at work at its root. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a welcome one. this is demonstrated by the case of the lombardy outpost and here my thoughts go to governor roberto maroni, who helped make it great where 48% of managers in all of italy work. then we need more pragmatism and less ideology. we do not need preferential lanes, but fair competition on equal terms, in order to achieve real equality, not just ease, which can guarantee not only women's rights but also the economic and cultural progress of our countries. (the speaker agrees to reply to a 'blue card' speech)
the answer is "blue card". i believe that betting only on the pink quotas is a diversion from tackling the problem. we are facing the digital transition and there will be new professions of the future, especially in stem subjects, but we must be aware that only a very small percentage of women approach technical-scientific subjects, because they tell us that these subjects are probably the property of men. so we are working on this, giving guidance especially in schools among the younger ones, to bring women up and approach these subjects, where they also achieve even better results than men, to actually give them quality jobs, which allow them to balance work and family. this is true equality of opportunity, competition must be there, equal arms, but competition must be there. (the speaker agrees to reply to two 'blue card' speeches)
the answer is "blue card". in this connection, i have just mentioned the good practices of my region, which is the lombardy region, where 48% of women managers in italy reside, without the need to impose quotas. we have achieved better percentages, but i am not satisfied. the problem is that i do not think that quotas are an appropriate instrument. we need welfare, that is the only solution and that is the only keyword we have to pursue. to ensure balance and equal opportunities we must allow women not to have to choose between career and work. the creation of quotas in the boards of directors means only that women will be given preference in elite positions, but beyond the women who work in the boards and the women managers, there are millions of women who do not have the opportunity to access these positions, but who still have the right to have a career and to be able to balance their family life. so we can't do elite politics, we have to think about all women, leave no one behind.
in the name of the id group. mr president, commissioners, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to remind this house that the vast majority of european citizens believe that 'grants', as provided for in the recovery fund, are 'gifted' resources, that is to say, resources which have been made available out of thin air by the european union, whereas in reality they are obtained through the so-called own resources. it would therefore be appropriate to inform the european citizens better that, for own resources, and for the recovery of new ones, nothing is meant except taxes which directly or indirectly always end up in the pockets of the citizens. i am also very much concerned that all the planned grants will be offset by the planned revenue from the new own resources and that other extraordinary revenue, which will also be borne by the citizens, will have to be used in the course of the work. in short, the system is very much like the famous game of three cards at the village fairs, where it is already known who always fails. in the end, as you have now seen, it only takes a minute to make the citizens understand this misunderstanding correctly.
member of the commission. – mr president, thank you again for this timely and interesting debate and for your constant support to diversify the revenues of our union budget. new types of revenue will avoid undue cuts to union programmes or increases in member states’ contributions. the commission will present the second set of new own resources still in 2023. this is one year ahead of what is foreseen in the institutional agreement between the european parliament, the council and the commission. it underscores our commitment to sustainable eu public finance. the second set of new own resources would complement the first. it aims at generating sufficient revenue for the repayment of ngeu borrowing. as you know, the institutional agreement names two possible contributions for this second basket of resources, one linked to the corporate sector and one linked to the financial sector. but there are also other possibilities like a digital levy, for instance. in the field of corporate taxation, the commission intends to come forward with a proposal for a single set of tax rules for doing business in europe. this could form the basis of an own resource linked to the corporate sector. ngeu repayment is not a choice, but an obligation which we have towards investors and to taxpayers. it’s a test of unity and credibility of the union to repay our debts. and this unity will be closely watched by the outside world. our joint effort, therefore, should be to negotiate and agree a balanced package of new own resources. and again, it’s something which is very much linked to the credibility of us, to the capital markets, which are now very important for us more than ever in order to raise the necessary amounts to support our citizens.
the rapporteur. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, with our proposals, with our opinion, which i am sure will be adopted, we have achieved some 15 billion euros in revenue without penalising citizens, contributing to the objective of combating climate change, helping to ensure fiscal justice and accelerating the objectives of the digital transition. new revenues that serve, it is true, to pay off the debt, but we cannot stop there. it's not just to pay off the debt of nextgenerationeu: it is essential that revenue should also be obtained for the common projects, the strategic objectives of the european union, its strategic autonomy. if we do not invest in security, in the energy union, in strengthening our military capacity, in health, in civil protection, in what are the chip acts, the semiconductors, in secure connectivity, if we do not have these investments, we will pay a huge price. investments which should be in joint projects, because they bring effectiveness, efficiency. with fewer resources we can do much more, and this is a goal we must have: to make the commitments, to pay the debt, not to burden the next generations. on the other hand, we must have a budget which invests in a way that gives the european union autonomy, so that we can give the citizens of the european union a future. and in that sense, if we do not invest in these common projects, as the lack of the energy union shows, we will have and we will pay a very high price. the european parliament, myself and my colleague, valérie hayer, are already working on proposals for a new package and on making suggestions to the commission. the commission should come up with the second phase and the second package, also in terms of what the road map for own resources should be. the council must now decide unanimously. it is up to the council to move the european union forward. and i hope that the council, instead of rulers, for once, also has leaders. the european union needs them.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place on wednesday 23 november 2022.

the minutes of yesterday's sitting have been distributed. is there any comment? i declare yesterday's minutes of the sitting approved.
executive vice-president of the commission. – thank you very much for this question. so indeed, from the commission’s side we put forward orientations for a possible revision of our fiscal and macro—economic governance framework. the idea is now, we hope, that based on those orientations, a consensus will be emerging among member states, with the european parliament, with different stakeholders, and if this is indeed the case, we will be following this up with legislative proposals. so if everything goes according to plan, that would happen in spring next year. orientations, as we put them forward, are obviously already taking into account feedback we were receiving from member states, from the european parliament, from others during quite a long period of discussions. as you know, we launched this process already in february 2020 as there was some suspension during the covid-19 pandemic, but nevertheless we had quite extensive discussions. correspondingly, what we are putting forward is already, in a sense, factoring in those discussions and narrowing them down because we need to get more concrete on where we actually want to go with this review of fiscal rules. you mentioned the question of additional common fiscal capacity. well, we were clear in those orientations that here we are focusing on fiscal rules. so we are not opening another very – as we know – broad and also very controversial discussion on this common fiscal capacity. so here, indeed, it’s about how the future of governance framework could look like.
executive vice-president of the commission. – so as i was already mentioning, we clearly have this discussion also on a broader kind of future of the eu budget, additional elements, sovereignty fund, euro area fiscal capacity. we have all those discussions. so what i’m basically emphasising is that we are not making those discussions part of the discussions of our fiscal and macroeconomic governance framework because that’s already, i would say, a complicated and complex enough exercise. you mentioned, for example, specifically nextgenerationeu. we remember that a number of member states agreed on the nextgenerationeu explicitly on the condition that this is a one-off instrument. but we all know also that the debate on common, for example, euro area fiscal capacity predates nextgenerationeu, and we do not expect that this debate will disappear, but we are just emphasising that it is not part of this particular exercise.
the commission communication indeed proposes to improve the sanctions regime, which i think makes sense. it improves the corrective arm of the fiscal framework, so basically the sticks. but i’m wondering, what are the carrots you’re proposing or having in mind to ensure greater compliance with the rules? because conditional access to already—accessible funds like the structural funds doesn’t seem very compelling to me as carrots.
member of the commission. – i’m not sure that this sticks and carrots mechanism could work, but anyway i take your point. in my view, indeed, the effort we made with these proposals is an effort to strike a balance. so in this case, we can also say a balance also between sticks and carrots. the balance is on a more simplified mechanism where the rules are more transparent. we have a simpler single indicator of the evolution, which is based on the net primary expenditure. stronger ownership from member states, because member states are the ones proposing their plans of reduction of debt and investments. the possibility to use the investments in the common priorities of the union to have an even more gradual path of reduction of the debt and at the same time having more gradual, more realistic, rules, more capable to incentivise investments to have them also more enforced. this enforcement is not based on enlarging the sanctions but paradoxically, it is based on making the sanctions lighter because lighter sanctions are possibly also sanctions that we can really enforce.
you mentioned the individual ownership of the member states that you tried to focus more on in the proposal. i’m wondering, in this regard, do you think that this should also involve a greater role for national independent fiscal institutions?
member of the commission. – this is always a point we refer to in all our papers and documents. and rightly so, in my view. in an ideal world, we should work to strengthen these independent bodies. in the real world, we have to do this taking into account the fact that their reality is rather unequal, country by country, and so that it is not easy to say, overall, that these bodies should have this specific role. there is, i think, a common understanding on the fact that the commission is proposing to give to these bodies a stronger role, but we are not so much framing this from the legal point of view, taking into account exactly the fact that the reality is different country by country or from this independent body. so strengthen them, but not forgetting that they are quite different in their capacity and their level in their autonomy because they are independent – but yes, independence is a fight in some countries.
i want to go back on the investment issue and in particular on the national investments. i think this proposal is very positive, very good in a way that tries to find a better balance between the stability and the growth, and investments – national investments – are crucial to that end. the only thing that i wonder, in the legislative proposal that i’m confident will be presented very soon, i think that we will need a little bit more clarification on some aspects of these national investments: how they will be treated, which kind of investments, which conditions. i mean, i would like the commission to elaborate a little bit more and to share with us some clarification on this matter.
executive vice-president of the commission. – indeed, so it’s very clear that in coming years, we will need to deal with both issues. we will need a lot of investments for our green and digital transitions, for the resilience of our economies and, at the same time, we will also need to put current high levels of debt on a clear downwards trajectory. so it’s about finding the right balance. so one element in our intentions is about giving more space or allowing member states to have more gradual debt-adjustment trajectories if they are performing, growth enhancing and fiscal sustainability, supporting reforms and investments. so those are the two elements, that’s the investments, which we want be supportive of growth and fiscal sustainability, and also responding to common european priorities and obviously also, of course, country-specific challenges. so we outline these elements as ones which we will take into account when deciding whether to allow member states to have a more gradual debt-adjustment trajectory.
let me be a little bit more specific. in the communication of 2015, the commission provides a clear and specific region for certain investments, you know, that takes into account not only compliance with macroeconomic criteria, but also the type of investment. that’s what i wanted to look into, with particular reference, but of course not limited to co-financed investments that are part of european projects. i think this is an important part. i would like to have clarification. are you considering proposing something similar in the new governance as well? because i think in the upcoming years, that would be very, very important and very valuable.
mr. high representative, thank you for your clarifying words. looking ahead, we can say that the food crisis could kill more people than the pandemic. the numbers of food insecurity and hunger in the world are shocking. the european union is a major player in the world economy. what ukraine was putting into the global market before the war was millions of tons a month, so we're just seeing the first signs of the disaster that's coming. so i ask you: in the effort to put together emergency food aid, can you tell us what changes the union is going to make to the aid system to improve it, since it was not sustainable even before the war?
mr. high representative, thank you for your reply. in order to quickly resolve the obstacles to the black sea grain initiative, which has its own peculiarities, for example, it does not contain a dispute settlement provision, and which grants the secretary-general and turkey a high degree of independence as mediators, and this seems to me to be the most important practical point of the agreement, would the european union like to introduce provisions or rules to that effect?

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, enlargement policy is the most important test of the credibility of the european union and of our values. while this process has immense importance and added value for countries in the process of joining the european union, at the same time, at the level of the union, some member states are unfortunately behaving as if it were some sort of irrelevant and secondary process for european interests that would be best forgotten. our reflections on whether to open accession negotiations with countries have opened the way to the strengthening of the influence of other global and regional actors, some of whom have openly anti-european views. the report successfully identifies the key issues of the accession process, the transformative role, transparency and consistency. but let's look at the western balkans, what was happening in north macedonia and albania, for example, let's look at what's happening with kosovo. the serbian government must first and foremost align itself with the european union's foreign policy positions and i think we need to make that clear once again. we urgently need to make the member states and those who want to join aware that the european union is still an attractive community. ladies and gentlemen, we are aware of the fragility of peace and geopolitical turmoil, and i therefore welcome the efforts being made, and i am hopeful that the aid in bosnia and herzegovina, which must continue on its european path, of course, after the elections, will be given. the enlargement process must be given a new impetus to restore its integrity!
i would like to thank mr picule for his efforts in this important area and i am in favour of the enlargement of the european union. however, i do not agree with the proposed reform which would allow decisions on enlargement to be taken by qualified majority rather than by consensus, because in this way some of croatia's neighbours, such as serbia, could join the european union without croatia agreeing to it, and in this way many of the issues that croatia has with its neighbours would remain open and would burden both croatia and the european union in the long term. the second thing is, it's clear that as a representative of croatia, the last country to join the union, i think that the enlargement of the european union is a success for the european union, but i think it's not good to overly equate the success of the european union with enlargement because if that's the most successful european policy, what will happen when the european union expands to the end? when we reach the borders of europe, then there will be a collapse. so i think that the success of the european union should be sought within the european union itself, by strengthening subsidiarity, genuine democracy, pluralism and a real approach to politics.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, dear mr picula, i just want to remind all of us that this commission was the commission who put back enlargement on the table. the beginning of the mandate of this commission, we have adopted a revised enlargement methodology which makes the enlargement process more credible, more transparent and more dynamic, and ensures a better political steer over it. we have put a much stronger focus on the fundamental reforms – as this has become the core issue of the whole enlargement process – as the key goal of the transformation power of the enlargement. we have made it possible to move faster for candidate countries if they deliver faster, for example by the introduction of the cluster system. we have made it possible to integrate our candidate partners into eu policies even before accession. the last months have demonstrated that the eu enlargement policy is living a new momentum. so i don’t think we need new procedures, but we need political will, both from the eu and from the candidate countries. enlargement is a geopolitical priority for us and for our allies, therefore. the idea of joining the eu is interlinked with a need for reform, freedom, security and stability. we have seen with the new applicants it is our responsibility to live up to these expectations to further strengthen the enlargement policy based on strict but fair conditionality, and the principle of own merits will have to continue.
rapporteur. – madam president, mr commissioner, thank you all once again all of you for your remarks and contributions. i want to thank first of all the shadow rapporteurs for our excellent cooperation throughout the process. of course, thank you all colleagues who took part in the debate with ideas, contributions, hopes and in some cases scepticism. as my introductory remarks were mostly focused on what the eu should do to accelerate the enlargement process, now i wish to focus more on the countries aspiring to become members. the success of the enlargement process also depends on them, as you said mr commissioner, it’s true. it is a twofold process and a partnership. excluding ukraine, which is fighting for its freedom, we see persistent political crises these days, tensions between the countries, and foreign interference. however, we need good news and positive examples of development and cooperation. we need to see that you follow our principles and values in practice. moreover, we need to see political consensus in your countries on the eu accession that would be above internal political differences. a european future is what your citizens support and deserve. europe is where you belong.
thank you for your report, mr picula. that concludes the debate. the vote will be held tomorrow. written statements (rule 171)
in writing. the neglected enlargement policy needs a new impetus, as it is an effective geopolitical weapon against russia, which is increasingly a threat to european peace and security. to restore its credibility, the eu must reform its decision-making processes and abandon the requirement of unanimity for the admission of new members. the eu also needs to fulfil its commitments to the western balkans and the eastern partnership countries and not to offer candidate countries alternatives to full eu membership. the eu must pay more attention to ensuring that the people of the candidate countries have a better understanding of the union's support and the results achieved. more should also be done to prevent third parties from interfering in the political, democratic and electoral processes of the candidate countries. the eu should also and rapidly punish countries where the reforms needed for accession have stalled or been reversed, and reward those candidate countries that have made sustained progress, for example by granting them accelerated access to the single market.
the next item is the debate on the report by giuliano pisapia, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, on a european parliament recommendation to the council, the commission and the vice—president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy on the situation in libya (2021/2064(ini)) (a9-0252/2022).
the reporter. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the recommendation on libya is the result of months of intensive work. i would like to thank our institutional and civil society partners for the fruitful exchange of views, and especially the shadow rapporteurs for their cooperation and contribution. the situation in libya is becoming more and more dramatic and the postponement of the elections has shattered the hopes of the libyan people for peace and democracy. libya has become a dustbin where new actors join old ones, without making any progress on the issues of democracy, justice and national reconciliation that libyans have been yearning for for years, if not decades. in recent decades, many actors have blocked libya's democratic and development process. the european union can and must become a protagonist of peace in libya too, after a period of, let us say it boldly and truthfully, often deafening silence. this is another reason why the european union must appoint as soon as possible a special representative for libya to support the mediation work initiated by the new un special representative. they need our technical assistance for a new roadmap that will finally lead to credible, inclusive and democratic elections. the recommendation proposes that a european union electoral mission be sent to assist the electoral process and that all necessary technical support be provided. we must use the force of reason, but not just the force of reason, to help the libyan authorities and put an end to the violent repression of civil society organisations and the shameful situation in the detention camps. the use of eu funds to support, among others, the strengthening of the rule of law, gender equality, social inclusion, local communities is therefore welcome. local and foreign militias are enriching themselves on the backs of migrants and asylum seekers. the violation of human rights is systemic and impunity reigns supreme. this too requires a central authority that takes responsibility for guaranteeing the rights and duties of all. a stable and prosperous libya could make a significant contribution to reducing regional unemployment and preventing many people from falling victim to human traffickers. there are points in the recommendation, very important points that we must not forget: the commission is also aware of the fact that the european parliament has been involved in a number of important debates on the human rights situation in the middle east, including the rights of women and children, the tragedy of displaced persons, religious and ethnic minorities, the fight against gender-based violence, the grave problem of landmines, the proliferation of weapons, the abolition of the death penalty. i think we did a good job. i hope, therefore, that the recommendations we shall be voting on tomorrow will not just remain on paper, but will be implemented and not ignored, as has unfortunately happened in the past. we need all of the european institutions, and i repeat, all of them, to commit themselves to making their contribution so that libya is more important in our agendas, in our thoughts, in our commitment. we owe it to the libyan people and their desire for peace.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we are discussing a very important report today, because the libyan question is a geopolitical, energy and migration time bomb. unfortunately, the bomb that explodes in our hands today was set off by the same european powers that brought down and overthrew the gaddafi dictatorship, but without having a plan for how to rebuild libya afterwards. after many years, no international actor has yet come up with a solution to the libyan problem, and the international community is content to have crystallized a civil war on the libyan people. libya is right on the southern border of europe and nato. while europe is rightly committed to defending the north-east front, we are neglecting the mediterranean front. foreign powers like russia and turkey are taking control of libya and unfortunately here too europe is the last to understand and solve issues that are geopolitically strategic for our continent. we have seen in other contexts how immigration is used as a weapon of blackmail. do we want to allow the main port of departure for illegal immigration to europe to be controlled by others? we, more than anyone, need to protect the southern tip of our continent. to talk about libya in this house means to talk about issues such as political stability, the role of hydrocarbons and the immediate halt to migration flows. italy cannot take on the task of stopping illegal immigration to europe on its own, as ministers piantedosi and salvini are already doing, there is a need for identification centres in libya run by international organisations and for funding for frontex to fight human traffickers. we are claiming a leading role in the mediterranean. stopping traffickers on the beaches does not endanger anyone, but the business of the traffickers themselves and their protectors at all levels. perhaps it is time for the entire international community, including europe first and foremost, to go to libya for good and to clean up the mess it has made.
on behalf of the ecr group. – madam president, commissioner, colleagues, the world is better without dictators. yet, 11 years after the arab spring and fall of muammar gaddafi, we have to step up our efforts in order to help libyans bring stability and peace to the country. we have to support, with all our might, the efforts of the un special representative, who is already in tbilisi, and also support the acceleration process of coming to presidential and parliamentary elections, as well as a referendum that is to adopt the constitution and thus abandoning the constitutional covenant still in and in power. we also have to use necessary financial resources supporting operation irini and the eu border control mission.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we europeans are paying for our very serious mistakes and our deep divisions in libya. others have since filled a vacant space: turkey, russia, egypt, the gulf states. the risk we run as european institutions, commission, member states, parliament, is our fundamental irrelevance. the european union needs credibility, unity and determination to regain its role, as the pisapia report, which we are discussing and comparing, shows. we must work politically for a new berlin, to ensure a free and united future for libya, a united future that holds libya together, to guarantee a democratic process to elect a new parliament and a new executive, to free the territory from the presence of too many foreign arms and private armies, to demand the closure of unworthy detention centres and the release of immigrants, deprived of any right to citizenship, to ensure, along with all this, an economic, social and infrastructural programme for the reconstruction of libya. that is the task we must now carry out.
madam president, commissioner, colleagues, the previous eu policy on search and rescue attracted criticism for incentivising human trafficking. unfortunately, the current eu policy on search and rescue promotes torture in captivity. one injustice has replaced another, and the stories that are emerging from reception centres in libya are truly chilling – so much so that the united nations fact—finding mission has concluded that these stories are so widespread and so compelling as to be suggestive of crimes against humanity. and this is a phrase, of course, that is not used lightly. as regards search and rescue, sometimes, in my view, the intrinsic rightness of something is impossible to ignore. it’s impossible to consider the secondary consequences. and i would have thought, uncontroversially, that rescuing somebody from the sea is intrinsically the right thing to do. so i would urge the european union to restore search and rescue. i would urge the european union to legislate for the migration and asylum pact, and i would urge the european union to cooperate with ngos.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i must thank mr pisapia and the other members who have worked on this document, because it took a great deal of work and courage to negotiate effective recommendations, despite the attempts by the right to insert misleading messages, to criminalise solidarity and therefore the work of ngos at sea by promoting them as a factor of attraction. the vote tomorrow must make it clear where we stand. the so-called libyan coastguards are responsible for intercepting boats carrying migrants and refugees and for illegally detaining them. investigate the atrocities and the mechanisms of monitoring and accountability and then we must stop funding and cooperating with those in libya who are accused of serious violations and involvement in human trafficking.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, libya must be stabilised, our security is at stake, but if we are to achieve political stabilisation, europe must play a leading role in correcting the tragic mistake of 2011. we can no longer afford to have a country on the southern shore of the mediterranean that is constantly divided, with weak governments, influenced by turkey, russia and other regional actors, leaving the field to armed militias, often infiltrated by jihadists, who manage the illegal trafficking of migrants to europe. of course, the conditions in libyan detention centres are terrible and we must bring international organisations there, but this cannot make us accept that it is human traffickers, often with the benevolent complicity of ngos, who decide who can and cannot enter europe. in north africa we must bring investment in return for a real commitment to combating illegal immigration, with coastal patrols, the destruction of the boats of the divers and everything that will be necessary to put an end to this shameful traffic, which does nothing but enrich the criminal organizations that are holding libya and all of us in check.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, a few weeks ago a wolf's head was found hanging on a road sign in a village in the valchiavenna region. it was a deplorable, violent act, to be condemned, which nevertheless testifies to the degree of conflict between large carnivores and local populations and demonstrates the failure of the life programmes, with more than 24 million euros spent. the reality is that the wolf population in europe is constantly increasing: the number of confirmed cases is now over 21,000, although we know that there are many more. farmers and ranchers, the true guardians of the mountain ecosystem and biodiversity, feel abandoned and even mocked by those institutions that should support and protect them. in italy alone, 18,000 predations were recorded in five years, with 44,000 animals killed by wolves. and that's just a small part of it, because farmers don't even file complaints anymore. it is said that the first step in solving a problem is to take note of it, commissioner. but you do not turn your head the other way, you allow all the possible instruments to be put into place to overcome this situation. the key word must be management, otherwise the conflict will only get worse. there are proposals that can be shared - i am thinking of the swiss proposal - that can find a sustainable meeting point between the needs of local populations, which, in my view, will always have priority, and the needs of nature and the conservation of biodiversity. do not squander centuries of history, tradition and sacrifice in pursuit of a false myth in our mountains, which instead risks bringing back the wolf but making man disappear.

in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i will speak as the simple parent of a boy who is 100 per cent disabled and not as a member of this parliament, and for a minute i will ideally be the father of all the disabled who cannot be here. i have read the report 'towards equal rights for disabled people' carefully and wonder why all the european institutions have never paid full attention to the real problems faced on a daily basis by disabled people, their families and those who lovingly care for them. the marginalization, discrimination, especially in the workplace, the insurmountable bureaucratic difficulties, the differences in health care and economic support within the member states are still too much ignored, when it is precisely by full respect and attention to the disabled that the real level of civilization of an organized and democratic community is measured. i am speaking as a member of this parliament and i can only say that the report we are discussing includes, in addition to people with disabilities, other categories of citizens who need other forms of protection, which no one denies, but which are certainly not comparable or superimposable to those of disabled people. why can't disabled people be the subject of a specific and autonomous relationship and why is it necessary to include other categories which have nothing to do with the world and the needs of disabled people? yet we are talking about 87 million european citizens, 24 million of whom are severely disabled, not to mention children. are they not sufficient to exclude other categories from protection? finally, i would like to thank our president, mrs roberta metsola, for giving us this pin of parliament, and i am one of those who can wear it with my head held high.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we have recently seen a renewed and unanimous awareness of the issue of disability, as evidenced by the numerous legislative proposals and the increasingly frequent institutional debates. it is clear, however, that this is not enough and that it must be made clear. there are too many barriers to the active participation of people with disabilities in society, from training in schools, where barriers to inclusion still persist, to employment, where prejudice often trumps skills. if we add to this the fact that in many member states there is still no legal and tax recognition for carers who assist disabled people, we see that there are still many gaps. in short, there is an urgent need for practical but also cultural change, which allows people with disabilities to feel that they are protagonists and not just recipients of welfare policies, and which allows each of us to recognize ourselves in a truly inclusive and accessible society for all.
member of the commission. – madam president, it’s non—negotiable. the strategy for the rights of persons with disabilities is anchored in all the rights of the un convention. recognising persons with disabilities as rights owners is the entry point to a change of perspective in disability policies. persons with disabilities are citizens entitled to, like everybody else, to take their decisions, have ambition, develop their talents and flourish in their private, educational and professional lives. we listened carefully to you while setting the priorities in the strategy, as you know, and we expect continued cooperation with you, the house of european democracy, as the strategy becomes a practical reality. all three institutions must take seriously the implementation of the strategy for the rights of persons with disabilities and discuss ways to address and to better our practices in that regard. i value all opportunities, including today’s debate, to develop our work. together, we can progress towards building a union of equality.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place on tuesday 13 december 2022. written declarations (article 171) the following documents shall be submitted to the court of justice:
in writing. ensuring equal rights for persons with disabilities is essential for a socially sensitive european union committed not only in words but also in deeds to european values. for many years, a detailed and very important debate has been taking place here in the european parliament on the equality of persons with disabilities. however, we must recognise that this policy issue is a multi-dimensional and multi-layered problem requiring a complex approach. among our fellow citizens with disabilities, children, women and members of ethnic minorities deserve special attention, as they may be affected by other disadvantages, and are affected throughout the union. this is why we must also focus on facilitating integration processes on the path to equal rights. thus, in the areas of education, employment, housing and health, there is a need for coordinated programmes that address the interests of people with disabilities and that are able to address the full range of challenges posed by social disparities. hungary is committed to improving the living conditions of citizens with disabilities, ensuring their rights and promoting their integration into the labour market. i would also recommend that the european parliament adopt the hungarian good practice, encourage the political, social and economic participation of people with disabilities, consciously dismantle the barriers in the fields of education, housing, employment and health and take the interests of people with disabilities into account, rather than politicizing the issue and using it for ideological purposes.
in writing. there are about 87 million people with disabilities in europe. the data on these people is alarming: only half of them are in employment, 28% are at risk of poverty and social exclusion and 52% feel at risk of discrimination. in the last week alone, i've read two stories that have hurt me immensely: the first concerns a disabled girl who was denied boarding at bergamo airport because the battery in her wheelchair was not compliant. the second is about a group of visually impaired people who were denied entry to the notre dame de paris show, despite having paid for a regular ticket. it is not acceptable in 2022, to have to read news like this again! it is precisely against discrimination that we must fight as european parliamentarians, acting in the various sectors of society, from work to school, from culture to mobility, so that people with disabilities do not suffer from double discrimination in their everyday lives. the european charter on disability should also be implemented immediately in all eu countries, with immediate and mutual recognition of the status of disabled persons. these are commitments we can no longer postpone!
the next item is the debate on the commission statement on the outcome of cop27 (2022/2966 ((rsp)).
executive vice—president of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, if we want to evaluate cop27, i think we need to start a bit before – a couple of months before – and see where we were internationally, leading into cop27. i attended several meetings, including the g—20, where major emitters were trying to step back, quite substantially, from the agreements we had reached in glasgow in cop26. so the months leading up to cop27 weren’t very auspicious for the most ambitious amongst us. secondly, a confrontation was growing between the developed world and the developing world on the issue of adaptation, but especially on loss and damage. so this combination of major emitters trying to step back from their commitments, and an increasing confrontational attitude from developing countries because they felt not being heard by the developed world, did not bode well for the preparation of cop27. now, i think the results are a lot better than what i expected before and i want to start by thanking both the council and parliament for playing a very active role on the spot, but especially in the preparation. because one of the myths that was created in the months ahead was that because of the war, europe was stepping back from its commitment in the green deal, was digging up a lot of coal and that all our commitments would be lost. and thanks to the very hard work of the co—legislators, we could go to sharm el—sheikh and explain very clearly that we are not just sticking to our commitments of reducing our emissions with at least 55% by 2030, but that we would overshoot that commitment already on the basis of the three pieces of legislation agreed between the co—legislators, between parliament and the council. so, we could take that thing at the beginning of the second week of cop off the table, and we could still show clearly that we remained ambitious. in the days leading up to the end of the cop, tensions were high because of attempts to step away from the commitments in glasgow, and also because of the proposal put on the table by g77 plus china on a fund for loss and damage, which would mirror earlier funds and which would not look at countries in terms of their role in emissions, but only whether in 1992 they were considered developing countries, yes or no. i think at some point in close dialogue and cooperation with both the member states and the ministers present, who were all extremely active during the cop, and the delegation of the european parliament, we came to the conclusion that europe should try and bridge these differences by coming up with a plan, especially on loss and damage, that would not start from 1992 but from the situation now. so that would do a couple of things: that would address the responsibility of all those who are major emitters and all those who can afford to take responsibility; that would focus the loss and damage fund on the most vulnerable countries and not spread it out to all developing countries; and that would be able to use what we call the ‘mosaic of measures’ from all other instruments, including, for instance, reforming the international financial institutions, putting the mdbs in a different position, the private sector engaging with the private sector etc. when the european union put this on the table – and i discussed it on behalf of the eu with the secretary general, with my colleagues from the us and china – especially the us and china were taken aback a bit that we were doing this. china thought that the g77 plus china plan was the one that would carry the day; the americans thought that there would be no plan at all. so when we came up with this idea, i think we were able to create – as the secretary general of the un put it – a new situation where we did not have a confrontation between a classical developing world and a developed world, but we would distinguish, especially on the basis of whether you are a major emitter or not, and look at those countries that needed support more than those who needed less. i think this was an important breakthrough that allowed us to bring parties closer together and also allowed us to insist on at least not stepping back from our commitments made in glasgow. and we can use the mitigation work programme now to make sure that these commitments are still solid and strong in the months and the years to come. all in all, my verdict – as you know – on the mitigation side was negative at the end of the cop. my verdict on the loss and damage fund is positive because it will allow us – if we act collectively and strongly in the months to come – to shape it in a completely different form than previous attempts to address the issue. and the combination between the mitigation work programme and the work on the fund would allow us to shape it in a way that takes our commitments into the future in a positive way. i know that the incoming presidency of cop is very ambitious on these issues. i also know, we’re still faced with huge challenges in the time to come, but all in all, the results of the cop will allow us to move forward, will allow us to bring along the major emitters who, some of them, wanted to step back from the commitments, who are still in to the commitments, and will allow us also in the combination of the just energy transition plans to also take some of the major emitters in the global south with us in the right direction. all in all, my verdict today is positive. we would have liked to see much more on mitigation. we got, i think, a good result on loss and damage. i think we also have an opportunity to work very closely with those most vulnerable also in adaptation. and i think, all in all, the european union showed real leadership, and we were the ones building the bridges, we were the ones who were able to get a result that everybody could sign up to.
on behalf of the renew group. – madam president, commissioner, colleagues, i listened to your speech and i was struck by how you characterised the achievement of loss and damage. and it is an enormous achievement because one felt immediately afterwards that it wasn’t presented in that way by the commission, that somehow or another the g77 position was challenged and maybe there was a better way to do it than loss and damage. and i listened to the distinction you draw between 1992 and today and different developing countries, and all of those are very legitimate. but this is an enormous achievement – what has been done at cop27 – because for the first time it recognises those who pollute more, those who possess more must do more, and that if you break it, which we did, you fix it, which we should, and that is now locked into the principle of how we’re going to approach this issue. it’s about climate justice, something that my compatriot mary robinson has spoken so passionately about over the years. and it’s an achievement because we have a credibility problem in the european union. i’ve just come back from marrakech, where i had discussions with my african liberal colleagues over two days, and they are struck by the fact that we’re opening coal mines, you’ve mentioned it yourself, commissioner, they are struck by the fact that we’re pricing them out of the lng market, that we have allowed for loopholes in relation to gas exploration, and they are challenging us to be real partners on this issue. and i sincerely believe that we have to get over the credibility issue that is really challenging the european union. and i will say, finally, that i think you are correct in saying that we have made a solid achievement here, but we also cannot gaslight g77 countries with the positions that they rightfully took.

and there i have a couple of questions. first of all, the very simple one, when will europe increase its ndc? because that’s what we are calling parties for to increase their ndc. we did it when we came out of glasgow. now we do it again when we come out of sharm el—sheikh. so when will europe take that step? i think it would be very helpful to hear from you on when you’re going to push for that. secondly, on fossil subsidies, we are always saying that we want to finish fossil subsidies, but tomorrow we will probably have the last negotiations on repowereu, where there will be fossil subsidies. there will be. but i think then mostly important is how to make sure we limit them and how to make sure that at least it’s not going to oil, which is also on the table and hopefully can still be defeated. so, i here also really call upon the commission to take a strong stance and to really support some of the proposals from parliament. the clock was not really on 2 minutes. i have no idea where it was, but i’ll be brief, but i am sure not at 2 minutes yet, madam chair. so that’s going to be important as well. but i think the last most important part is how are we going to build alliances with third world countries, former third world countries, to indeed to overcome this north versus south divide. and i think that’s also very crucial. and we did not succeed enough last year or this year. so how can we improve that for next year? last point – which also my colleague burkhardt mentioned – it’s on what the un needs to do for the room and the space for the civil society. it was really concerning to see that there was hardly any space for civil society; at a cop there needs to be space for civil society, so i would also like to hear from the commission what kind of actions towards the united nations we’re going to take to ensure that civil society can play its role when it’s needed.
on behalf of the ecr group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the only outcome of cop27 was the creation of the 'loss and damage' fund, money that will be paid by the more developed countries to the developing countries, despite the latter's refusal to commit to reducing greenhouse gas emissions. i do not share this ideology, which imposes self-flagellation on europe and the most advanced democracies of the world, as if development were a crime to be expiated, but what i am referring to is the principle that those who pollute less will have to pay for it, and that this also gives them an unfair advantage in world economic competition. it is no coincidence that china, which alone emits five times the co2 of the entire european union, will probably be among the beneficiaries. the european union is also a major player in the fight against climate change, and we are all aware of the fact that the united states is the world's largest producer of greenhouse gases.

urban areas. this is why we must also focus on innovation and research to make these areas more welcoming, also with the invaluable help of universities and local authorities who have the most real madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the problems facing the airport sector at the moment are primarily linked to an employment policy which has not been able to protect workers during the pandemic. thousands of employees have been laid off, and now airlines and airport companies are struggling to fill the vacancies, overworking those already on the job. this is a worrying situation because it can put the safety of travellers at risk. the commission has already taken the necessary steps to ensure that the european parliament is able to take part in the debate on the future of the european union. in addition, the shortage of ground staff, which has been reduced by 29%, is leading to shortcomings in the operations necessary for the operation of airports, which mainly relate to baggage handling, or to the assistance to be provided, for example, to disabled people. this is unacceptable! the european union should be at the forefront of protecting travellers and workers. let's move, thank you!
the rapporteur. madam president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would first like to thank all my colleagues, the shadow rapporteurs, the secretariat, the staff of the political groups and the parliamentary assistants for the constructive work we have carried out over the past few months, which will conclude tomorrow with the adoption of our report on the action plan to promote long-distance and cross-border passenger rail services. this is very important work, which has enabled us to define the priorities and requirements of the sector in a unified way, with the aim of improving the operation of the railway network at more affordable costs. a competitive sector of our economy which employs thousands of workers who provide services to citizens, but which needs to become more attractive than other modes of transport for passengers and goods. our document pursues a wide range of objectives, including the creation of a single, safe and fully interoperable european rail system; improving sustainability and job creation. it was considered essential to ensure the intermodality of the railway system with other transport systems, including through the promotion of single multimodal tickets, in order to foster a continuity solution for passengers who can travel easily with a single ticket across different modes of transport. we want to protect the rights of passengers with our proposal: equal accessibility, improving transport efficiency, reducing travel times and prices, promoting fair competition between railway operators and between different modes of transport, and the need for the full involvement of young people, both as users and as workers. i am confident that the development of long-distance and crossborder rail passenger transport will play a significant role in promoting the european single market, ensuring a level playing field for the various players, while preserving the principle of mobility as a service and its role in promoting the socioeconomic cohesion of europe's regions. from the beginning of this journey, i have set myself the objective of promoting the point of view of all those territories which are most disadvantaged, stressing the importance of developing local connections, also to connect the suburbs and islands to the big cities. that is why i wanted to emphasize together the need to improve connectivity, with particular attention to remote, island, mountain and sparsely populated regions. there can be no effective crossborder transport without connections to remote areas, even within individual member states. the report therefore stresses the importance of stimulating investment to complete the ten-t in a timely manner, giving priority to allocating funds to strategic projects, eliminating bottlenecks and missing links and completing last mile segments, in the logic of maintaining a market-oriented approach for high speed and long distance, while ensuring public service and continuity of routes. the aim is to digitise, simplify legislation and eliminate cumbersome technical provisions and procedures in order to ensure better train availability. it will also be important to adapt the training and certification of railway staff to future needs and to make the sector an attractive option for young people. we have many objectives to achieve by preparing legislative proposals, and therefore, while thanking the commissioner for her willingness to do so, we ask the european commission to proceed in this direction, in accordance with what we have indicated in this document, in the interests of the citizens and the sector.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, article 80 of the treaty on european union on immigration and asylum policies states that the union's policies are governed by the principle of solidarity, of the fair sharing of responsibility between the member states, including on the financial side. according to the latest frontex data, over 280 000 irregular border crossings to europe were recorded in the first ten months of 2022, an increase of 77% compared to 2021. in dealing with the migration emergency, italy has been left completely alone and the much-vaunted european solidarity has unfortunately remained only on paper. we have had enough of promises from the european union, we want quick answers, we want a shared management of migration flows and we expect common solutions. italy cannot be europe's refugee camp and that alone.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the amendments to the directive on the promotion of renewable energies proposed by the commission set even more unrealistic and unsustainable targets for the member states than those already provided for in the fit for 55 package of july 2021, requiring a 13% reduction in energy consumption by 2030, which can only be achieved through massive deindustrialization. the measures to speed up the authorization of renewable energy production facilities will certainly benefit the companies that produce and sell solar panels, but it is simply illusory, indeed misleading, to think that they can make up for the energy deficit that currently afflicts europe, because of choices based solely on ideological reasons and geopolitical objectives that have nothing to do with, and are in conflict with, the interests of european industry and the european economy. a new list of useless, self-destructive homework is the last thing we need: i would like to see the european union's policies stop choking its member states with absurd prescriptions and start the process of reversing before we crash.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, madam commissioner, honourable members of parliament, thank you for putting this important topic on the agenda and for holding this policy debate. let me start by congratulating the rapporteur, ms salima yenbou, as well as the culture and education committee for their excellent work on the very comprehensive report. culture and cultural diversity are a vital part of our society and should therefore be considered essential dimensions of the european project. in the last two years, and as the european parliament correctly highlighted, culture and cultural and creative sectors have been under particular stress due to a number of factors. against this background, i would like to underline that the 2018 new european agenda for culture and the 2016 joint communication of the european commission, ‘towards an eu strategy for international cultural relations’, still reflect the eu political priorities in the cultural fields and with regard to both the internal and the external dimensions of cultural policies. furthermore, the council welcomes the special focus that the european parliament report has on a number of elements and, in particular, the accessibility of culture, eu funding opportunities, the engagement of civil society, as well as the contribution of culture to social cohesion, sustainable development and the fight against climate change. i would like also to recall that the council has been operating on the basis of multiannual work plans for culture prepared by member states since 2002 and implemented in coordination with the european commission and with the support of the creative europe programme. these work plans serve the purpose of providing joint solutions to common challenges, as well as more structured procedures for cooperation in the area of culture at european level. the eu work plan for culture for the period 2023 to 2026 was approved by the education, youth, culture and sport council on 29 november 2022 in the form of a council resolution. this plan addresses new priorities and challenges such as the current energy crisis. furthermore, the external dimension of culture has been significantly strengthened including, inter alia, through specific support to ukrainian artists and the protection of ukrainian cultural heritage. the synergies with other relevant policy fields have been discussed for many years, and we can still see a lot of barriers and challenges when trying to communicate with different sectors. thinking out of the box, it’s still a great challenge. let me conclude by stressing that the european union, in its complexity, has at its disposal a significant number of initiatives and instruments in the area of cultural policy. and we should now focus not only on implementation but also on its complementarity. we should aim not only at the efficient use of those instruments, but mainly at efficient outcomes of cooperation between different policy fields. we count on the support of the european parliament in this endeavour. dear mr president, madam commissioner, honourable members of parliament, thank you very much for your attention.
member of the commission. mr president. mr minister, ladies and gentlemen, first of all, let me congratulate the rapporteur, mrs salima yenbou, on her work, her commitment and her vision. i also thank the fictitious rapporteurs, the members of the cult committee and the members of the european parliament. thank you for your work of evaluation and reflection on all that has been achieved in recent years in the context of the new european agenda for culture. yes, four years have passed since the commission adopted this agenda in 2018. this was preceded in 2016 by the joint communication of the commission and the high representative entitled 'towards a european union strategy in the field of international cultural relations'. now, in accordance with the strategic framework defined by this agenda, i would like to present our action in the cultural field along three lines. first, the social dimension, then the economic dimension, and finally the external dimension of cultural heritage. i would like to begin by drawing attention to the social dimension, i would even say the societal one. it is about using the power of culture to make everyone feel included. in a word, what brings us together. we have supported research, the work of experts on culture, social inclusion, mental health, gender equality, civic engagement and the working conditions of artists. we have worked closely with the member states. we developed targeted recommendations and exchanges of best practice. however, we cannot stop here on the pretext of what we have already done. on the contrary, we must be fully involved. the work continues. for example, we have succeeded in putting the contribution of culture to mental health at the top of the commission's agenda, as was shown in president von der leyen's state of the union address this year. an inter-service group has been set up at the commission, and we have co-organised an event with the european parliament on this, with the support of the chair of the cult committee, sabine verheyen. in this sense, the reading grid of the agenda has made a difference, both in terms of procedure and in terms of our support. as regards the project selection procedure, the new creative europe regulation 2021-2027 is much more incisive in its promotion of inclusion. no project can now be supported without a strategy for gender equality, inclusion and diversity. in the background, we can also see the adaptation of our devices. this is the case with our support for artistic mobility. do you remember the iportunus pilot project, now called culture moves europe? with a budget of €21 million until 2025, it will enable around 7,000 artists, creators and cultural professionals to go abroad, co-create and present their works to new audiences. finally, the social dimension also means thinking about our actions for the younger generation, in this european year of youth and beyond. with this in mind, i will launch a european authors' day on 27 march to celebrate the talents of our authors and to share the love of reading and the power of works. i'm counting on your support here. i would now like to mention the economic dimension of our actions in the field of culture. you are right, madam rapporteur: in the face of the devastating effects of the covid-19 pandemic, which has hit the cultural and creative sectors particularly hard, maximum flexibility in the implementation of our support programmes, coupled with the recovery and resilience mechanism, was a response to support the recovery. i would like to remind you that over 10 billion euros, or an average of 2% of the overall budget at european level, will be allocated to the cultural and creative sectors, and i would like to thank the european parliament for its unfailing support. beyond the closures related to health restrictions, the pandemic has highlighted the fragile situation of cultural professionals. in this respect, work on the status and working conditions of artists and cultural professionals is a priority. in 2020, we published an expert study that brings together different cases observed in member states and offers important recommendations. i am glad to see this subject included in the council's work programme on culture adopted at the end of november. now count on my efforts to keep this subject at the top of the european agenda. i am convinced that, in the face of the great challenges of our time, we need to unleash the full creativity of the cultural and creative sectors. to do this, we need a strong sector that can work as an ecosystem. in this respect, the european institute of innovation and technology's knowledge and innovation community 'culture and creativity' is a major step forward. this new kic will create a pull-up effect to create immediate opportunities. a consortium of 50 partners from 20 countries is kicking off this ambitious project, with a start-up grant of €6 million from horizon europe. we have also launched concrete projects around which i intend to unite the sector. this is the case with our project european collaborative cloud for cultural heritage, a european cloud for cultural heritage. this tool, dedicated to cultural institutions, will make it possible to advance the digitisation of cultural works and, in so doing, will open up new possibilities for the enhancement of our heritage. i now come to the last axis: the strengthening of international cultural relations, illustrated in particular by our preparatory action 'european cultural area'. the western balkans, africa and ukraine are among our priority areas of cooperation, and concrete examples are: a ministerial platform with the western balkans launched last june, support for ukraine with real flexibility of action and, in particular, a call for €5 million for professionals in the cultural and creative sectors in the creative europe work programme, or effective support under the cultures move europe programme, our cultural cooperation with acp countries, with specific cultural actions and initiatives, such as creative togo, with €6 million allocated to strengthen the creative capacity of local actors. the question, of course, is: 'do we give visibility to good practice? can we have a critical mass that allows us to once again show the unique strength of culture in promoting our values, in what brings us together and what makes our societies more resilient? other finally, madam rapporteur, good news: today the college is due to adopt the action plan to combat trafficking in cultural goods. i am, of course, delighted to continue working with all of you on these various subjects. thank you very much for your attention. i shall be very interested in your recommendations and reactions.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we are rewriting the priorities of the european cultural agenda. as you can imagine, the arguments are many and varied. covid has left thousands of people and artists without jobs. that is why we have wanted to protect the entrepreneurs in the sector, almost always the owners of small and mediumsized enterprises, by demanding that easy access to credit be guaranteed; the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a welcome one. as the arts and culture sector provides opportunities for growth, learning and development, i believe we have a duty to look at women entrepreneurs in this field as well. it is therefore our task to ensure that culture becomes increasingly a cross-cutting sector, that it enters into our daily lives. it is our duty to protect it and with it to protect our european artistic heritage. to this end, i hope that this house will vote tomorrow on our amendment on the protection of artistic and cultural heritage, which provides, where necessary, for exemptions for the cultural sector from the prohibitions on the use of certain materials. it may seem a legislative gimmick, but not only does the integrity of our historic cathedrals, our murano stained glass, our hand-painted bell-ceramics, which are renowned throughout the world, depend on it, but so do the families of the artisans and restorers, who are increasingly and indifferently denied the use of the tools of their work. in this time of war and catastrophe we cannot afford to put at risk our european cultural heritage, which is part of our identity and which, once lost, will be lost forever.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the work we have done on this report, thanks also to rapporteur salima yenbou, has been important and has seen the cult minimum wage does not apply and many workers, if laid off, have no alternative sources of income. in addition, differences in legislation between member states on the legal status of artists hamper cross-border collaboration and work. the arts and culture are not a commodity, they keep us alive, they bring hope of emancipation, they give meaning to solidarity and to the exercise of our freedom. in culture, both at eu level, by strengthening programmes such as creative europe in the next multiannual budget, and at national level, where states should develop new alternative and stable sources of funding for cultural and creative sectors that today only recover a fraction of the economic value they generate.
